THE LATE MRS. lIASSALL

Impressive Tributes At Her Funeral.

The remains of Mrs.. John Hassall, of 31, Bold-street, were interred on Sunday in the presence of a large and sorrowing circle of relatives and friends. Mr. Thomas Dale, of the Working Men's Mission, administered the last rites. Before leaving for the Cemetery, a brief funeral service was held ,in the Mission, con- ducted by Messrs. T. and G. pale, both of whom paid tribute to the memory of the deceased in touching and impressive manner. The chief mourners were: Mr. John Hassan, Mr. and Mrs. J. Taylor (son-in-law and daugh- ter), Hilda and Beatrice (grandchildren), Messrs. D. Hill, W. lull, S. Hill (brothers), Mrs. W. Boyd (sister), Mesdames W. Hill, S. Hill, J. Hill, T. Hassall, G. Ashley, Edmonds and F. Atkinson. The bearers were Messrs. S. .Millington, Q. E. Til- ling, J. Birch, W. Worrall, A. Walker and H. Edwards.

The floral tributes were numerous and beau- tiful and showed in what esteem the deceased lady was held. The following is the list:—

CATARRHAL COLDS AND STUBBORN COUGHS

ThP Remedy you can Trust is VENO'S LIGHTNING COUGH CURE.

Every fourth person you meet has catarrh. it begins with running at the nose, the result of catching cold. If neglected it soon becomes chronic. Then it passes to the throat, the stomach, the bowels. It causes asthma, deaf- ness, huskines, hoarseness; it sets up dyspepsia and bowel troubles—there is no end to the clangers of catarrh. 'My life was a perfect misery with breathlessness and bronchial catarrh," says Mrs. Hall, of 89, Clarence- street, Shieldfield, Newcastle-on-Tyne. "I was frightfully choked up, and if I ventured out I would have to stop to get my breath, perhaps twenty times in a street's length. Nothing did any real good till I got Veno's. That quite cured rne." Ask for Veno's Lightning Cough Cure, 111 d., is. Sd. and 35., of all chemists.

ESLL RIGHTS RESERVED

TEA-DRINKING ENGLAND.

There is probably no single name in the vo- itabulary of the English speaking peoples that is oftener used than that of the Eastern shrub that is the source of our commonest beverage. Its only rival in the way of fre- quency would perhaps be the word " wea- ther.' Men may gossip from time to time of tobaicco, or wine or football; women may periodically and sympathetically discuss the affairs of their neighbours, and the dresses Of their friends; but both of them, once a day at least, have cause to speak of "a cup of tea." And yet a little more than two and a half centuries ago tea was unknown in this country. You wou:d search in vain through every page of Shakespeare in order to find any mention of it. Shakespeare never tasted such a drink. His liquid 'refreshment was ever of a stronger nature. When we became acquainted with the product of the Chinese shrub, it was at first so valuable that only the richest could afford the luxury, and the East, India Company considered a two pound packet a fit and handsome present for the king. It cost, even less than two hund- red years ago, anything from twelve to thir- ty shillings a pound, and the practice of tea- drinking was denounced on economical, mor- al and medical grounds, with almost as much fur- as the habit of taking beer at the pre- sent time.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
SOURCES OF SUPPLY.

Things have changed since then. The value of the tea imported into this country ia twelve months is now nearly £9,000,000. Most of it comes from India and Ceylon. At one time China was the chief source of sup- ply, but comparatively little Chinese tea Is now imported, only about one-tenth of the total received. Personally I regard this as a great boon, for not one 'Eng:ish lady in a thousand, knows how to prepare China tea to make it palatable. Wilien I think of the ditch-water I have had to swallow in English drawing-rooms under the name of •" China tea," I shudder at the memory thereof, and sigh for a coolie's teapot in the sun-baked lands of the East.

The tea ,shrub requires a fair supply of mOisture, iron, and vegetable refuse in the soil, and a slope steep enough to prevent moisture settling around the roots. The tea shrub grows to a height of about three feet. The leaves are plucked all the year round, 3hiefiy by women and girls, who put the young leaves and the little leaf-buds into a basket, and take them to a tea-factory. The leaves and buds are first allowed to dry na- turally, and are then rolled, and finally dried or baked. After this they are sifted into various grades. The best consists of the little leaf-buds; the next quality includes some of the young leaves; the coarser leaves form the third and cheapest variety. The best Chinese tea is exported to Russia via the Siberian railway, and quite recent- ly when staying in a Russian monastery I got some of the genuine article. As the tea has to be conveyed to the rail chiefly by means of camels, it is necessary to pack it as small as possible. In some cases the leaves are ground, steamed, and then compressed into brick-shaped blocks. In this form they are exported as " brick tea," which is used by the Mongols as money, a brick being worth sevenpence-halfpenny. The Thibetans drink tea all day long, and are very fond of a mixture called "buttered tea" made of stewed tea-leaves, rancid butter and barley flour.'

===== 0002601_19160115_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
TEA-TASTING.

One of the first things that happens to tea. on its arrival in England is "tasting," in order that the exact quality of the imported prcduct may be determined. "Tea-tasting" is a science, a profession; the success of the tea-merchant is largely related tO that of his ()facial taster. The gentleman vhose busi- ness it is to ruin his digestion that he may please your palate, goes down to the docks with a "sampling order." He takes about a pound of tea from each of several boxes, and then returns with his burden to the scene of his gustatory experiments. In front of him is a long row of cups; there may be as many as thirty or folty. Into each of these he puts a pinch oi tea. from a sample obtained at the docks. A little boiling water is poured upon the leaves; a mixture of cheer- ful aromas greets the nostrils; and the taster gets to work. He takes a sip from each cup and when he has finished, he can arrange them in batches which indicate the quality of the sample, and hence the quality of the •contents of the cases at the dock. The abili- ty to distinguish teas in this way is a gift, like violin paying, and the practice is a fine art. The curious thing is that the expert taster can take, twenty or thirty different teas in succession, and still preserve a dis- tinct idea and opinion of each one. If you gave a man thirty different kinds of intoxi- cants, and told him to taste each one in suc- cession he would not, if he were blindfolded, be able to distinguish whiskey from water after about the tenth sample, although he might be perfectly sober during the whole of the experiment.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
THE USE OF MACHINERY.

When the tasting operations have been completed, the tea is sent off to the people. who have ordered the different qualities, and by them the various teas are blended. Some of the teas, as they arrive here, are absolute- ly .undrinkable on account of their strength and their taste. The blending is done by machinery and some machines are of Such a size that they can blend over a thousand pounds of tea at one operation, sOme five or six different qualities being thus completely mixed in a very short time. Great care is taken, both in sending the tea to England in the large cases, and also in distributing it in smaller packets to the retail grocer and the householder, to keep it free' from .all moisture. The imported tea is brought to us in cases lined with sheet lead, and as we all know, the packets of tea sold by the grocer are usually wrapped in foil: The making up of the small packets, a work once done by hand, is now carried out by ma- chinery, a single machine being •able, to turn out nearly two thousand packets in an hour. There is a disposition in all departments of :he tea industry to employ as much smachin-, cry as possible. It is not 'merely that the hand of man has been superseded by that of iron on this side the oceans, but even in China and India where labour is as cheat, as it well can be, as much of the work as possi- ble is done by machinery. The one country i where there is still faith n the value of the human factor in the preparationi of a fine ar- ticle is Japan. There, n the manufacture of the better class green teas no machinery whatever is used, the whole process being carried out by hand. The popular belief is that it is impossible to procure with machin- ery the delicate aroma produced by the. old fashioned methods. For the production of teas destined for the export trade, however, machinery is regarded as permissible. What wiil not do for the Japanese is regarded as quite good enough for us. The same remark applies to China. We get the refuse. The wrapping's that protect the tea from moisture also serve another purpose. Tea is almost, as. susceptible to the aromatic inflti- clices of other .plants as a child to measles. If a packet of tea' be left unprotected in the neighbourhood of, say a bundle of bananas or a bag of apples. it will in a few minutes acquire some of the odour. The connoisseur al.so declares, that in an equally short space of time, the flavour of a high grade tea can be to some extent destroyed.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
FARM AND GARDEN.

BIG CROPS NEEDED.

"The biggest and most profitable crops which are -necessary for the welfare of the nation" is the advice given by Messrs. Gartons- Limited, the well-known seed experts and farm plant breeders, of Warrington, to their customers in their newly-issued catalogue of farm seeds for 1916. Judging by the numerous County Coun- cil and other trials in which Messrs Gartons new and regenerated breeds of seed have figured, the firm claim all such 'breeds of their raising are the most productive and the most profitable in cultivation. Their new regenerated Abundance Oat or ;n- -stance, has produced 25 per cent. increased yield over the old breed in numerous trial, and is largely cultivated in all parts of the country as well as in our numerous Colonies abroad. Messrs. Gartons' advice to their customers re- garding oats is that the stiff-strawed varieties should be cultivated more generally during the coming year, and we think this advice is very wise considering that we shall have little or no surplus 14bour to deal with laid crops of corn next summer. Formerly, with plenty of labour, British farmers have not troubled their heads very much about laid crops, but matters are now very different, and they will do well to turn their attention more to such breeds of Messrs. Gartons as "The Record," "Yielder," "Tartar King," "Supreme," &c. Sonic enormous crops which might almost be called- terrible of mangels have been grown from Gartons' im- proved breed of Large Yellow Globe, the heaviest being 123* tons per acre, gaining the champion prize of Britain. Th. ,firm's improved large red intermediate mangel has also grown some very big crops ,up to 118 tons per acre, gaining an important prize in Cambridgeshire. These crops seem all the, more heavy when we realise that the average crop of mangels in the United Kingdom is returned by the Board of Agriculture at under 20 tons per acre, Pro- portionately heavy crops have been registered by Messrs. Gartons' improved breeds of swedes and turnips. We would advise all interested agriculturists to write direct to the firin's head office at War- rington for a copy of this most interesting and instructive publication, which will be sent post free on application.

ENFORCED SALE OF HAY:-

In the case of Chilton v. the Army Council a claim had been made for the value of hay impressed in the counties of Berkshire, Buck- inghamshire And Oxfordshire. Judge Scully's award of £3,665 is less than the military authori- ties had offered and the applicant had refused. The Judge ordered the applicant to pay £5O to- wards the costs of the Army Council. The price awarded amount td 925. 3d. per ,ton. The Judge declared that farmers' interests were thoroughly safeguarded by the Farmers' County Com- mittees.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
IRON DUKE'S DAIRYMAID.

Mrs. .Applin, who was a dairymaid in the ser- vice'of the great Duke of Wellington, celebrated her 98th birthday recently. Mrs. Applin has for sxty-eight years lived near Stratfieldsaye Muse, near Reading, and is the only surviving servant of the first DA A doctor says her pulse is more vigorous that of many people forty, years younger. One of her neighbours is Mr. Horne, who was coachman to the second, third and fourth Dukes, and is now in his ninety-second year.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
TREES IN ARABLE FENCES.

A farmer has been writing to the " Times " to say,these are quite a mistake. Ile is right, says "Farm and Home,", it is a bad practice and unnecessary. The trees rob good ground, and do little good themselves. They shade crops that want no shade. It is not even good for the tree, which, spread about in a hedgerow, never makes a tine tree, as it would in a wood. There is no bole to measure. The place for trees is a wood or grove in which they can nurse and shelter each other, and keep their roots cool in hot summers, and make long boles, instead of spreading all over the place with cordwood. The practice is very bad for the fence, too. One cannot make a good fence with a lot of trees stuck along it. The hideous wire fence now might well be adorned with trees, but not the real live fence, which wants all the light for itself. In a wooded country we may have to fence found a wood, and the trees often grow near the ,ience, and in that case it is not easy to hare a good fence. The only fence' worth having is r quick fence.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GOOD DAIRY COW.

"What are the signs of a good dairy cow ?" is a question which has been asked for years. Certain signs are agreed upon by all, but hitherto a deeply-rounded bottow line has not been considered essential. Professor Haecker, who has done a great deal of investigation in this line, however, puts great value upon it. He says: Suppose we take two cows similarly built as to angularity, except that one shows great depth through the middle, and the other lacking. depth ; both being about the same size ' will require an equal amount of food for sup- port, say 811). of digestiblo nutritive matter. The deep cow will eat and digest 161 b. of diges- tible nutrients, using 81b. for herself as food and support, and 81b. she will convert into dairy products; or, in other words, you have a half interest in all the foods she eats. You are an equal partner in the business. The cow lacking depth through the middle will take about 121 b. of digestible nutrients per day, using 81b. for herself as food of support, and this she converts into dairy products, in which case you have only a third interest in the food.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
PLENTIFUL, BUT DEAR. Further Advance in the Price of Wheat.

A glance at our market reports published to- day, says last Monday's "Times," will show that the rise in the price of wheat continues unchecked. At all country markets advances ranging from is. to 2s. a quarter are recorded, and in-some eases up to 60S has been paid. The official returns. give the week's average prices realised for home-grown wheat as 555. Sd. a quarter, as compared with 465. 2d. a year ago, and 30s. 11d, in the corresponding week of 1914. The reason for the upward movement is to be found in the heavy advance in freight rates, which are affected by difficulty in securing suffi- cient tonnage to transport the crops. Actually there is no scarcity of wheat, the world's supply at present being estimated at 28,000,000 Oar- ters,-or 2,000,000 quarters more than at this time last year. The increase is even greater when compared with two years ago. The effect of the present situation on.stocks of wheat in this country is shown in the quar- terly returns. These give the stocks of wheat at United Kingdom ports on January 1 last as 758,600 tons, as coMparesl with 1;359,000 on Octo- ber 1, 1915, and 1,600,000 on January 1, 1915. and stocks of flour as 228,300 sacks, against 286,000 and 461,000 respectively.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
WORK FOR WINTER EVENINGS.

We lose by the lapse of those home industries which used to be so general in rural England. In the fruit season of 1915 the trade was much hampered, owing to the shortage of empties— the willow baskets of simple construction and different types in which fruit is sent to market. For these receptacles, growers and salesmen are dependent on professional basketmakers at ,home and abroad, and while the war has checked the importation of the foreign article, basket- makers at home have had to neglect their regu- lar customers in order to execute military orders. Why should not these baskets barnacle in the homes ofocottagers, and be a means of profitably occupying long winter evenings? Basketmaking is not a fine art, the skill is quickly acquired, anolh the other evening in a village school I saw a ilozen. men and bogs turn- ing out strong and useful fruit pots after a few lessons from an expert.' lam aware that County. Education Authorities are called upon to reduce their expenditure on what is known aq technical instruction, but here is a case in which thelabjects aimed at justify the spending of money en instruction.—" Agricultural Econo- mist and Horticultural Review."

PEASANT FARMERS' NEED.

Thee Agricultural Relief of Allies' Committee is making arrangements to ascertain the prob- able requirements of Serbia and Poland in the way of agricultnral help, when the Kaiser and his allies are driven from the territory they at

===== 0002601_19160115_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
PALATABILITY OF FOODS.

In making up the ration for the dairy cow one of the fundamental principles is to have the mixture palatable or pleasing to the cow's taste. There are many foods that the cow will con- sume readily or can be induced to eat, while there are others that she will refuse entirely. Hence, as record cows are constantly being produced the subject of palatability is assuming greater importance. By having the foods palatable the digestive juices are selected more freely and the food is more thoroughly digested and assimilated. Thus the feeder can practise scientific feeding with no greater ex- pense than though he chose his foods unwisely. Most feeders do not make an individual study of their cows; but the best feeders, on the other hand, consider each cow in the herd a unit in feeding. For instance, on one Ohio dairy farm we find that as many as fifty cows are fed in- dividually. A small feed room is provided where a daily record is kept of each cow. Here, then, is the place to. determine the palatability of feeding stuffs. In the determination of palatability certain grain mixtures are fed to the cow to see which she Will eat most readily. Then one feed may be substituted for a certain component in the mixture and the results noted with each in- gredient as it is introduced. Ultimately the feeder learns the fancies of each cow iii the herd in this manner.—" Hoard's Dairyman."

===== 0002601_19160115_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
THE APPLICATION OF BASIC SLAG TO GRASS LAND.

From the information before the Board of Agriculture it appears that many farmers who desire to apply basic slag to grass land may have been unable, owing to difficulties of transport and other causes, to obtain a supply of this fertiliser. In these circumstances the Board think it desirable to call attention to the fact that while there are certain advantages in applying basic slag to grass land early in the winter, the season for the operation may properly be regarded as extending to the end of March. Should the spring months be wet even later applications may be effective next season.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

MANURE IN THE KITCHEN GARDEN.

Manure as a rule is pplied when the ground is being prepared for the various spring crops, but I doubt if such an early application is really beneficial to.the crops. Too early manur- ing in the case of light sandy soils is a mistake, as by the time the various crops are able to take advantage of the manure its virtues have been washed out. A good lesson in this may be learnt from the market gardener who as a rule manures the ground, ploughs it in and at once sows or plants as the case may be. No doubt in many gardens more especially this year the manuring and digging have been attended to as opportunity offers, as owing to the shortage of labour the work if left undone would have been very much in arrear at the busy season of the year. One often sees the manure wheeled out and distributed in small heaps all over the ground. This is a mistake as the fertilising qualities are lost through evaporation. It would be much better to lay up the manure in one or two heaps according to the size of the plot and then distribute it, and at once dig it in.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
LIME FOR FRUIT TREES.

Little thought is given to the value of lime in the soil, more especially that in which stone fruits, as peaches, apricots, plums and cherries are growing. When the strain imposed on fruit trees in perfecting the stones is borne in mind, it will not be found surprising that so many fruits fall at the stoning period. To prevent this a dressing of lime or the addition of lime nibble to the soil is all important. Those there- fore who at this season may be planting any of the stone fruits should take care to mix lime freely With the soil in which the trees are to be planted. Lime, too, is advisable in the case of apples and pears, as well as the various bush fruits. Raspberries also are benefited by the addition of lime to the quarter in which they are grown. In the case of roses, too, which have been growing in .the same position for several years we have noted a great improve- ment from forking some lime into the surface of the bed.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
EARLY PEAS.

Many readers, no doubt when the weather im- proves, will be tffinking of sowing on a warm border some of, the preSent day early varieties of peas. A word of warning may be here given as to the need for thin Sowing. There may be an excuse for sowing the early peas somewhat thickly as they db not branch so much as the taller growing varieties, but when we sow the stronger habited sorts then their branching habit ought to be considered. In most cases a pint of good seed would be ample for a row or rows, say 30 yards in length; wide drills ought also to be drawn, this being far better than crowding the seeds into narrow drills.— Wick, in "Farm and Home."

SUCCESS IN THE POULTRY YARD.

The Winter Feeding Problem.

By E. T. BROWN.

To feed fowls properly is half the battle in successful poultry-keeping. This does not only mean that the foods themselves must be right, but they must be fed in the right manner. As in everything else, there is a right way and a wrong way of feeding fowls, and if the poultry- keeper is guilty of the latter he is merely courting failure.

The Necessary Elements. When egg production is the chief end in view, as it is in ninety-nine out of every hundred cases at the present time, the food should contain a fairly large proportion of albuminoids necessary for the formation of the white of the eggs, which is composed almost entirely of nitrogenous matter. Moreover, the food requires to he somewhat heating in order to maintain the temperature of the body. The food, when consumed by the hen, first of all goes to the production of heat, and then any that remains over assists in the formation of eggs or flesh. Thus the food should be fairly strong in carbo-hydrates, the element -contri- buting to the maintenance of bodily heat, and , especially should this be the case during cold weather. Fat is not required in large quanti- ties, hub a little assists in keeping up the temperature of the body. There is no perfect food for laying hens, perfect in as much as it is suitable under all and every set of condi- tions, during hot weather and cold, for morning feeding or evening, and for birds at liberty or in confinement. Natural versus Artificial Food.

Both natural and artificial foods possess cer- tain advantages. When fowls have access to a good soil they are able to procure a large quantity of natural foods for themselves, espe- cially during the spring and summer months. There can be no two opinions among experi- enced pohltrymen as to which is the better kind of food, natural or artificial, as not only do the, fowls thrive better upcn the former, but the ,food bill—the bane of the poultry- keeper's life—is greatly reduced. Natural food may consist of grit, worms, insects, and seeds, with which, though hardly a food, lime may be included. All of these are extremely bene- ficial. It must be remembered, therefore, when discussing the best foods for poultry, natural foods are not included, but at the same. time they should always be supplied in preference to artificial, however good the latter may be. This is one of the reasons why I have so ,constantly advocated the employment of port- able houses, because When fowls 'live •under these conditions they are able so largely to support themselves.

The Curse of Overfeeding. I am not exaggerating when I say that quite half of the fowls in this country are over-fed, and particularly is this the case so, far as the laying stock is concerned. Yet there is no greater mistake than that Of over-feeding. Fat chickens are rarely so healthy, nor do they grow so quickly as those in a lean, hard, con- dition. Fat male birds do not fertilise the eggs in a satisfactory manner, while fat hens pro- duce fewer eggs, a larger portion of which is infertile. No hard-and-fast rule can be laid down regarding the quantity of food to supply. This varies in accordance with the breed, the time of the year, and the conditions under which the fowls are kept. The only thing to do is to examine the birds every few weeks. If they are too thin, then increase the rations; if they are too fat reduce them. Mash versus Grain.

In spite .of the arguments which have been advanced on the other side of the question, I do not find any sufficient reason for the reversal of the old order of -feeding, and it is my experi- ence that a warm mash breakfast is the best for the layers, actual or prospective, at this time of the year. I am inclined to the opinion that it is over-feeding, and not soft feeding,' that is at .the bottom of the objection to mash food in. the morning, and the regulation of the required quantity demands creful considera- tion on the part of the feeder. There is, on the other hand, the danger of bringing the birds to the .beginning of their laying season in an over-fat condition; and there is, on the other hand, the risk of forget- ting, or incorrectly estimating, their increased -needs when they are in full lay. It is, of course, true that circumstances alter cases, but I have obtained the most consistent results from free-running laying stock in winter when feeding on warm- barley meal, middlings and meat; with a good feed of oats and wheat, alternately, in the evening.

To Encourage Exercise. A plan which I have followed with marked.] success for some years past is to feed only half rations of mash first thing in the morning, following this at about nine o'clock with a little grain scattered among some straw or chaff. This is one of the reasons why I am so strong an advocate of a scratching shed attached to the roosting compartment. It en- ables one to provide the fowls with exereise and. shelter, both vastly important. details in winter management, without the birds having to venture into the open. The floor of the scratching shed should be covered with straw- or chaff—the latter is the most suitable—to a .depth of one inch or five inches. Into this the grain should be thrown, affording the fowls many hours of work in order to satisfy their appetites.

-Without exercise it is wellnigh impossible to keep fowls healthy. Their' internal organs quickly become deranged, while they are very liable indeed to contract such vicious habits as feather picking and egg eating. The scattering of grain about 9.a.m. keeps the birds busily employed for two or three hours, for they will continue working hard until they have _picked up all the grain. In this case, the afternoon supply should be thrown down about 2.30 or so, -which will keep ,the: birds occupied .until they go to roost. When'this plan is followed the mid- day meal should be withheld. The Use of Stimulants.

Stimulants ai undoubtedly a very im- portant factor towards plentiful supply of eggs. They must, however, be used with care, and not overdone. During cold weather the addition of a little stimulating powder to the morning mash is very beneficial. as it assists in maintaining the temperature of the body. With backward pullets, too, a little hastens development somewhat, and probably causes the fowls to commence to lay rather sooner than they would otherwise. There are Some very excellent powders on the market at the present time, including mustard, and there is no difficulty in selecting a good one. How and in what quantity they should be used must depend upon circumstances. The best method of supplying the powder is to mix it with the morning mash, as in this manner each bird receives its proper share. The quantity to be supplied depends upon the preparation that is used. As a general rule full directions are sent with the powder, and these should be fol- lowed carefully. In the next article I propose dealing with the best foods for poultry, and how they should be used.

FATAL SHOT BY A SENTRY.

The shooting accident which occurred on Berwick Bridge a month ago, when a sentry fired to stop a motor Car and wounded Miss Nora Loft, of Berwick, terminated fatally on Monday. Miss Loft, who was twenty years of age, was taken to Berwick Infirmary, where part of ' the injured leg Was amputated. She died there on Monday morning.

COMING OF THE CHEMIST.

One, of the developments of the war which is very 'noticeable at present is the appreciation of the services of the chemist. He has not hitherto 'been held in such high esteem, in this country its in Germany, and the positions open to him have been, in business, comparatively , humble and ill-paid. Henceforth there is a promise of improvement, and the analytical and experimental chemist will probably, in agricul- ture and in trade. become a person of far greater importance in the community.

INSURED AFTER MATH.

But Soldier Claim Was Allowed

By The Prudential Co.

SPECIAL AVAR TREATMENT.

Lance-Corporal Harold A. Fryer, of the King's Own Scottish Borderers, son of Mr. Alfred Fryer, 58, Wilkinson-street, Warrington, went out to France, and on September 25th he was killed in the great "push." News of his death did not come through very quickly; it was not until October 16th that the sad event was an- nounced in "The Examiner." Ten days after death, but before the news had reached Eng- land, an insurance policy on his life was issued according to terms which had been negotiated with the Prudential Assurance Company, on the eve of his departure. The company in the cir- cumstances waived all objections and paid the claim in a manner which was acknowledged in a letter sent by the lance-corporal's father to Mr. S. Brocklehurst, the superintendent of the company at Warrington.

GENEROUS CONSIDERATION. This letter ran as follows :- 58, Wilkinson-street, Warrington. January 7th, 1916. _ _ _ _ .

_ Mr. Brocklehurst,—Dear Sir,—l do not think I ought to allow the generosity of the Pruden- tial Assurance Company to pass by unnoticed, knowing, as I do, that your office, along with

===== 0002601_19160115_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LATE LANCE-CORPORAL H. A. FRYE

other companies, is being hit most severely at the present time by the claims of soldiers. On the 25th of September, 1915, my son, Lance-i Corporal H. A. Fryer, was killed in action in ' France. Before going to France, when in camp training, and just prior to leaving England, he proposed for a policy of assurance with the Pru- dential Assurance Co., Ltd., London, and un- fortunately met his death while serving his King and country, ten days prior to the issuing of the policy. When. official notification of death came to hand from the War Office, I made these facts known to yourself as the local superintendent, and you kindly interested yourself in my case, and brought the matter before your head office. Although by the terms of the policy nothing whatever, was due, yet the company ,gave the ease most generous consideration, and instructed ! their agent, Mr. Jas. Green, to receive one weeks premium. Your company then paid 'over to me this amount of !the claim, which would have been payable under other circumstances. I feel having been dealt with so honourably, I cannot allow the incident to pass without mak- ing it known to the public that they may rely with confidence upon every consideration being given by this and other insurance companies, and that the reports that claims are not paid upon soldiers is absolutely untrue.—Yours grate- fully,

ALFRED FRYER Enlisted After War Broke Out.

Lance-Corporal Fryer, who was twenty years of age, received his education at the National School, Hamilton-street School, and, later, at the Secondary School. He was a member of the Church of England Men's Society, and was connected with St. Ann's Church all his- life. He enlisted on September Ist, 1914, having pre- viously been employed as a clerk by the War- rington Tanning Company, with whom he had been for a matter of seven years. He had only been at the Front two months.

!lIDER OF CRAGANOUR.

Warrington Jockey Joins The

Colours.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
BUT STILL WITH THE

HORSES.

Lawrence Williams, the Warringtonian, who has achieved fame in the racing world, has joined the Veterinary Corps and is now serving in France, where he is having experiences which have prompted hint to write interesting letters home. One disclosure he makes is that he is now known as Little Tich. _

In Warrington his figure is a familiar one, particularly around Bank Quay. Diminutive in size. he is the son of Mr. and Mrs. John Williams, of 34, Lovely-lane, and for a number of years was engaged in the pursuit of fame and fortune as a flat race jockey. A great lover of animals, as a boy of fourteen years he noticed in a Sunday newspaper an tdvertisement for apprentices to a racing stable in the South of England, and, as his build and height were considerable factors in his favour, he made application at the well-known training establishment of Mr. W. T. Robinson, of Fox- hill, near Swindon. At that time his exact weight was 3st. 121 b., and like many more who have followed this par- ticular calling, the life was an exacting one,

===== 0002601_19160115_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
LAWRENCE WILLIAMS.

demanding, as it does, strict training and care- ful attention. But with determination and resolve the young apprenti* soon earned the respect and egard of his master, for, by his strict sense of duty mid obedience, he was soon engaged on trial mounts.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
Joys of Real Racing.

- . From William Saxby, the well-known jockey, who was then connected with the same stable, he picked up many hints and proved worthy of the confidence bestowed on him .by his employer. After an uphill fight he experienced the joys of real raciing at Folkestone, and occupied fourth position in an apprentice handicap. • A little While afterwards he obtained his Ant win on Chauton at Newbury, his ,mount win- fling. by a length in a considerable field, at a price qnoted eight to One. He was congratu- lated by a host of distinguished personages of the turf after the successful event. Although this success Created elation to him- relf and his admirers, his chief pride and joy are in the achievements associated with Craganour, the famous racehorse which was disqualified after winning the Derby a year or so ago. The Warrington lad rode her in many trials on the Wiltshire Downs, tending her prac- tically night and day, and also travelling with her to Epsom. After nearly six years' service with Mr. W. T. Robinson, having in the mean- time obtained other successful mounts, he joined the staff of Mr. P. Gilpin, another talented trainer, of Newmarket, with whom he stayed until within a few months of racing 'oeing 'practieally abolished pwing to hostilities. Tie journeyed home and had intended to remain until the cessation of war, but when he 'learned that men were wanted for the Veterinary Corps he offered his services and was duly accepted. At present Williams is 21 years of age, and weighs slightly over 7st. A week after - enlist- ment he was 'drafted out to France, where he ,is at present. _ . _ . .

Writing to his parents on Sunday, Private Lawrence Williams stated: “To-day is Sunday, so I am writing again, seeing that I have a few hours to myself. It LS a beautiful Sabbath morning, quite different to What we have been loving lately. The tin of salmon and 'other dainties you sent me I am having for tea, and as I 'am eating I shall fancy "I am having it at home, within sound of the church bell of St. Barnabas. I am pleased .to say I am re- ceiving many letters from the homeland, to- gether with newspapers and bcioks, which are a God-send, for they help to cheer one up out here. One thing is really troubling me, and that is, there is a shortage of soap, which is irritating, especially after p, hard day's work. I am in the 'best of health. Nothing worries me, and the surroundings are ideal. Lately I have come across many of the boys and. men from the stables, whom I met before in my old sphere of life; in fact, it. is like being at New- market or Foxhill. Christmas itself I spent in , rather a quiet way, and at times I could not help but remember those at home. It seemed so strange being in a foreign land without the gay festivities as at home, but I am hoping that next year all those who are now fighting for King and country will be by their own firesides with those they love and cherish.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
Men From The Stables.

"The French pegple where I am stationed are exceedingly kind to us. For the English they have a great admiration, but, oh, the jabbering of the peasant women is awful at times. The boys in my section .call me 'Little Tich,' the mascot of the 13th. We have plenty of amusement amongst ourselves, and a cosmo- politan crew we are, to be sure. Scotch; Irish (two from the Curragh stables), Welsh, and last, but not least, Lancashire boys. We have riding exercise on .horefe—well, not quite up to the. standard of those at Foxhill, &e., the journey across the channel being very trying to the poor beasts, but we soon put them in ship-shape form, which is, of course, what we are out here for. "Kind regards to all inquirers at home." Private Lawrence Williams, as a youth, at- tended the Warrington-People's College. and is of interesting personality, inasmuch as his call- ing is out of the ordinary. The Silks he wears in the above photograph are the colours of Mr. J. Buchanan. the famous sportsman and whisky They.' are a. black and white silk blouse, red cap with gold tassel.

DRINK RESTRICTIONS.

Sir,—Your correspondent, "Mr. Simpson, Liverpool," promised in his last letter but 'one that I should have the last word on ,this sub- ject, and with your kind permission (although Mr. Simpson has not kept to his word) I intenJ to have it. I repeat that the logic of facts is against Mr. Simpson very seriously, and I would commend to his consideration the result of th 3 West Newington election, when a Mr. Terrec: who recently wired to the Lord Mayor of Man• ehester the congratulations of .a conference ?) iepresenting 400,000 London Trade Unionists on the opposition ( ?) of Corporation of Manchescer 'to proposals of Liquor Control Board," °Wry polled 787 votes when he stood as the candidate on the "No Liquor Restrictions Ticket." ===== 0002601_19160115_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
WHY ARE TWO MEN OUT OF

FIVE UNFIT?

Sir,—The recruiting figures presented to the Governmentare ominous. Lord Derby estimates that 40 per cent. of the available men are unfit for military service. Why is it that two men out of every five are physically disqualified? The chief cause of physical degeneration and deteriorated physique is alcohol. Even our drinking habits should not blind us to the fact. How can we hope to raise an Imperial race in districts where the public-house at every corner reduces the chances of healthy upbringing of children? In such districts children die off like flies, and if they live rarely attain the physical standard that they would if we were not, as a nation, cursed by alcohol. Drink is no friend to the Director of Recruiting, as Lord Derby has no doubt discovered. It makes neither good sol- diers nor good citizens. It makes for national weakness. not national strength. Before long the nation will appreciate the words of the Right Hon. John Burns, M.P. : "The tavern through- out the centuries has been the ante-chamber to the workhouse, the chapel-of-ease to the asylum, the recruiting station for the hospital, the ren- dezvous of the gambler, and the gathering ground for the gaol." • G. ERNEST WINTERTON, Secretary, Manchester, Salford and District Temperance Union. 20, Cross-street, Manchester, January sth, 1916.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
LABOUR AND DRINK.

Sir,—The "fresh and interesting piece of evi- dence as to the approval which the abolition of alcohol secures alike from employers and em- ployed," put before your. readers by. Mr. Alex- ander Thomson% will, I hope, he taken with a grain of salt. Mr. Thomson's " evidence " con- bists of an extract from a letter received from a Russian employer by the MacLeod of MacLeod (whoever he may be). This anonymous Russian employer favours us with the usual teetotal clap- trap, andanforms us that: "Our workmen had a meeting the other day, which ended in a unanimous .vote for the -total abolition,of alco- holic drinks for ever." Frankly, I cannot regard this "evidence" as of any value whatsoever. We know how completely the sentiment of the English workers on this question was misrepre- sented by the master shipbuilders' deputation last spring. In Russia • they have very persua- sive methods with 'recalcitrant workmen, and if the MacLeod's correspondent's statement be strictly accurate, we can still draw no conclu- sion as to the real views of the Russian labour- ing classes. Liverpool, Jan. 7th, 1916

J. E. SIMPSON.

A Real League of help.

To have continued to attend to the needs of the poor in the district, despite a year of war, was in itself a satisfactory return for the ma.ny sacrifices which have been made by the mem- bers of the League of Help associated with the Working Men's Mission Church who assembled for the annual meeting on Thursday night in the Thewlis-street Schoolroom.

Councillor George Dale, the president of the society, occupied the chair, and, in a brief speech, alluded to the good work which had been .done during th 4 past year. It had, he said,' been a. trying time for all concerned, and great credit was due to everyone who was con- nected with the League for the manner in which they had striven, despite many handi- caps, to keep the organisation floating, and thus, through its efforts, continue to help to alleviate those in distress in the district.

In. presenting the. seventh annual -e-rpo--rt the Secretary (Mr' J. Dutton) showed that .2205 had been disbursed to help the needy cases since the inauguration of the League in 1908. During the past year 60 persons had been assisted, and, in addition to monetary gifts, provisions and clothing had also been distri- buted.

In moving the adoption of the report, Mr. Thomas Dale spoke of the association of the League with the Central Guild of Help, and then went on to express his pleasure in the thought that the work was linked up with their church life. He urged the members to con- tinue in their good work, and made reference to the fact that probably their efforts would need to be redoubled after the war, at the same time expressing the hope that such a state of affairs would not come to pass. This having been seconded, the report was unanimously „passed. After the customary votes of thanks, :the officers were elected as follows :—President, Councillor G. 'Dale; rice-president, Mr. A. Tilling;, hon. secretary and treasurer, Mr. .T. Dutton; auditors, Messrs. H. Jones and 'l'. Ball.

MANCHESTER AND lAN EftPOOL DISTRICT BANK.

The directors of the Manchester and Liverpool District Banking Co., Ltd., report that, after providing the sum necessary to write down the Bank's Consols on conversion into 4-1 per •cent. War Loan and after making provision for rebate. on bills and for bad and doubtful debts, the gross profit for the second half of 1915 was £319,685. The expenses amounted to £124,619, leaving, with the balance brought forward, the net sum available of £280,576. The directors ecommend a dividend of El Is. per Aare (171 per cent, per annum), amounting to £165,900, subject to the deduction of income tax, leaving a balance carried forward of £114,676, as against £65,672 a year ago., In con- formity with the decision of the principal banks of the country the directors have taken the Bank's holding of War Loan it the net cost price. The reserve for depreciation of invest- ments, however, of £200,040, together with the balance carried forward of £114,676, not only covers the depreciation in the Bank's invest- ments on the basis of current and minimum prices, but provides by far the greater portion of the•depreciation in the War Loan.

An Amalgamation.

The directors intimate that a provisional agreement has been entered into with the directors of the Bank of Whitehaven, Limited, foT the amalgamation of the two banks by the transfer of the business of the Whitehaven Bank to the District Bank, as from Ist January. The directors recom- mend the shareholders to divide the bank's shares into shares of a smaller denomination. The balance sheet for the half-year shows : Current, deposit and other accounts, £28,666,698; acceptances, £2,010,541; cash in hand and in the Bank of England, £4,100,476; money at call and short notice, £2,213,109; bills of exchange, £1,682,343; British Government securities, £8,452,600; other investments, £2,386,661; advances in current accounts, £13,113,378; bank property, £500,000. The total assets amount to £34,464,711,

===== 0002601_19160115_art0058.txt | included=true | included =====
LANCASHIRE AND YORKSHIRE.

At the forty-third annual general meeting of the Lancashire and Yorkshire Bank, to be held at Manchester next Friday week, the directors will report that, notwithstanding the disor- ganisation ot business caused by the war, the position of the bank continues to give them every satisfaction, and the number of its cus- tomers is still increasing. During the past year the bank has made liberal subscriptions to the War Loan, besides giving substantial assist- ance to the various schemes for improving the American Exchange, &c. The net profits for the year ended 31st December, after payment of all expenses and making provision for all bad and doubtful debts, &c., amount to £153,415 18s. id., which, with £21,048 3s. 9d. brought for- ward from the previous year, makes a total of £174,464 is. 10d. The directors recommend, in addition to the interim dividend of 16s. per share, paid in July, amounting to £69,012 165., a further. dividend of 16s. per share for the half- year ended 61st December, 1915, subject to deduc- tion of income tax, amounting to £69,012 165.; making a total distribution of 16 per cent. for the year, less income tax. They appropriate to reserve fund £5,000, and carry forward to profit and loss new account £31,438 9s. 10d. In the valuation of investments the recom- mendation of the London bankers has been adopted, the War Loan, 1925-1945, being taken at net cost, and the other investments within the prices of 31st December, 1915. "It has , always been the principle of the directors," says the report, "in dealing with the profits, to make full provision for ordinary business contingencies, and our inner reserve is amply sufficient to cover these, but the fall in the prices of investments produced by .the war is not an ordinary incident, and they think it should be met from the reserve fund criated for such exceptional conditions. They have there- fore transferred £lOO,OOO from the reserve fund, for depreciation in investments caused by the war. A further large number of the staff have offered themselves for military and naval ser- vice, and are doing good work for their country. Unfortunately, we have to deplore the death of six of our brave men who have succumbed to the perils of war."

CRUSHED BETWEEN WAGONS.

‘l, (nliman's Terrible Death At Dalian' Lane Sidings.

Mr. S. Brighouse (coroner) held an inquest on Tuesday evening "on the body of Frank Maloney (50), who resided at Gleeson's Lodging- house, Cloth Hall-Yard, and who died, as the result of an accident receiVed, whilst in the employ of the London and North-Western Rail- way Company. Mr. Bland appeared for the London and North-Western Railway Company; and Mr. Brothers, H.M.'s Inspector of Factories, was also, present Joseph Holbrook, traffic inspector over the goods .and coal siding, said that at 11.40 a.m. on Saturday he was ,superintending the shunt- ing of the van goods into the mineral side. of the Dallam-lane branch. A number of wagons were already standing in the siding, and before giving the shunter the signal to "come ,on" witness went to the siding where the accident happened to see if the line was clear. He afterwards went on the other side. He noticed a man loading coal at the siding, and he gave him warning that -a wagon was to be shunted on to the truck he was dealing with. Witness then gave the shunter the signal, and about a minute afterwards a man camp up to him and said that someone had been crushed between the wagons. It proved to be Maloney, who was picked up and taken to the Infirmary. Maloney said that he was reaching over the buffer when the wagons "buffered up", and he was caught between them.

A verdict of "Accidental death" wa:. e- t-lir/led.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
6 4. *r n G " A b.„

Trades Council's Strong Oppo- sition To Compulsion.

MR. DUKES PREPARED FOR ANYTHING.

For trenchancy of expression, the following resolution (against the Government's compul- sory service proposals) agreed to by the War- rington Trades Council at their meeting in the Socialist Hall on Tuesday night, has rarely been surpassed : • -

"That this Council protests against the insidious attempt to impose conscription upon the country, believing its promoters to be more anxious to Prussianise and enslave the workers than to face the question of military necessity consistent with the economic and industrial obligations due to our Allies. "The ulterior motives of the conscription- ists are too obviously intended to .cripple trade unionism, split the unity of the workers, and bring about industrial feudal- ism by pseudo methods: (fl the securing of compulsion of single men. This accom- plished, their insatiable desire will then be extended to the married until the masses have been dragooned into an hegemony , of militarism comparable only to the ostenta- tious Prussianism we are now fighting to subdue. "We pledge ourselves to oppose any and every form of compulsion to the utmost of our power, and call upon all free men to rally to the movement for its resistance!'

The resolution was passed unanimously. The Chairman (Mr. E. Plinston) observed that they had always been opposed to conscription, and were going to oppose it to the fullest extent. Rendering the Bill Impracticable.

The Secretary (Mr. Charles Dukes), who at- tended the Labour Conference last week, said he came away from it impressed that there still reniained in the Labour movement a section sufficiently strong to offer such opposition to the Military Service Bill as to make its opera- tion impracticable. In his opinion, the majority of workers were opposed to it, and this was because since the beginning of the war ,they had lost all the advantages won by trade unionists. There was no doubt about it: they had to wage class warfare—the employers were already doing it—and they had against them,, in addition, the Press and the State. What Labour had to fear was the opposition of the minority in their own ranks. The utterances of men like Mr. John Hodge, M.P., were utterly against the interestS of the workers. "If the present Bill is carried, without there is ,strenuous Labour opposition, you have got compulsion for all men. The married man whq thinks he is going to be immune is under a delusion. In anticipation of this I have only to mention that the heading of this week's lead- ing article in " The Regiment' says, 'Married slackers next.' The married man who has attested has acted very unwisely, and it his standard of patriotism is, 'I will go if you will send somebody else before me,' I don't think much of his patriotism." (Applause.) In con- clusion, Mr. Dukes said he intended to 'opiaose compulsion through and through, and he was prepared to accept any responsibility. He wanted their backing. A Single Man on Marriage.

Mr. T. Howard declared that the Council was still neutral as to who went to France. and who stayed in England, but they were not neutral when it came to attempting to place on the Statute Book an Act which was going to com- pel even one portion of the community to take tip arms against its will. "I have a personal objection to it," he added; "I am included under the heading of 'Single Men First.' I am not hiding behind the. ambush of a woman: ' How long has matrimony been an obligation to the State? When You take on matrimonial respon- sibilities you don't, consider the State. It is foresight for personal convenience—and some- times it is accident" (Laughter.) The com- pulsory measure was not being pushed forward to win the war or to defeat Prussian militarism, but to institute in England what our ,brothers and sons had gone out to France to destroy, i.e., the very essence of Prussianism itself. That being so, no effort should be wanting to prevent such a Bill becoming law. "I, as a single man," he concluded, "object to any political measure which compels us to do something against our wills when we have always been debarred from giving expression to our views. On politics I have never had a word to say through the ballot boxes, and I object to being miae a scape- goat.' " Mr. John Haley remarked that the Bill had ; been brought forward on the frivolous pretext of the single men pledge. Most of the men who were to be forced into the Army had never had a -vote, and the State had no right to call on men• for an obligation unless they were prepared, at the same time, to give them a political privilege. In justifying the action of the Executive Com- mittee in sending Mr. Dukes to the conference, Mr. Haley considered that they would not have done their duty if they had not done so. "We should have, been justified if we had spent our last shilling," he declared. "We are opposed to military. compulsion, because we can see behind it industrial compulsion, and4lie'coti..rse we have taken has been in the highest sense patriotic— patriotic to the workers of this country." (Hear, hear.) The No-Conscription Fellowship.

The Secretary announced that he had received a letter from the secretary of the Warrington branch of the No-Conscription Fellowship, in- viting the Council to send two delegates to a round-table conference to be held on-Thursday, to consider a proposal to hold a public demon- stration. "I have left oxii the place of the meeting and the name of the secretary," added Mr. Dukes significantly. Mr. Dukes and Mr. Haley were appointed dele- gates. Mr. Dukes said that on the Council's instruc- tions he had written to the Town Clerk asking for the names of those who had been abointed to the Advisory Committee, which dealt with applications for exemptions from military ser- vice. He had received a reply from the secre- taries of the Civilians' Recruiting Committee stating that the Advisory Committee had been "appointed by the Warrington Civilians' Re- cruiting Committee—the same committee which desired your Council to co-operate and to send representatives to aid Lord Derby's scheme, but,

===== 0002601_19160115_art0062.txt | included=true | included =====
"Apex of Impertinence."

The Council was in a protesting mood. Next they, objected to the suppression of the Glasgow Labour paper, "Forward." Mr. Howard, in moving the resolution, characterised it as the • apex of impertinence." In a communication from the" War Emergency Workers' National Committee," the Council were asked what amount of bonus should be applied for on behalf of old-age pensioners. It was agreed to stipulate is. 6d. per week. _

In the same communication it was stated that a .resolution had been passed' by the committee, requesting that in areas where many women were -tngaged in factories, the • keeding of the, children should Lake place in schools, the par- ents to be charged the cost price of the meals provided, since the inability' to feed the chil- dren was due less to I.?tkv.ert:y than to lack of time. Mr. Dukes observed that there were two sides to the question. lie did not agree that the wives of soldiers should be encouraged to work in the factories. The -Council's Executive Com- mittee T had taken a view that the Government allotment should be adequate enough to meet the requirements of all domestic calls. If they encouraged anything which drove women into the factory, their objective, which aimed at getting a sufficient amount to maintain the women folk and children of the soldiers, was in danger of being imperilled. It was decided to write to the War Emergency Committee pointing out this view.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
"BLACKMAILING P.VIIIIOTISM."

U.D.C. Secretary Criticises Lord Derby.

Mr. George Benson, secretary of the Manches- ter Branch of the Union of Democratic Con- trol, delivered a vehement speech in the War- iington Adult School Room in Academy-street, on Sunday evening. Speaking first of conscription, he said that England entered this war not so much to fight against the German people, as to crush Conti- inental militarism. Instead of a war of peoples, it had been .represented as a war of the free spirit of the English against the military spsr:, of Germany. If that were true, England had been: defeated, .not by the Germans, but by a .small unscrupulous clique within our own borders. Militarism, instead of being crushed, was threatening to crush England. The armies of England were still unconquered, and would probably remain so. What would the nation profit if it gained the whole world, but lost its own soul?

===== 0002601_19160115_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
"Cynical and Unscrupulous."

. The method which the .conscriptionists had adopted had been unscrupulous in the extreme. The Northcliffe Press, the heart and soul of the whole offending, had carried on the campaign cynically and unscrupulously, in spite of the disastrous effect upon England and her Allies. When the National Register Bill was before Parliament, the opposition had been only over- come by a definite pledge that it should not be used for conscription. That pledge had been broken. During the Derby canvass,3fr. Asquith had been tricked into another pledge to con- script single men if they did not attest in suffi- cient numbers. But he also made a pledge that the net figures, after all reductions had been made, alone should be considered. This pledge was being Ott off intentionally by the conscriptionists. " Lord Derby's Effrontery."

The Derby canvass had been hurried and in complete. It 'was a bungle from beginning to end. And now, even such figures that hadjeeen arrived at, had not been put before Parliament. Lord Derby had the effrontery to submit' to the country an estimate of. what he thought his figures would be if the returns from the local depots were added up. Why was this? The simple reason was that the conscriptionists, with Lord Derby at their head, saw a chance of brow- beating the voluntaryist majority in the House of Commons by threatening to force a general election, which would be most disastrous iu a time of war. By means of' threatening all the disasters and upset of a general election in face of the enemy, Lord Derby, Mr. Lloyd George and the rest of the gang were blackmailing the patriotism of those people' who believed in vol- untary service.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0065.txt | included=true | included =====
Negotiations for Peace Suggested.

The way out of the war was not by continua- tion of the fight, but by negotiation fqr the abolition of those factors that kept nations apart. The war would end as stalemate. Even ir we achieved the impossible and obtained a crushing military victory, it would be useless, for in thirty years Germany would be as power- ful as ever. Let us state what we were fighting for, and let us show the Germans that we really were fighting a defensive war and the peace we desired was infinitely less harmful to Germany than the continuation of the war.

Iron Crosses of Value. There is much satisfaction in the German Army at the announcement that iron coins to the value of ten million marks are to be substi- tuted for nickel and copper. It is now hoped chat those crosses may yet prove to be worth something.—" Punch."

"TWELFTH NIGHT."

Proceeds For Bold Street Soldiers' Home.

BANTAMS' APPRECIATION.

very pleasing function was held _at the

Masonic' flail, 'Warrington, on -Wednesday afternoon.

Described as a "Twelfth Night" tea, it was on similar lines—though on a smaller scale— to the recent " Tommies' Tea" which proved such a great success at the Parr Hall. The object of the tea was such that ensured its immediate success; it was to raise funds to help the work being done at the Bold-street Soldiers' Home, and £35 found its way into coffers.

Tho room was crowded when *he opening ceremony was performed by his Worship the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock). who was accompanied by the Mayoress.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
AN ERRONEOUS IDEA.

Mrs. James Dodds, in giving the Mayor a welcome, said that as they all knew the tea was on behalf of the 'Soldiers' and . Sailors' Home, which was held in the Bold-street School. There was a most erroneous idea abroad that the Home was exclusively for one denomination. It was nothing of the sort; it was purely undenominational. They took in soldiers of all denominations and one was just as welcome as another. She was sure she need not commend such a worthy object to them, for it spoke for itself. Especially should it appeal. to Temperance workers in Warrington. She considered that the work which went on in the Home from week to week was a piece of real,. practical Temperance work,, and, as such, •should appeal to Temperance workers. Before, declaring the proceedings open, the Mayor said the Bold-street Home had been a mat source of satisfaction and pleasure, and he would say helpful to the soldirti s and sailors who had been in War- rington. He received a • letter or Monday morning from the Colonel, who had command of the 12th Battalion South Lanca- shire Regiment—the "tantams" they were generally -called—and he said that now they had got to the place where they were training for the war the men missed the Bold-street Home and the companionship and pleasure which the Warrington people had provided.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON AN IDEAL PLACE.

The Colonel wanted to know if he (the Mayor) could open a fund in order that the good work of Bold-street might be .continued. That matter was going to be dealt With later. but it was a source of satisfaction to know that the soldiers really appreciated the services which had been rendered to them. Now that they had gone to a place where there was not much 'recreation, they missed it very much. Recently, he had attended a meeting of the Liquor Control Board and a number of the Mayors who were present complained that the temptation was so great for soldiers that they were liable to fall into it. He was able to tell them that at Warrington they had a Soldiers' Home, where they had everything for the men, except intoxicating liquor. Complaints were also made against soldiers' wives, and, again, he was able to say that at Warrington they had a place where soldiers' wives and children could go in the afternoon and talk things over. So he was able to hold Warrington up as an ideal place. He was sure that the men v.-Quill appreciate their efforts that afternoiin. He had pleasure in .formally declaring the proceedings open. (Applause.) Around the hall were a number of stalls, all well-laden with goods, and the sale then commenced. Tea was provided, and, during the evening, a short programme of musical items and recitations were gone through. THE WORKERS,

The contributors to the concert programme were Miss Truslove (soloist), Mrs. Haviour (violinist), Miss Parkinson (pianist); and Mies. Henshall (elocutionist). The Misses Liston give a display of dancing, which was much appreciated. The general arrangements for the tea were in the hands of Mrs. Savage, Mrs. Monks, Mrs. Smith, Mrs. Dodds, Mrs. Hollingworth, Mrs. Nadin and Miss.Dring. The ladies assisting with the tea were Mrs. Savage, Mrs. Monks, Mrs. Hollingsworth, Miss Pitman, Miss Gandy, Miss Houghton, Miss Whyman, Miss Brough, Miss Collin, Miss Lee, Miss Sileock, I(fiss Dolan, Miss Roberts, Miss Nicholson and Miss Parkinson. The stall-holders were as follows:

Plain and Fancy Stall.-31rs. Dodds, Mrs. Nadin, and Miss Dring. • Provision and Confectionery Stall.—Mrs. Smith and Mrs. J. Gray. Mrs. W. Walker had charge of the competi- tions.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
LADY'S KEENNESS.

Sister Of Warrington Gentleman Helps To Capture German.

By the ready manner in which she .noticed the accent of a German sailor, who had escaped from an internment camp, Mrs. Crosier, of New. castleton, Liddesdale, sister of Mr. J. L. Daven- port, of Warrington, was able to give informa- tion to the police which led to his recapture. The sailor was a member of the crew of the Blucher, which was sunk in the North Sea by Sir David Beatty's squadron. The Germat wasi interned at Stolis, Harwich. _ .

On Thursday last week Mrs. Crosier read in a newspaper a description of the German, who had escaped from the camp. On the following morning she was in a grocer's shop at New- castleton, when a man entered carrying a bundle under his arm. With a foreign accent he said "orange." and this aroused Mrs. Crosiez 3 suspicions. She looked closely at him and noticed that he answered the description which had been published in the newspaper. When he had left the shop, Mrs. Crosier told the shopman of her suspicions and suggested that the police should be informed. He agreed and ; a messenger was sent to the Police Station with a note which stated his belief that the missing German was on his way South. The police soon got on his track and he was overtaken on the Carlisle road. The stranger was identified as the escaped German, and after being de- tained for the night by the Newcastleton police was sent back to the camp. . Mr. Davenport was spending the week-end with his sister when the incident occurred.

THEIR, "MONTHLY LEAVE."

Soldiers Who Absent Themselves

From Duty.

A couple of soldiers were charged with being absent without leave from their regiments. When the first one was charged, the Chief Con- stable said that this was the' fifth time in as many months. He was ordered to be handed over to an escort.

With regard to the second absentee, the Chief Constable commented "This is. another man who takes it into his head to leave once a month." He was also ordered to be handed over to an escort.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
AY OFFENCES.

Defendant Who Marked Ticket To Pass Time Away.

Three men were summoned at Tuesday's Borough Court for offences committed against the L. & N.-W. Railway Company, and in each case a fine was inflicted.

Henry- Burrows (17), of 25, Sandhill-terrace, Latchford, was charged with travelling' between VUlcan and Warrington on December 17th with- out paying his fare. lie was further charged with travelling from Vulcan to Warrington on the following day without paying his fare. Mr. Tait, the company's solicitor, who prose- cuted, .stated that the defendant was employed at Vulcan,and lived at Wa'rrington. On Decem- ber 17th be booked at Warrington, and on arrival at Vulcan managed to get by the ticket- collector without giving his ticket up.. He marked the outward 'half of his ticket with a W, making it look like a return half, and gave it back at Warrington. On the following day he gave up the return half which had been pur-' chased on. the 17th, and which was not available for the 18th. The marking was most ingenious, and he (the solicitor) could not tell the differ- ence between the genuine ticket and the other. For his defence, the defendant, who had pleaded not guilty,. said that to pass the. time away he thought he would write something. He could not find any paper, so he pulled the ticket out to practise. On Friday night he did not think what he was doing, and gave up the wrong half. He was, fined 28s. A Transfer.

Thomas Tilley, of 102, Pierpoint-street, was summoned for travelling from Vulcan to War- rington on December 17th without a ticket. He pleaded not guilty. Edward Pears, who W(IS in a soldier's uni- form, and who resides at 73,. Lythgoes-lane, pleaded guilty to transfering the outward half of a ticket available from Warrington to Vul- can.

Mr. Tait said that both the defendants were labourers employed at Vulcan, and resided in Warrington. On the 17th December Tilley came to Warrington from Vulcan and handed to the ticket collector the outward half. When questioned About the matter, he said that he must have given up the wrong- half in the morn- ing. • Shortly afterwards the ticket collector ascertained that the proper half had been given up by a passenger who had arrived,by the same train.' Pears gave the ticket to Tilley whilst in the train, and when questioned ,by Detective Hewitt, said he had forgotten that the ticket WaS not transferable.

Evidence bearing out this statement was given by Thomas ' -Thomas,. tieket collector; George Wood, booking clerk; and Detective Hewitt. Tilley admitted telling the ticket-collector a lie, but said that he had already bought a weekly ticket and had lost it.

Pears said that he did not think he was doing anything wrong. He had been out to the Front and was, now on munitions work.

Tilley was fined,los. and Pears ss.

The case in which Joseph Stafford, of 42, Back Dallam-lane. was summoned by Mrs. Ashton, of 70, Back Dallam-lane, was again before the Borough Court on Tuesday. AFTER THIRTY-TWO YEARS.

Christmas Night Quarrel Results In Separation.

Richard Hughes, of 82, Plumpton-street, was summoned by his Wife at the Borough Court on Tuesday for an assault alleged to have been committed on Christmas night. The defendant was represented by 11r. T. S. Steel.

Mrs. Hughes stated that her husband came in drunk on Christmas night. She went to bed first, but later came down to see what he was doing. He then picked up a chair and assaulted her. They had been married 32 years, and there had been eight children. The defendant had served the greatest part of his time in gaol. - Mr. Wm. R. Laws (police court missionary) said that when the complainant came to see him her arms and eyes were bruised.

The defendant pleaded that on Christmas night his wife was more drunk than he was. He added that he did not assault her at all. Defendant was fined ris.

Mrs. Hughes was also granted a separation orders the alimony being Bs. a week.

A SOLDIER'S REQUEST.

Wanted To Be Handed Over To The Pollee.

John Kelly, a private in the South Lancashire Regiment, stationed at Orford Barracks, was charged at the Borough Court on Wednesday with breaking a plate-glass window, the property of Mr. James Johnson, of 78, Orford-lane, and doing damage to the amount of £B. Owing to the absence of one of the chief wit- nesses, the Chief Constable asked for a week's adjournment. He stated that shortly after 11 a.m. on Monday Mr. Johnson heard a crash of glass and•on•going into the street saw a boot in his window. Wlien questioned about the matter the prisoner said, "Hand me over to the police." He was taken to the Orford Barracks by a member of the Garrison Military Police. The adjournment was granted.

BOYS W ITII A SWEET TOOTH.

At a .Tuvenile Court held on Wednesday, before Messrs. J. W. Hayward and W. T. Wil- liams, fair boys, were summoned for stealing goods from two shops. The Chief Constable said that the first charge was concerned with Mrs. Walton, who kept a shop at 40, iChester-road. On Sunday afternoon she had occasion to leave the shop to go to the back of the premises. On her return she saw two of the boys in the shop and they asked for a pennyworth of sweets, She went to the win- dow and saw that two boxes of chocolates were missing, and on looking on the shelf she saw that a tin of toffee Also was gone. The two boys said , that they had seen two other boys take the goods and offered to chase them on their bicycles. They

went out of the shop and Mrs. Walton did not see them again. Three of the boys .had also taken a box of chocolates from the Shop kept by Mrs. Astill, at 124, Knutsford-road.

When apprehended by a police officer, the boys admitted the offences.

Three of the boys were fined X 1 each and the other was fined 15s. •

THE ROYAL MAIL.

Mr. Patrick Maginn Again Stopped By

The Police.

Mr. Patrick MagMn, of Arpley-street, War- rington, appeared as a defendant at the Dares- bury Petty Sessions on Tuesday morning, and was fined 10s. for driving a motor car—the Royal Mail—not provided with lights according to .the regulations. Acting-Sergeant Wilcock-son stated that late on the night of January 2nd defendant was driv- ing the Royal Mail along Chester-road, Walton Superior, without a rear light. He had a head light, but instead of this being on the off side it was in the middle. There should either have been two head lights or one on the off side. • The.defendant said that, when stopped by the police, be was surprised to know that his rear light had gone out. He could only account for it by the presence of several dips, which must have jogged it out while he was travelling. . It was stated that defendant had just been fined at the Warrington County Sessions for a similar offence, and also at Runcorn. In fining him, the Chairman advised him to keep his lights in order.

THREE SOLDIERS' WIVES.

Prosecuted For Disorderly Conduct. The story of a street brawl which resulted in Police Court proceedings, was told on Thursday, when Annie Kimblin, 8, Refuge-square; Mar- garet Bethel, 6, Refuge-square; Ann Quin, 10, Refuge-square; and Martha Coleman, 7, Refuge- square, were charged with disorderly conduct. Bethel and Quin pleaded notzuilty, but the others admitted the offence.

A police officer stated that in consequence of information received at the Police Station on the 10th instant he proceeded to Refuge-square. He saw that a disturbance had taken place and , that several women were in the street. The officer also alleged that the women used a dis- gusting" language. Bethel abused him and told him that he "ought to be in the trenches and be well shot."

Bethel said she only told him that he ought to have a Suit of khaki on. Mrs. Coleman said that it was she who had telephoned for the police. The Chief Constable declared that this sort of thing had been going on for some consider- able time. These women were all drinking to gether. Three of- them had husbands in the Army. They generally behaved in a shocking way, pulling each other's hair, &c. These pro- ceedings were brought in the interests of the respectable people who lived in the square. The case against Quin was dismissed on account of -her age, and the other three were bound over to be of good behaviour for six months and to find a surety of £5. Failing this, they would have to go, to prison- for-four- teen days.

Respectable Before Husband Went To The vtVar."

Annie Bluck, of 5. Wright-street, was charged on Thursday with assaulting a neighbour named Mrs. Martha Reyes. The complainant said that on Tuesday she heard a noise going on and went to the door. She exclaimed "What a beautiful street." The defendant told her to go in and also said that it was she (the complainant) who Shad got her (the de- fendant's) money stopped. The defendant struck her in the face. Evidence was given by a number of nigh- hours bearing out this statement. One woman said that the defendant charged

her with having told the Chief 'Constable that she (the defendant) kept a "bad house." This was not true, as she had never seen the Chief Constable before that morning. • The Chief Constable: You, have missed a treat, then! (Laughter.) The defendant declared that the evidence of the witnesses was a "pack of lies." The Chief Constable said the defendant was before the Court on December 21st. Her con- duct lad not been reported to the War Office Her. husband came over on leave recently, having heard of her conduct, and would not go near her. She blamed her neighbours for this. Mr. Laws (Police Court Missionar.Y) had told him that she was very respectable before her husband went into the Army.

She was bound over for six months to be of good behaviour.
