MUNITIONS TRIBUNAL.

Employe Who Left Work To Get *. Drink.

YOUTHS' AFTERNOON OFF. Ten youths from Wigan, aged between 16 and 20 -years, were summoned at the Warrington and District Munitions Court on Monday after- noon for being absent from their work between the hours of 2 p.m. and 3 p.m. on December 27th.

It was stated that after the dinner interval they started- work, but left it in About ten min- utes. For some time past there had been a growing tendency amongst boys to take extra holidays. One of the youths pleaded that the proceed- • ings had been brought because they had at- tempted to join the Army. Another pleaded that they had not been given the holidays to which the Government said they were entitled.

These statements were denied by a representa- tive of the firm, and the defendants were fined is. each.

Wanted a Drink.

Thomas Owen Jones, of Sutton Oak, St. Helens, was alleged to have left work at 3.30 p.m. on December 31st for the purpose of obtain- ing drink. - A representative of the firm said that he was seen to enter and leave a pliblic-house just oppo- site the works.

Jones, who was not present, was ordered to he fined ..il.

Left by Himself.

A St. Helens man complained that his dis- charge certificate was being unreasonably with- held. He said that his father and family had removed to Pendlebury, and, owing to the con- dition of his health, he could not go into lqdg- ings.

The certificate was refused. Absolutely Essential.

Two fitters from Widnes asked for a discharge certificate on the grounds that the works in which they were employed were not using skilled labour to the best advantage. They maintained that if they went elsewhere they could increase their output by .50 per cent. Their foreman contended that they were abso- lutely essential to the works, and that it was very rare that the men were standing idle. He suggested the cause of the trouble was that there was not sufficient overtime. The certificates were refused.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
INDUSTRY CAN SPARE 1,000,000 MEN.

Reverting • to the question of Mr. Runci- man's estimate of a million men, we should have thought that industry could not spare this number without seriously crippling neces- sary trades. But recognising that the offi- cials of the Board of Trade have access to in- formation which no ordinary individual pos- sesses, the estimate should be accepted. .We are surprised at the confidence ilh which many set their opinion against that of experts, and how many are prepared not only to in- struct the President of the Board of Trade, but also the Prime Minister, the Secretary of State for War, in the conduct of the cam- paign. It is no doubt true that looking around locally, there appears to be few who can be spared from the necessary industries and services. But we have to remember that there must be a considerable number of men engaged in Bervices which are not necessary, and which could no doubt yield a considerable , number of men. We suggest for example the trades engaged in the manufacture of pianos and other musical instruments, which, we understand, at present are very active as a result of the demand which has arisen conse- quent upon higher wages. A very good case could be made out for the manufacturer of pianos in ordinary times, but these are not ordinary times, and what might be deemed almost essentials in prosperity and peace should be ruled out in day t 4 of stress and labour shoitage. Several other trades be- sides this could, and possibly may have to be, shut down altogether. We may have to face great changes involving discomfort and prob- able loss. If all will keep their eyes .on the great national object, victory and a peace that Germany will not again have the power to break, these burdens will not feel so heavy.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
GOSSIP.

Accustomed as he is to presiding at most local functions he attends, it was not surpris- ing that Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., should immediately go to the chair at Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday.

After the first case, the Clerk (Mr. Henry Greenall) looked round at the magistrates and remarked to Sir Gilbert that as Mr. F. A. Frost was the senior magistrate he should be presiding.

Sir Gilbert smiled in astonishment at, his mistake, and immediately vacated the posi- tion, saying to Mr. Frost, "Oh! I'm sorry. I forgot."

Union of Democratic Control pamalets luiving been placed on the seats for the Trades. Council meeting, on Tuesday, mem- bers either sat on them or picked them up to read.

The Secretary (Mr. Chas. Dukes) remarked that because, the committee had requested these to be sent, it did not necessarily mean that they, agreed with their contentions.

Considering the attitude of the Council to- wards Conscription, it is rather curious that Mr. Blatchford's portrait should still hold a conspicuous position on the walls of the Socialist Hall.

But it has been there many years and is fading fast. So, in their view, no doubt, is his glory!

Warrington people seem to be improving in paying their debts. The business at the County Court on Thursday was very light indeed, and there were no contested cases.

The Court rose much earlier than usual. As this was the first sitting of the year, it is a good omen for the future.

A discussion on cocoa at the Board of Guardians' meeting on Wednesday scuttled Mr. George Mather's title to participate use- fully.

He admitted the fact himself. "If it were about ale or whisky .1. might say something worth hearing, but I don't know anything about cocoa," he observed frankly.

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell mocked a deprecat- ing attitude, but Mr. Mather winked and smiled across to him.

At the same meeting a gentleman, speak- ing of a patient in the Workhouse, said he was suffering from pains in the lumbar muscles. "Lumbar!" corrected one, who had evidently been studying first aid.

I am told- that the visit of " 134-tty "'to the Royal Court Theatre contributed something of a record, for the " takings " were the largest since the theatre was taken over by Sir Joseph Beecham.

Had Sir John French or Sir Douglas Haig attended the Warrington Insurance Com- mittee' meeting on Thursday his communique would have been: "There is nothing to report to-clay."

The meeting only lasted three minutes, the business merely being the adoption of the minutes of the various committees.

Armlets are more prevalent about the town this week, but they are not all being worn in regulation fashion.

Some are wearing them over their 'wrists— like policemen wear their duty bands.

A friend of mine asked a sergeant whether he could keep his armlet as a souvenir when he was called up, but the reply was, "No, you might use it for patching."

When shall we be saved from those who coin new words and eccentric phrases'?

Last week we had at the Hippodrome an artiste who described himself as an " acre- median," and he turned out to be an acrobat and comedian combined.

Next week we are to have two artistes who declared themselves to be "The most eccen- tric eccentrics that 'ever eccentriqued." It is certainly an eccentric way of putting it. VIGILANT.

. "We are both grate business men," said th'.,% horseradish vendor to the coal dealer.

Men should be known by the good they do rather thatt the goods they have.

Getting Wise. "Before I married,' said Mr. Henpeck, "I didn't.know ;what it meant to sppport a wife." " I presume you know now?" "Yes, indeed. I looked up the word sup- port' in the dictionary and discovered that ono of its meaning is 'endure.'"

===== 0002601_19160115_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
WHY COMPULSION HAS BECOME NECESSARY.

• The necesSity for the Military Service Bill arises out of the tremendous issues which are at stake at. this hour. The risks involved in this conflict affect not alone the lives and liberties of the English people, but those of all Europe. The freedom of the human race from military despotism is•at stake. These seem to put aside the reasons which govern the thought and action of many who in ordin- ary times are Opposed to any form of military conscriptiOn, 'and who recognise that the con- ditions are such that we must put forth our full strength, and that there must be national unity. " A.G.G." expresses the truth well when he says: ",Do not let us in this rugged and awful crisis forget in petty recriminations that we are fighting for the soul of the world. Let us rather remember that thousands of our coulitrymen are giving their lives for our liberties. If Germany can dictate her terms to Europe the Sword will have won; we shall live under the dominion of the sword." The conditions which have brought about the pres- ent political crisis cannot be summed up better than in the words of Mr.. Samuel :

"If hon. members agree, firstly, that our duty requires us to seni every man we can spare ; secondly, that we can spare 1,000,000 men; thirdly, that under the Derby scheme only 300.000 men will be forthcoming unless the Prime Minister's pledge is redeemed; and fourthly, that the Prime Minister's pledge was neces- sary to secure the totals ObTained ; then they must come to the final conclusion that a measure of this kind is inevitable."

We cannot see that any flaw can be found in this reasoning. The facts, are correct and the inference cannot be questioned. We are not concerned about fine definitions as to what is a "negligible quantity" accord- ing to the Prime Minister's pledge. We want to get beyond the pledge to the reason why the pledge was given. This still holds good, pledge or no pledge. We know that there would be a sufficient number left in every locality (if some such Bill is not made law) whose presence would be a constant reminder of the unfairness of the present system, which would be especially felt by those who have relatives at the Front exposed to danger, or who perhaps have already suffered through disablement or death ox some bread-winner. These single men, avoiding all peril to them- selves, would be observed filling positions and securing emoluments in which those who had gone to the Front would have shared if

they had 'adopted 'a like. course. , • Several writers since this Bill was intro- duced have ',brought -forward this same point of inequality of sacrifice in connection with .men • starred because of their occupations. We quite admit the force of this, but the. cases cio not stand on the gope footing. The' starred men are not Wt- at 'the bench and lathe as a privilege to thein, but as a necessity for, the country, while the unattested men are at home because of their own choice, some with good reasons others without. A rigorous re-examination of the reasons why certain men have been starred may be necessary, but this does not invalidate the soundness of the principle of retention for necessary work. This condition, only now proniinentry brought to ' the front and used against compulsion, is not new; it has existed ever since the fact was recognised" and acted upon that Certain men ! were more valuable to the country in industry than in the Army. Strict equality of sacri-- fice is an ideal impossible of attainment. How can- the equality or sacrifice be estab- lished while many families have by death lost husband, father, and breadwinner? The governing principle should be that each one should be ready to giVe the 'best service he can to the country, and the country has a right to call for this. What his best form of service is cannot be altogether arranged by the individual, but of necessity has to be de- termined by the needs of the particular forms of service. This then is .the principle which must be applied, the compulsion: We should all accept, a compulsion that may keep the turner at his lathe, the chemist in his labora- tory.' It may bring back from the front the men who can render better service at home; it may send the Piano-maker, the jeweller, and many others, whose labour is unnecessary, to the training camps, or to the bakery or.the railway at home. it may. before we have fin- ished make many more calls upon us; it may yet call upon those who are more advanced in life to take some definite part of the national burden and in the, national danger. If we have any adequate conception of -what is involved in this struggle we will all respond to the call or the, command.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
ONLY THE SHADOW_ OF VOLUNTARY

SYSTEM LEFT.

It is contended by some that had facilities ' been afforded for the 651,000 unattested single men to give their reasons, the number who ought to have attested would have been re- duced to a "negligible quantity," and that time should have been given and machinery erected to carry this out. We do not see the need or the wisdom of this procedure. While it avoids the letter of compulsion it acts in its spirit. Have we not gone as far as in honour we ought under a voluntary system? Many are of opinion that we have already gone too far, and in so doing have parted with the sub- stance and clung to the shadow of voluntary- ism. To add to what we have done already by compelling men to appear before a tribunal, in which they are obliged to state their reasons-, for holding back, and we suppose be subject! to persuasion, is not in accord-with tho spirit of voluntauism. 'Further, are -these inen en- titled to this further concession, or have they asked for it? The question of enlistment has been before their minds for the last seven- teen months, and specially so for the last two. They have been bombarded with letters, cir- culars, canvassers, 'open-air and in-door ap- peals, and they have deliberately by their in- action, refused their services. 'ender volun- taryism they are quite within their right (ha lever may be said of their duty) in hold- ing back. Even if the figures (651,0 W were reduced to one-half or even less, all who have been engaged in the work of canvassing know that there is an appreciable number of young single men who have held back without a suffi- cient reason--:that is sufficient in view of the ,national need. To have a further compulsory investigation of the men appears to be unfair to the men, i.e., unfair while the voluntary system stands, nor is the plan a wise one. The President of the Board of Trade with his experts has declared that a million men can be spared from industry, and the Cabinet and the Military Authorities, with their special knowledge and responsibilities, have declared that these men are needed. The tribunals dealing with the classes already called up have an obligation placed upon tlidm to select one million men, married or single, who will do the best .service at the Front. There are certain

===== 0002601_19160115_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
FOR- WHOM DO THE TRADES COUNCIL SPEAK?

It would be interesting to know for whom the Warrington Trades and Labour Council speak nowadays. We notice that when a deputation was sent to represent this body at the congress of delegates of Trade Unions last week, a claim was made on behalf of Mr.

Chas. Dukes—though we do not know thaf was with his sanction—that he represented

10,000 Warrington workers. Does he? Mr. Chas. Dukes has been persistently in opposi- tion to all movements that have been under- taken for the better and successful prosecu- tion of the war. He was opposed to mili- tarism even in the mild form in which we understood it before the; War. When in the autumn following Germany's onslaught on humanity an appeal was made to the country to close up the ranks in face of the common foe and concentrate upon his defeat, what did Mr. Dukes and several of his associate's do? They forced an election in three of the wards; and, although they were ignominiously de- feated, Mr. .Dukes had the satisfaittion of keeping his hat on while the election crowd in front of the Town Hall sang "God save the. King "—deliberately, There have been '-tivo 'great efforts made .since to save. our grand voluntary military system. . What did the Trades and • Labour Council do? In face. of repeated invitations to give .the recruiting .committees assistance. ,they .as repeatedly refused and • hid-themselves - behind the Rica that they were "neutral." We have often marvelled, that ' , Englishmen can 'remain neutral in the present crisis in their country's history; we have marvelled still more that Englishmen could be found who could publish the filet that they were neutral. But there it I is.

. The Trades and Labour Council have this week passed a resolution against the Bill which the Government have been compelled to introduce in order to make a small fraction of the men of the country do their duty. The resolution describes the Compulsion Bill as , an insidious attempt to impose conscription upon, the country in order that the 'country may be PrussiNnised and enslaved;' that trade unionism may be crippled; and that the masses may be dragooned into militarism com- parable only. to the ostentatious Prussianism we. are now fighting to subdue. It will be seen by our report of the Trades and Labour Council -meeting that .there is much more similar language, which We are told was agreed to unanimously. Of 'course, the answer to all this is simple in the extreme. It is this: The Bill is only a temporary measure. It L would never have seen .the light of day had ' Mr. Dukes and those who think like him not • remained neutral. It is hardly fair to talk of Prussianisin when they , have been specifically asked to help to. save us, even from partial conscription, and in vain. The only justifica- tion for the attitude of the Trades and Labour Council at the present moment would be the alleged menace to the unions afterwards; but Mr. Asquith has so satisfied .the Labour repre- sentatives as to the safety of industrial ,or- ganisations that Labour retains its place in the Government, and Parliament has passed the second reading of the Military Service Bill by 431 votes to -39,. or a majority of 392. There. is one thing certain: That if we all re- mained neutral when voluntary recruits were being sought and then declared against ser- vice when compulsion was introduced, there would soon be no trade or even country left to fight for. The Germans would see to that,

as they have seen to it in Belgium, Northern France, Poland, Serbia, and Montenegro. It is sufficient for us, *as the conductors of a newspaper that is at mortal enmity with com- pulsory military service, and will be still when this tremendous crisis is past, to know that Lord Kitchener, who stands outside and above politics, says it is necessary to save the situation. -Lord Kitchener has been at- tacked because he was supposed to be holding

out against conscription. Now ;the force of circumstances has made him declare for com- pulsion, and as the expert who knows and has I charge of the job, we are bound to follow him. But what we are immediately concerned about is the title of the Trades and Labour Council to claim to represent Warrington working men. The claim, as we have said, was made for Mr. Dukes that he represented 10,000 working men of Warrington at this ;conference. As a matter of fact, there re- main only a little over 800 unstarred men in 'Warrington, single and married combined, who have not attested. The remainder have declared neutrality "off," and are serving or preparing to serve their King and country. At the election, when he broke the truce, Mr. Dukes and his companions polled 353 votes against 2,0 n by their opponents, and the voters are mostly working class folk. Later they remained neutral, and so deprived the working men of representation on the Parlia- mentary Recruiting Committee, and there-

Concert for Relief of Wounded Soldiers.

Lovers of music will welcome the noticd in our advertisement columns of a concert to be held on Sunday, January 30th, at the Royal Court Theatre for the relief of Belgian wounded. It is under the patronage of His Worship the Mayor (Alderman P. Peacock); and there is to be a brilliant 'array of Belgian talent, who will sing chiefly in English. In- cluded in the programme are the renowned Mme. Rizzini (soprano, Royal Opera, The Hague), Mrs. Armand Blackley (mezzo soprano); M. Colbert (baritone, Theatre Royal, Verriers), M. Caveye (violoncellist, of the Paris Conservatoire), Mme. Van Ilee (soprano, Royal Conservatoire, of Antwerp), M. Adrien Van Roey (tenor, Antwerp Opera House), M. -Camby (the famous Belgian violinist), and M. Paul Kocks (Chef d'Orchestre, Theatre Royal, Liege). Tickets can be obtained at the Box Office.

Bold-street Cripples' Guild: New Year's Party.

There was a large attendance at the annual party for cripples held on Wednesday in the Cairo-street Schoolroom. The Guild, although largely worked by the Wesley Guild of Bold- street Church, is entirely undenominational in its sympathies and seeks to brighten the lives of some two hundred cripples of all ages. In normal times, instruction classes are carried on in order to enable the cripples to earn a livelihood, if at all possible. Owing to war conditions, however, this part of the Guild's work has had to be suspended this year. A considerable percentage of the cripples was present on Wednesday, when tea was provided. After tea, games, crackers and choruses were the order of the day. Later still a concert was provided. The Rev. Percy ,meson was in the chair. Mr. Whitfield gave several recita- tions in Lancashire dialect. Miss Isherwood sang several solos, and the Brick-street Inde- pendent Choir rendered several glees and also a sketch. Pound Day at Raddon Court. Rather more than usual interest was centred in the monthly Pound Day -held at the Raddon Court Red Cross Hospital on Thursday. Very fine violin solos were rendered by Miss Rey- nolds, and Miss Hill delighted with a number of pianoforte selections. Mrs. Liston's little daughters danced very charmingly. Songs were also rendered by several of the patients, Sergeant-Major Northcote amusing the audi- ence with his clever skit on a cavalry officer in the song entitled "My word!" Privates Kiag. Donaldson and Indhope gave some favourite Scottish songs, and there were several other good performances. Thanks were accorded to all the performers, and special reference made to the invaluable help of Corporal Molyneux (South Lancashire Regiment), who is shortly- to leave on furlough, for his ever-ready willing- neSs to play accompaniments and assist in the organisation of the various entertainments of the soldiers. The committee of the hospital wish to thank the following ladies and gentlemen, who kindly contributed groceries, cakes, rabbits, eggs, live fowls, fruit, jam, money, &c.:—Mesdames Parr, Hewitt, Pigot, Woods, Birtles, Robertson, Booth, Parkhouse, Hogan, Broadbent, Ireland, Dash, Kerfoot, Brarahall, Reynolds, Grounds, Williams. Gough, Ginsberg, Wainwright, Smart, Platt, Evans, Garnett, Ince, Davies, Evelyn Willis, - Donnelly, and Kelsall; the Misses Belfield, Stone, Silcock, Lee, S. Broad- bent, and Gilbert; and Mr. Coxon. A hand- some bed-spread was presented by Mrs. Bram- hall to be drawn for in aid of the funds of the Hospital. Golborne-street Sunday School Concert. A concert to raise funds for the new piano fund was held at the Golborne-etreet Sunday- School ,on Thursday evening, and was an im- mense success. The Rev. -IL Ogden (pastor) was in the chair. The programme opened with the French National Anthem, sung by the scholars. Other items by the scholars were an action song, "We Children," and an action song and clance, "The Sauey Sailor-boys." Two sketches were given. The first was entitled "Aunt Clegg quarrels with Mr. Tulliver" (from George Eliot's novel "The Mill on the 'Floss"). The characters were: Mr. Tulliver Master Harry Wilson; Mrs. Tulliver, Miss Elsie Littlewood; Mr. Clegg, Master Stan. Spittle; 'Mrs. Clegg, Miss Annie Pennington; and Mrs. Pullet, Miss Nellie Lawson. The other sketch was from Dickens's "The Cratchit's Christmas Dinner." The characters were: Mr. Bob Cratchit, Mr. Few; Mrs. Cratchit, Miss R. Tynan; Martha, Miss Evelyn Pepper; Peter, Master George Savage; Belinda, Miss Beatrice Jones; Mary, Miss Lily Green; Edith, Gwennie Jones; and Tiny Tim, George Wells. A dialogue entitled "All a Mistake" was given by Mr. J. Webster and party. The characters were: Mr. Stubbs, Mr. J. Webster; Mrs. Stubbs, Miss L. Grace; Miss Lucy, Miss G. Tynn; Augustus Bush, Mr. W. Michael; Shop Boy, Master H. Wilson. Mr. J. Web- ster's party also gave a representation of a Negro Auction. The quartets, "Annie Laurie," "Moonlight and Music," and "The Catas- trophe," were pleasingly sung by members of the choir. Miss Dale sang the two songs "The Motherland's Calling" and "My Caravan," and two violin solos were given by Miss Olive Balmford.

Catholic Chaplain in Dispatches. Father Lane-Fox, Roman Catholic chaplain with the London Irish Rifles, who was formerly at Warrington, was mentioned in dispatches by Sir John French, published on New Year's Day.

Minister's Son Obtains Commission. Mr. C. F. Morgan, son of the Rev. Ebenez.v.. Morgan, late of Padgate, and now of Prestwici. has been gazetted Second-Lieutenant in the East Lancashire Royal Field Artillery.

Fewer People in the Workhouse. It was stated at a meeting of the Board of Guardians this week that the inmates in the Workhouse number 456 and, in addition, th?Ae. are 74 wounded soldiers. The number of in- mates a few months ago averaged 600.

A fire—caused, it is thought, by the fusing of an electric cable—broke out at the Latchford Tanhing Company's works, Thelwall-line, on Sunday afternoon. Consequently, when the War- rington Fire Brigade arrived, under the dimc don of Superintendent F. IT. Turner, th rr services were not required.

At the Warrington Board of duardians meec- ing on Wednesday, a letter was read from Mr J. L. Fryer, of Padgate, calling attention to the increased cost of labour, foodstuffs and (tali/ cows, and asking, in view of such facts, that his present contract to supply milk to the Padgate Cottage Homes at as. per dozen quarts, be can- celled, and a, new contract at 34. 6d. per dozen quarts be made. The V.C.'s Wife.

Piper Laidlaw, V.C., who for a time was in the Lord Derby Hospital at Winwick. received a public presentation at Berwick On Saturday, which included War Loan scrip for £5l, and gifts for his wife and parents. Speaking for Laidlaw's birthplace, Captain G. H. T. Swinton, of Swinton, said that deeds like Laidlaw's tended to shorten the war and bring victory nearer. Piper Laidlaw, in thanking the subscribers. said that their gift would be put to good use, for his wife :would get it all, and she knew how to take care of it.

THE EDITOR is again

in trouble.

TUN WEEK he has been charged with not printing a sufficient number of papers by a gentleman who, last Saturday, found "THE EXAMINER sold out at three shops. He only just managed to get the last copy at the fourth shop.

TERRITORIALS

Change Divisions and Leave with

General's Praises.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
ELL LED AND WELL BEHAVED.

Have Fought Gallantlg and Suffered Ileavilg.

The 4th Battalion South Lancashire Regi- ment (Colonel B. Fakelough commanding), after an almost continuous presence for ten months in or near the fighting line in France and Flanders; have been moved from the Divi- sion of which they have formed a part to another Division not so nearly connected with the actual fighting. t•

===== 0002601_19160115_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
PUT THEIR BACKS INTO IT.

The General commanding the old Division, in bidding them good-bye last week, said: "Colonel Fairclough and officers and men of the 4th South Lancs. I am sorry to say this is the last time I shall have the pleasure of seeing you on parade. You are now going off to join a new Division. It is just a little over ten months since you jomed this Division at I think, and since then you have been through all the rough times the Division has .had to go through. You have had your full share of trench work, and have been in several s nasty places. On' June, 16th you fought most gallantly at , guttering very heavily in olitcers, non-commissioned officers and men.

" Latterly, as I required a good Battalion as pioneers, knowing I should get good diggers from South Lancs. men, I selected this Bat- talion. I have seen you working myself a good deal and have received most favourable reports from the C.R.E. of the work you have carried out since July 25th. It has been of great value to the Division to have a Bilttalion that has worked so well. You have gone through a vast amount of work and have put your backs into it. You have worked in various .places, and wherever you have been working I knew that the work would be car- ried, out thoroughly and well.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
A Great• Reputation.

"You leave the Division with a great repu- tation, and I and the whole Division are very sorry to lose you. It is like cutting off a limb to lose such a fine Battalion and such a gallant one. You are going to a Division which is not yet formed, but I pm sure you will not fail to make it a splendid one if you ,stick to your guns and work as thoroughly and well as you have done with me. The Battalion has been ably commanded, well led, anct well behaved. lam sure that so far as I can,' shall watch your future in this country with the greatest of interest, as I am sure you will.distinguish yourselves. lam exceed- ingly sorry to lose you. "Don't forget always to stick to your duty, for we shall win this great campaign—which is the greatest campaign you or I have, or evet- will have, a part in—by hard work and sticking to duty. Remember the peace' and happiness of Europe depends:on you men. We have all got to put our shoulders, to the whql; rant if you continue to Work and. fight as well as you have done, I have no doubt as to the result. I wish you luck, and, I hope you Will.- be as happy where you ltre going as! feer sure you have been with this Division."

Acting As Colonel Of A Regular Regiment.

Major George It. Crosfield, of the Warring- ton Territorials, has been given the temporary command of the 2nd Battalion Suffolk Regiment in France, the colonel of which has sprained his article. .Though the appointment .is temporary, it is a great compliment to the gallant Major, and an honour to his Battalion and to War- rington.

QUARTERMASTER AND LIEUT.

INGRANI.

An Explanation.

There seems to have been some misundp.- standing caused by our remark,, in dealing with the Territorials mentioned in dispatches, that Quartermaster and Lieutenant U. 11. Ingram had rendered valuable services " at the base." The base is, of course, miles away from the front. The Quartermaster has been at his post with the Battalion from the beginning', and has frequently. carried out his duties under 'dangerous 'conditions and under fire. In this way he has rendered great services to the Battalion; and his reward, as we further stated a fortnight ago, " was for "gallant and dis- tinguished services in the field."

KILLED.

SECOND-LIEUT. MORRISON.

Second-Lieutenant Morrison is not a local man. We learn, however, that he was educated at Ripton, that he was a first-class boxer, and that while'at the Front he was absolutely fear- less.

WOUNDED.

SECOND-LIEL TENANT ARI HAMS.

This gallant officer, who has been wounded, resides in Loudon, and, like Lieut. Morrison, came to the Battalion from outside to assist in leading it. When the history of the war comet; to be written it will be found that the Battalion owes a great deal not only to our local officers, but those who Lave joined its ranks from afar.

CORPORIL TOMBS'S UNCLE.

. Exploit During The Siege Of

Delhi.

"HERO OF THE DAY."

In an interview with an " Examiner " repre- sentative a few weeks ago, Corporal Tombs, V.C., stated that he was a nephew of Major- General Sir Henry Tombs, K.C.8., V.C., of Indian Mutiny fame.

Sir Henry Tombs was an officer of great dis- tinction, and took part in the siege of Delhi. In a book recently issued , entitled "The History of the Royal and Indian Artillery in the Mutiny of 1857," by Colonel Julian R: J. Jocelyn, the writer pays high tribute to Corporal Tombs's uncle. At the time of the siege of Delhi, Sir Henry was a Captain and Brevet- Major of the .2/1 Bengal Horse Artillery, and it is said that he "was one of the finest officers the Army has ever produced. He was not only brave in himself, he was the cause of the bravery that is in other men." How con- spicuous was his gallant leading may be judged by the fact that in the first few weeks of the Mutiny he was twice wounded and had five horses shot under him. In the capture of the Idgah battery on June 17th, 1857, he was, as Lord Roberts recorded, "the hero of the day" and, on July 9th, he won the Victoria Cross by an exploit which Colonel Jocelyn describes•with animation. Hills, of the same regiment, now General Sir James Hills- Johnes, who was also awarded the Cross for .his bravery in• this action. "had charged the advancing,- rebels. single-handed. It did not occur to him that he was putting himself in the line of fire, for .his mind was dominated by the one idea of giving his nien time to load, and never did an artillery officer do a more heroic deed. Ile cut down the first. man he encountered, struck a second, and was then ridden down, horse and all. Untouched by the ' hoofs that thundered over him, he rose to his feet, and had just regained his lost sword when three men? two of whom were wounded,, came at him. Hampered as he was by a heavy cloak soaked in rain, he wounded the first man. with his pistol, and catching the lance of the second ' man with his left hand, he wounded him with his sword. The third man (on foot) then came up, and, wrenching Hills's sword from his hand, threw him on the ground.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0134.txt | included=true | included =====
TOMBS'S GLORIOUS SHARE.

"Meanwhile Tombs . . walked down from camp to visit Hills's picket. As he approached his destination he became suddenly aware of a confused mass of horsemen galloping past on his left, while some thirty paces distant he saw his subaltern lying at the me'rey of a sowar. In a moment Tombs's pistol was levelled on his left arm, and the rebel fell dead, Shot through the breast. Then the two officers ascended the mound and were watching the movements of the enemy when Hills caught sight of his own pistol in the hands of a rebel approaching on foot. Ile and Tombs immediately went to inter- cept him; but the man came on with great courage, cut at Hills, who perxieit the blow, aind then slashed through the wadded cap cover worn by Tombs. Turning to Hills again, he gave him a severe wound on the head, and was then run through the body by Tombs, who had been saved by his head-dress."

Dig, Dig, Dig.

In a previous letter to his mother, written in November, Private Randles stated

"It is nine .months last Saturday since we set sail for this terrible country, so I think we are deserving of a short leave. We are like navvies—dig! dig! dig! all day long. Still we must not grumble, but must stick to the old

A

Scout law—` Whistle and smile under all cir- enmstances.' I read about H. Walker and those othei two boys being out here on a hos- pital ship, and I wish I had known, for I could have seen them. The ship was out here at first when we came, and was also at Malta with us. We have had bad weather lately and the wind is awful. There are whirlwinds whi!n lift the tops off our dug-outs Unless they are fixed down very firm." Private oßandies has a brother—Sergeant John Bandies—in the Army, attached to the 3/4th South Lancashire Regiment as drill instructor.

TILLEY, D.C.M.

Honour For An Old Warrington Footballer.

Company-Sergeant-Major James Tilley, 1,5 South Lancashire Regiment, who is one .1f those awarded the D.C.M., is .the celebrated Rugby full-back who played for Widnes and then for Warrington at the .time they won he Northern Union Cup. The son of a famous athlete, he followed in his father's footsteps. and became the idol of Widnes in athletic circles.

Local football enthusiasts hays heard with great satisfaction that their old-tithe favourite has gained this high distinction. When the Warrington Club secured his services from th.ir near and. dear rivals, Widnes, . who, at that time, were passing through adversity, on December 27th, 1906, it was regarded as an portant captura in the Northern Union-footboll world. Some time previously his confrere, .ke Taylor, had preceded him to Warrington, an this was, perhaps, a factor in Tilley throwing in his lot with us. He played for Warrington two days after he signed on: it was agai,ist _ _ _ _ _ _ _

Bradford, at Bradford, and he discharged a use- ful part in the victory which was achieved. On this occasion he did not occupy his accus- tomed position of full-back, figuring on the left 7 wing in place of Fish, who was at that time incapacitated. Be filled this post in sever 11 matches, but his chance of reverting to his favourite position came when Berry, who, :.t that time, was Warrington's full-back, tratii- ferred his affections to the sister code. Tilley immediately captured the eyes and hearts t.f the Wilderspool crowd. By his gentlemanly conduct on the field, allied to his skill as a tackler and tactician in getting the ball away, he was regarded almost as indispensable. Will built, but of medium height, he never hesitated in challenging a burlier opponent. One in- stance which stands out in the memory was the checking of Jenkins, the powerful Wig centre, at Wilderspool, when this visitor seemed certain to score. Jenkins was running strongly and Tilley dashed across and brought' his man' down unceremoniously, no one being more surprised than Jenkins himself. Tilly always showed rare courage. His touch-kicking invariably showed good judgment, rivalling Fish, whose skill in this respect. was remarkable.

What he lacked in speed he more than maclA up in his quick-witted action in approaching oncoming opponents. He seemed to be able 'co anticipate which direction they would track. The crowning achievement in his football career was when he become the proud possessor of a Northern Union Cup medal, al. the result .of Warrington's great victory in fhs final tie at Manchester against Oldham, in 1907. Five years later he ceased his connectiln with Warrington and went to Runcorn, who were then experiencing a shortage of goad players.

TRIBUNAL TO SIT NEXT. WEEK.

Their claims for exemption from milita:y service having been rejected by the Warrington Advisory Committee, between forty and fif tv men in the groups called up for the 20th inst are to appear before the Tribunal next week The first sitting takes place on Monday. Th proceedings will be conducted in private. _

Recruiting has not been at all -brisk in War- rington this week, though it has not been negligible. Not so many as were anticipated have taken advantage of the reopening of the attestation office at the Police Station. While a considerable number of those wnc• have attested have called for their armlets this week, many are still indifferent. They should remember it is the King's desire that the cloth symbol of patriotism should be vyor•t. From to-day onward rejected and discharpd men may obtain their armlets.

On Monday armlets will also be given out at the Lymm Conservative Club Assembly Room to Groups 1,2, 3,4, 5, 6 and 7.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
BRAVO, TERRITORIALS!

Honours are coming thick and fast Upon our local Territorials, whose commander, Colonel Brereton Fairclough, has been made a Companion of the Most Distinguished Order of St. Michael and St. George. The Battalion have now left the Division with which they have been actively connected in the very fore- front of the fighting for the last ten months, and have joined another Division vVith whom it is presumed they will have a well-earned rest from actual hostilities. Their old General, in bidding them good-bye, spoke of the great reputation they had made for them- selves through having ,put their backs into their work in true Lancashire fashion. Major Crosfield has been given the temporary com- mand of a Regular Regiment. Other local honours, which we report to-day, show how well the sons of Warrington and district—Ter- ritorials and Regulars—are doing their duty by themselves and their country.

PRIVATE JAMES FITTON.

NVell-Liked By Comrades Anti Died A

Noble Death.

Mrs. Fitton, who' now resides at St. Helens, but up to a short time ago lived in Foster-street, Warrington, has received news that her hus band, Private James Fitton, of the South Lancs. Regiment, has died of wounds received in actioic Several letters have been received, one . which, from Private J. Davies, a stretcher. bearer, runs: "Will you allow me as one of the last to attend your husband before his death offer you, on behalf of the section to which he belonged, our sympathy in your great loss. I am glad to Say, and I think you will be pleas •d to hear, that he died whilst on duty, and that he died quite bravely. He was also well like amongst his comrades." A letter from Nurse E. A. Long, sister Li charge of the hospital in which Private Fitton died, reads :--" It is with deepest regret that write to tell you of the death of your husban, which took place here. He was brought i.! early in the afternoon, practically unconscious, and he only lived a few hours. It may Comfort you to know that he did not suffer much pain, and we were with him all the time up to the end, which came quite peacefully. He will ly• buried in the cemetery quite close to the hoq- pital, and a little cross, with his name an i that of his regiment on it, will mark his grave." A chaplain also wrote a letter in which he said that Private Fitton died a noble death, aad that he muttered a few words, viz., "Love to my wife," before becoming unconscious. PRIVATE THOS. RANDLES.

Death From Dysentery And Enteric In • A Malta Hospital.

Mr. and Mrs. Thomas Handles, 23, Percy- street, Warrington, have this week received the tragic news from the War Office bf their son's death from dysentery and enteric whilst lying in the St. Andrew's Hospital, Malta.. The de- ceased, who was only 19 years of age, 'was well known and highly esteemed by a large circle of friends and admirers. He was attached to the Ist Field Ambulance of the Royal Naval Divi- sion, being drafted to the Front in February of last year. He had only been in training for about six weeks ere he witnessed the horrors of warfare. As a youth he was educated at the Holy Trinity Schools and worshipped at the Working Men's Mission. He was employed at Messrs. Crosfield and Sons. Ltd., in their printing department, and was extremely inter- ested in. the Scout movement, being a membez of the works' Boy Scouts. Whilst in Gallipoli he twice contracted dysentery, but not of such a serious nature to ensure his being invalided to hospital. On this occasion, however, he has not been so fortunate as to recover. The last letter from the unfortunate Private, dated December Bth, 1915, and writteii from the Malta Hospital, is especially interesting, and, at the same time, cheery : " I am glad to tell you that I am getting on fine, thanks to the generous treatment meted out to me. I am anxiously awaiting the time when they will send me to dear old England, but I am afraid it will not be this side Christ- mas. At least, I hope to have a good time here. You will have to start getting ready that 'burst-up' soon, for I have not two ounces of flesh on my bones now. I am glad I left the Peninsula when I did, because I believe they are having rotten weather, 'what ‘with rain and snow. You will have to save my Christmas dinner, not forgetting the pudding and the turkey. You see I have not had enough of 'Turkey' yet. I have lost all my bits of curios that I had; in fact, I have lost every- thing. When you leave the Peninsula yol

have to leave everything behind you, and, ‘of course, you never see anything again. I seem to have had bad luck with my things, what with losing my kit-bag and now my other mementoes. I received a letter from Mr. Jones, my Sunday School teacher, when out in Tur- key, but have been unable to answer it as yet. Tell him the.reason for my not so doing."

===== 0002601_19160115_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====
WOUNDED.

GUNNER HAROLD 1 'RAISON.

Wounded By Shrapnel In France.

Mr. and Mrs. Thomson, 141, Wellfield-street, Warrington, have this week, through the medium of a nurse in the 24t4 General Hos- pital, France, learned that their son—Gunner Harold Thomson, attached to the Royal Garri- son Artillery—has been wounded in action. His injuries- consist of shrapnel wounds in the head, left arm and left leg, and right hand, yet, in spite of his misfortune, he is progressing satisfactorily Gunner Thomson joined His Majesty's Forces in November of 1914, and was employed under his father in the pattern shop at Messrs. Crosfield's works. Eighteen years of age, he was educated at the People's College, and worshipped at the Working Men's Mission, also being a member of the Young Men's Class. Before being drafted to the Front he was stationed in Ireland. Gunner Thomson has had more than one narrow escape in the war, for, in one of his letters a while ago, he states: "We were just sitting down to dinner when we heard a roar, likened unto an express train thundering through a station, which I knew from experience was a German coal-box.' came straight for our billet, and the old Be!-

GUNNER H. THOMSON. ' Wounded.

gian lady with whom we stayed clasped her hands and ran-into the officers' room, but I sat in a corner trusting my God. Suddenly the floor rocked and we ascended into the air a little, gradually coming down; and, wonder of wonders, the shell had not exploded. Had it have done so, we should all have been blown to atoms, but we succeeded in making our escape before the second was sent over." Fritz Gets Excited.

In a letter dated November 6th, he writes "You will bn noticing that my letters are be- coming less plentiful each week, due to the fact that I have very little time for myself. To-night is our last night in the trenches, and, as luck happens, my officer has gone out to dinner. I am up each rmorning at 6 a.m. to prepare breakfast and to clean the clothing of the officers, and, believe me, they want some cleaning after a day in the trenches. The trenches are terrible, the heavy rains causing them to cave in, in many places. Unfortu- nately, five men were buried alive the other day, owing to such an occurrence. "Without exaggeration, we are up to the knees, so you can imagine what it is like. We were in action .one day last week and old Fritz got excited because we persisted in knocking down his front line parapets with our bowls, so he started throwing 15-inch shells over to us in return. You will judge for yourselg what kind of an explosion they make when I tell you that the concussion will break windows a good mile away. The shells, which you can see coming from a distance, look like small black specks, growing larger and larger as they 'approach, and then up she goes, and if it does not hit anything but earth the crater she makes would very nearly bury a tramcar. "It is a fine sight to watch the shells burst, but one has to be careful and keep under good cover, for when they burst chunks of steel from the shells travel almost half a mile and are almost red-hot when they drop." Patriotic Family.

Gunner Thomson comes of a fine patriotic family, for his mother's uncle was killed in the memorable Relief of Lucknow. \Another relative some years ago was pensioned off from the Navy, in which he had served practically a lifetime-.

The subject of our photograph is of a bright and sunny disposition and has a large circle of friends and admirers in the district. His father and sister departed for France on Thursday last on a visit to him.

PRIVATE H. TRAVERS.

Lost An Eye And Then—Captured.

Private Harry Travers, of the Lancashire Fusiliers, brother of Mr. Thomas Travers, of 5, Friars'-lane, is a prisoner of war at the

Eriedrichsfeld camp, Germany, having been captured-during the battle of the Aisne. He has been writing regularly to his brother, and in all his letters has asked for food. On a postcard, bearing the date December 13th, and received recently by his brother, Private Travers, after making his customary appeal for food, states :-- • . •

" I might as well tell you that I have lost my left eye. I was hit with a bullet:, . . I hope you spent a happy Christmas and New Year." Private Travers has previouply been reported "missing." _

He Hied with his brother for a considerable time, his parents being dead. Thirty-two years of age and a Reservist, he was called up im- mediately upon the, outbreak of war, having previously been employed as a boilermaker by Messrs. E. Forshaw and Son, Ltd. He was educated at the Mount School, and took a very great interest in football.

TWO BANK CLERKS MISSING.

First Warrington Man To Be Captured By Bulgars.

The first Warrington man who is believed to have fallen into the hands of the Bulgarians is the eldest son of Mr. Alfred Hallwood, of The Mount, Orford. He is Corporal John B.

Though he has only seen twelve months in the Army, Corporal Hallwood has served in France as well as in the Balkans. He was in the western sphere of the war for a month or two, when his regiment was sent to Salonika.. He took part in the early fighting, and, as a result, it is believed that he fell into the hands of the enemy. Previous to joining the Army, Corporal Hall- wood was a clerk at the Warrington Savings Bank and was very popular amongst a large circle of friends.

News hils this week been received that Lance- Corporal Gillies who, prior to the war, was .it the staff of the Warrington Savings Bank; :s missing. • "")

JOINING THE COLOURS.

Social Evening For Men Who have Been "Called Up."

In order that the men in the four groups who are to join the Colours next week may have an opportunity of spending a sociable evening before temporarily leaving civil life, a whist drive, folloived by a dance, was held in the John Crosfield Memorial Hall on Thursday evening.

Fifti-two tables had to be requisitioned for the whist, file room being full. At 10 p.m. light refreshments were served, after which the room was cleared for dancing. The dance programme was well arranged, and the evening proved very enjoyable. The prizes for the whist were won by the fol- lowing :—Ladies: 1, Mrs. Clayton; 2, Miss L. Wodtton. Gents: 1, Miss Ethel Turner; 2, Miss Woods. The last two ladies played gentlemen- hands.

The committee responsible for the general arrangements of the evening was comprised of the Misses West, Miss Dearden. Messrs. Harry West, George Holmes and Douglas Peacock. The M.C.'s were Messrs. E. A. Sanders, Geo. Holmes, A. Gleaves and F. Millington. Refreshments were served by a number of ladies in charge of Miss B. West. Her helpers were Miss Lingard, Miss Bell, Miss Nora Pem- berton, Miss Ethel Pemberton, Miss Ada West and Miss Clarissa West.

The dance music was supplied by Messrs. Louis Hales (pianist) and Ronald 5/.ers (violin- ist).

The proceeds, after expenses have been de- ducted, will be handed over to the Women's Sewing Fund.

ANTI-COMPULSION.

Proposed Local _Campaign And Mass

Meeting.

The Military Service (No. 2) Bill, now before the House of Commons, was considered at a conference held at the Warrington I.LU. rooms on Thursday, under the auspices of the No-Compulsion Committee, and the loe u.; branches of the Independent Labour Party, British Socialist Party, the Union of Democraiie Control, and the No-Conscription Fellowship. After a lengthy discussion, the following resolution was unanimously agreed to: "That this meeting of representatives of the above Societies pledges itself to oppose by every mean within its power the insidious attempt that is now being made to enforce upon the nation the principle of Compulsory service. Believing that any means of compulsory service would be direct negative to the best traditions of British liberty and a violent repudiation of the volun• tary principle, gilder which four million of on,* countrymen have taken up arms in defence of their liberties; and that as we consider no cas. for military necessity has been established, we are convinced that it is a commencement of an 'attempt to take advantage of the critkal positioa of the nation to enforce upon it a system V industrial and military compulsion." The No-Compulsion Committee are organising a campaign of open-air meetings for next week, ' and these will be followed by a mass meeting. at which the committee hope that a prominent politician will be the chief speaker.

. DANGEROUS WAR MEMENTOES.

Another serious accident arising out of the practice of soldiers sending home war memen- toes took place at Tornaby-on-Tees on Saturday. A workman at a shell factory, named Chisholm, took the fuse of a German shell into the works and showed it to another workman named Wilks. The latter was trying to take it to nieces when it exploded. His hand was blown off, one eye was seriously injured, and he was badly cut In the neck. Chisholm escaped, but another workman was struck on the leg by a piece of the fuse and injured. The windows of the building were also damaged. Wilks was removed to the 'Stockton and Thornaby.-on-Tees Hospital.

.

The lowest temperature ever recorded on .hr, 'earth was taken at Werchojansk, in the -n lerior of Siberia, January 15th, 1885. It was 90 •degrees and a fraction below zero.

Werchojansk is in the latitude of the pok of cold. There the earth is. frozen to a depth of about 100 feet, and in the warmekt season it never thaws.

'Absurdity of Overwork. Dr. C. Hutchinson Eely, a brain expert, was 41i-qcucsing the new cure for progressive paralyzas, a malady common to brain workers.

"Tuberculin has cured a third of the cases 't had been tried on," he said. "Hence it may .1,0 called a pretty good cure. But a better cure for the diseases due to overwork is rest."

Dr. Eeely thumped the table vigorously. "When a professional man tells me he is too busy to take a rest," he cried, "I tell him lil- ts like a workman who is too busy to sharpen his tools."

•........,

P,ELGIUM OF THE EAST.

Thrilling Storis3 And Facts

About Serbia.

MRS. T. J. RIDGWAVS LECTURE.

The stirring story of Serbia's history of tra- vail, besides instances of, her quaint customs, was told by Mrs. T. J. Ridgway in a brilliant lecture in •Lymm Grammar School on Wednes- day afternoon. From a tastefully decorated platform Mrs. Ridgway gave a vivid description of the life ard doings of this gallant mountainous people. Her opening remarks embraced early erbian history, intermingled with pl.easing stories of the kings and queo2A of bygone days.. Thence she went on to speak of the present war and of the great hatred these people had for the Turks.

With emphasis she explained that before the arrival of the Turks, at the end of the Middle Ages, Serbia had been a great and prosperous nation, with ports in the Adriatic. Owing to the advantage of communication with other nations she grew to be 4 highly -civilised and Christian State. "Indeed," said Mrs. Ridg- way, "there are still standing, • or were until recently, churches built by the clever crafts- men and decorated by the fresco painters of

France." From 1400 to 1804, she continued, Serbia was constantly massacred and pillaged, many of her people were reduced to slavery, or were forcibly converted to the religion. of Mahomet:, One over-powering fact dominated all Serbian history—the secular struggle against the Turks. "It is a .curious irony," went on the gifted speaker, "that it is the. Serbian race which for four centuries has furnished Austria with its bravest defenders against the Turks and against revolts within, and it is a sad fact to think how Many to-day are compelled to fight against their own kinsmen." Austria always cast covetous eyes on this small Balkan State. She thought it so easy of annexation, and, in fact, constantly threatened invasion, but as con- stantly drew back before the diplomatic inter-, vention-of the Triple Entente of Great Britain, France and Russia. "In 1914," continued Mrs. Ridgway, "this gallant race of .people, ex- hausted by the terrible experiences of warfare of the few years previously, were called upon again to take up arms; yet she was unwilling, for at that time she was in dire need of a long Ipeace--to recoup her finances, to gather the neglected harvest and attend to the land—and, above .all, to re-organise her decimated army. The menace, however, of Austria, fully cog- nisant .of Serbia's exhaustion, _could not be overlooked, and because to refuse to fight would have been..false to all traditions of this brave, simple folk, she :again called': her -sons' to her side and bade them fight for indenendence and • for liberation. And what a fight in very truth." (Applause.) Serbia's Sufferings. •

All Europe knew that the Austro-German third offensive against the Serbs was meant to crush them entirely. It was intended that the enemy should join hands with the Turks and Bulgars in order that an immense front might be drawn from Constantinople to the Baltic Sea. The time of year, the impracticability of the roads, the enormous difficulties of their extended front, the depleted army which had 'so recently and victoriously routed an enemy three times as strong, made the stoutest heart quail with fear. Theq; came a day when, owing to Bulgarian treason, there was no more ammunition. From France and Russia munitions had been sent with feverish haste, but they had not reached the Serbs owing to the Bulgars and Turks destroying the communications. The sad retreat, the refugees of all ages flying from home for safety. the atrocities and barbarities and awfulness of ,those days of sorrow would one day .be known, and, alas! they had their counterpart in other lands. Serbia might truly be called "The Bel- gium of the East." It was natural that Austria-Hungary should view Serbia with greedy eyes, 'for was she not the high road to Salonika, the important Greek Dort? The intention was truly to crush the Serbians ruthlessly, but with unheard-of bravery this mountain folk defeated their enemy and drove them back across the frontier, closing theg,eby the road from Vienna to Constantinople., This was at the end of 1914. Surely this victory could be called one of the bravest feats of the great war.-

- Perhaps the outstanding characteristic of the Serbian people was the far-reaching brotherhood which was inborn and fostered from infancy by the customs of the country. "The family," said Victor Berard, "the commune, the nation, the race, have a sense of fraternity which is not found in the same degree among any of the neighbouring peoples." Home Life and a Hopeless Theology.

Hereabouts the lecturer spoke of the home life of the Serbians. The garments worn by the Serbs, said Mrs. Ridgway i were invariably home grown and home made. The Serb was a true gentleman, as our nurses and doctors testified. He was full of gratitude, and never complained. The people lived mostly on fruits, vegetables, and a great quantity of rice was consumed. Meat, as a rule, was only eaten in winter. Pork was the chief food, but mutton, beef, fresh or home-cured ham were • eaten occasionally. Spring water was the usual beverage, but cer- tain homemade wines were drunk.

The religious views of the Serbian peasant were crude and original, and inherited from his ancestors, who lived long before modern culture sought to slay old beliefs. His belief, was so intertwined with heathen mythology and old legends that a hopeless theology was often the result.

At a meeting of the Refugees' Committee ,of Beechwood, held on Tuesday evening, it was decided,. owing to the present outbreak of scarlet fever in Lymm and the surrounding districts, to make a temporary hospital inside Beechwood, with a view to being prepared should the outbreak assume more serious aspects.

SELF-NEGLECT.

Partly The Cause Of Retired Miner's

Death.

Unusual 'evidence was given at an inquest held at Chorlton's Farm on 3londay evening, before Mr. H. C. Yates. „

The deceased was Mr.. Alichard Toft (73), a retired miller, of Agclen-lane, High Legh, who had been discovered lying dead on the hearth of • • , his home.

Miss Hannah Chorlton, a neighbour, stated that the wife of the deceased approached her near her cottage and asked if she had seen anything of her husbantl, as she (Mrs. Toft) .had not seen her husband for some time. She, also imparted the information that -there was* strange man in her hOnso, and, thinking some- thing was the matter, Miss Chorlton went to investigate. She discovered the.deceased in the manner stated. He was fully dressed. . The evidence showed that the house was in very dirty and neglected condition and a ver- dict was given that death was due to natural causes and self-neglect.

PRIVATE GEORGE LEIGH.

Unfit For Further Foreign Service.

Mrs. L. Leigh, who resides— at Newfields, Lymm, has this week' received the news that her son—Driver George Leigh, attached to the Army Service Corps—has been rejected as medically unfit for further foreign service. Some thirteen months age Driver Leigh was drafted to Alexandria, Egypt, on transport service, but returned to England about three months ago. Since landing he 'has been an inmate of a London Hospital, suffering from general breakdown. Driver Leigh. enlisted im- mediately on the outbreak of war. Aged. 27 years, he was employed at Messrs. Robertsol's Steel Works, Latchford, and worshipped at the Parish Church. He was a local footballer of considerable repute and took great interest in every branch of sport.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====
Picturesque Marriages.

A Serbian peasant, happily married himself, was ambitious to see his son married, and a peasant boy, hardly eighteen, was constrained to marry at his father's desire and according to his choice. Economical reasons prompted this act of the father, for workers were badly needed on the little farmsteads. The bride was usually a few years older than her husband. An elabor- ate and detailed ceremonial accompanies the be- trothal and wedding. At the latter the bride- groom, with his witnesses, office bearers and friends, after Music, singing and toasting under the parental roof, rode forth to the home of the bride, his arrival being announced by the firing of rifles and pistols, and the playing of bagpipes or violins. Meanwhile, the bride awaits in

one of the wooden huts in the courtyard the coming of her bridesmaids with the wedding gown, the gift of the father-in-law. When the bride .was gowned she was led to her nearest male relative, and, looking through a small wreath of flowers, she had her first glimpse of the bridegroom, while pistols and rifles were being fired.

Then followed the ordeal of kissing the hands of, every male guest. Minute Preparations for Death.

"The Serbian has no fear .of death," con- tinued the speaker. "Nearly every peasant has in his loft boards specially cut for his own coffin. Nrost old women have sewn in their belts money fR• their burial, and a wealthy peasant will have stored away a barrel of whisky, to be drunk for the good of his soul. While the body of a dead person is lying in the house the family eat nothing, and the house remains un- swept. After the burial the accumulated dust is at once removed, but the brush used for the purpose must be thrown away. Bread, salt, and a glass of red wine are left on a table near the dead man, as it is believed that his soul tarries for some time in the house, and may be hungry. . • . Women never sing during the first year of mourning, nor do they wear jewels or flowers; while working they often recite in monotonous tones the virtues and kind acts of the deceased, and a woman delights in calling herself the 'grey-black cuckoo bird.' (Laughter.) It must be a great deprivation for the peasant girls and women not to sing, for they constantly sing lyrical songs of their own composing, just as the boys improvise heroic poems." A tone of sober religiousness pervaded the life of a Serb, and some of the traditional prayers were fervent and beautiful. Every daily duty or action was nearly always rrefaced by the ,

sign of the Cross, and the words "In the name of God, help us, 0 God !" The first toast at a banquet was to God's glory. - -

In Honour of Gladstone.

Having touched on the Serbs' love of poetry, Mrs. Ridgway next alluded to their love .4 politics, and asserted that they were widely versed in the policies of the Great Powers. In a village in Southern Serbia a travdler, some little time ago,_ found the principal iiln was I called "At the Great Gladstone." .The inn- keeper and his peasant customers Were so ' pleased with Mr. Gladstone for mini* 'fe Aus- tria, "Hands off Bosnia!" that they kdttaured him in that way. Before concluding, with pathos it Voice, the Lecturer said that just now 'Were many homes 'of weeping in Serbia: to* the children laughed and danced, they Allne -sang merrily at their games and told eedhlytt'lYer the wonderful story of father's great deeds, emulat- ing in baby fashion the heroism of their ances- tors; while the little lads continually asked: "Am I not old enough yet, my mother?" And the women! The women, with anguish of heart, but the stern self-suppression, as befits the wives and daughters of .heroes, stood wait- ing—waiting until the shadows fall, till the d'arkness crept upon, their vigil—waiting for what the hour of dawn might bring, a type of divine,, patient love. Might the dawn of the great day of compensation soon break upon their aching vision! • (Loud applause.) The Rector thanked the lady for so kindly giving them an insight into the life of this valorous race and hoped that on another oc.:a- sion she would again .charm her many friends and admirers.

Immediately after the lecture the audience were provided with tea and refreshments at a nominal charge, and a surplus stock of goods from the recent sale of work, on behalf of funds for Serbia, was sold for this worthy cause.

MRS. YOUNG-OLD AND CHEEKY.

A grey-headed Irish woman named Mary Young, was sentenced to 14 days' imprisonment, at :Daresbury Petty Sessions: on Tue*clay. _for begging at Walton, and for assaulting P.C. J. IL Rough. The evidence was to the effect that the con- stable noticed the prisoner begging in Blies- raere-road, and arrested her. It was stated that out of 20 appearances she had made at the Court, 19 were for begging. On the way to The Police Station the prisoner struck the constable, and continually used filthy language. Asked where she came from, the prisoner replied "From Dublin, but I was brought up in Lancashire." "Begging," she added, "is not a great crime, it isn't as if it were murder."

CHURCH MISSIONS.

Society Which Made Progress Last

Year.

Financial and membership progress were reported at the annual meeting of the Stockton Heath 'Missionary Society',' held' in the Parish Room on' Monday evening, the Rev. James Collins (president) being in the chair. The reverend gentleman reviewed the work of. the past year, which, he said, had been very successful. They had now between 40 .and 50 members. In all, just under £2O had been raised, £l3 of which was due to the collecting box system. These boxes were taken home by the members, and the latter dropped contribu- tions into them regularly. On the year they were nearly £p better off than the previous year, and the societies for whom they had worked greatly appreciated their increased donations. The election of new officers resulted in Mrs. H. Davey taking the place of Mrs. J..lt. Woods hon. secretary (resigned), and Mrs. Hankey vice Mrs. Worrall (also resigned), as treasurer. The Rev. 'James Collins was re-elected president and the Rev. E. W. Clarke vice-president.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0172.txt | included=true | included =====
AIAKING SHELL-CASES.

Volunteers Who Are Doing Useful

Work.

Drilling, shooting, shell-making, and carrying on "Business as usual" is the part that a num- ber of Stockton Heath gentlemen are taking in the war.

For many reasons they are unable to join his .Majesty's Forces, so they have done the next best thing and joined the Voluntary Train- ing Corps. For the past few :weeks they have been having a Christmas vacation, but soon they will be hard .at work again. Though, during the Christmas holidays, drills have not been taking place, the shell-making has .continued. Every Sunday .morning the Volunteers work in an important munition fac- tory and turn shells out. This has been going on for, about a couple of months, and by now tilt Men are quite experts. In private life they are clerks, architects, &c., and before the war they were entirely unused to machinery of any description. ' But they were quite adaptable, and between them the have turned out hun- dreds of shell cases. The most remarkable fact of all is that they do not leave any " scrap " they have not spoilt a single shell case. In all the stages of their complicated' task they are skilled. The steel comes to them in bars, they, but it into lengths, groove and bore the pieces until they are finished shell cases. Though the work is done entirely voluntarily and is unpaid, it is absolutely satisfactory and is a credit to the Volunteers.

PAD GATE.

MORE SCHOLARS.

Wesleyans Make Progress In The Sunday School. The congregational tea in connection will the Wesleyan- Church waS 1104 j 1.4 the School room on Thn7clay evening. 27. • The Rev. A. J. Morton, speaking on Sunday school work, said that there were 7,530 Wesleyan Sunday Schools in England, and 6,000 of that number had young people attending public wor- ship. Reid, of Warrington. gave a very ' inspiring address, and congratulated th Church on the way they were carrying out the Sunday school work in Padgate. He empha- sised the desirability and necessity of training the children, especially by encouraging them to identify themselves with the church in its ser- vices. He thought. perhaps, in the past too great a barrier had been placed between the church and Sunday school. We welcomed the children in the church and were pleased to see them. The .period, that existed between' child- hood and manhood, like the blossom, might go into fruit all right, or it might be blasted an ! spoiled. We must realise how much depended on what we did during the few hours we had the children on Sunday. We had, in our hands the pliable stuff, fresh material. Upon which impressions could be made, which in all proba- bility would be retained until the end of life. In -Scotland to-day there were 120,000 childre.) who had never been connected -with any Sunday school. All the statistics showed a falling off4i Sunday school attendances, and it was for us to be alive and leave no stone unturned to rem?dy these decreases. GREAT SANKEY.

A MOBILE FIRE.

Cotton Bales Alight On Motor Wagon.

At about eight o'clock on Tuesday evening it was discovered that a quantity of cotton, the property of the Rex Mills C 0.,; Ltd., Middleton, had caught fire while being conveyed along the high road at Great Sankey, on a steam motor wagon belonging to Messrs. Crossley-, motor haulers, Victoria Buildings, :Manchester. 'ln- formation was immediately conveyed to the Warrington Fire Station, and the brigade were soon on the spot. On their arrival, however, they found that the nearest hydrant was 500 yards away, tiut they worked vigorously, and saved two of The four tons of cotton. About 4.50 on Wednesday afternoon information was again received at the Fire Station that the cotton, which had been left on the roadside, had again got alight. Superintendent Turner dispatched the motor fire engine and a comple- ment of men, in, charge of. Deputy-Superin- tendent Cook, who, upon their arrival, found that three more bales of cotton had ignited.' The appliances were got to work, and in a short time the flames were extinguished. The damage, which is covered by insurance, is estimated at £3OO or £4OO.

VOLUNTEERS.

Platoon Formed As 4esult Of Meeting.

A 'Public meeting was held in the Sankey School to try to raise a branch of the South-West •Latcashire Regiment,: which should include both Sankey and Penketh. A well-filled room was the answer to the advertised invitation, which also brought gratifying results. Mr. W. Furness was in the chair, ond intro- duced the subject with clearness and forcibility, emphasising the apparent inertia amongst the farmers' sons, who, under the Present circum- stances, were exempt from military law, and showed a lack of interest in their duty to the State and their fellow men.

Platoon Commander P.A. Frost, C.O. Grap- penhall detachment, Cheshire Regiment, gave a short description of the origin and work done by the Volunteers, pointing out each man's duty to himself and the State. It was up to him to be doing while it was not too late. At least two hours per week might be laid aside especi- ally for drill purposes, with lasting benefit. Mr. Walter Ashton, one of the joint secre- taries of the Lancashire Volunteer Regiment, also made an earnest appeal to all those to whom it applied. _ _

When the vote was put to the meeting it was carried unanimously.

A committee of eight, four to represent San- key and four for Penketh, was formed. They were :—Sankey: Messrs. Cairns, Foster, lax- table and Shoobridge; Penketh: Messrs. Davies, D. Houghton, T. Jackson and Smith Tunstall; with Mr. J. Littler as secretary, and Mr. IT. Goulden as his assistant.

The drill instructor for Sankey is Mr. Foster, and for Penketh, Messrs. Mainley and Davies. The nights for instruction are: Sankey. Tues_ day and Thursday; Penketh, Tuesday and Sat- urday. Final details are to be arranged by the committee as soon as possible.

RUNCORN RURAL COUNCIL

Asked To Fall In With County'3

Scheme.

A meeting of the Runcorn Rural District. Council was held on Tuesday, Mr. T. Antwis- presiding. A letter was read from Dr. Meredith Young: (County. medical officer) stating that his com- mittee had agreed to undertake the home visita- tion Of cases of measles as part of their schema in regard to the Notification of Births Act, pro-- -videcl the Rural Council agreed to this course.. He felt sure thetCouncil would see the wisdom of combining this work, and while his committee did not anticipate any objections to the pro- posal, he would be glad to hear from the Clerk as soon as convenient. •

'Dr. Adams (the medical officer) said that measles were now compulsorily notifiable, and the County Council intended to work with the Education Authorities in the matter. It would be wise for the Rural Council to co-operate witk the County Council. Otherwise, the Council would have to burden themselves with officials, especially in, times of epidemics.

After -a short discussion it was decided to, make inquiries as to the cost of the scheme and! how the charges would be met. Mr. Woodward moved that the next meeting' of the Council be a fortnight hence, and that after that the meetings be held monthly. , This was seconded and agreed to.

PENKETH.

THE OLD FOLK.

96. Guests Enjoy Themselves Al.

The annual treat for the "old folk" of Pen- keth and Sankey was held in the Wesleyan Schoolroom on Friday evening, Dr. Murray, president, being once more in the chair. There were 96 guests present. One hundred and forty invitations were sent out, but many were not able to accept them. After tea, .a first-class concert was thoroughly enjoyed by many -friends, besides the guests of the evening, who included sixteen Belgians from Hall Nook. The artistes and songs were: Miss Edith Johnston, who sang "Land of Hope and Glory" and "0 Rest in the Lord"; Mr. Draper, "Star of Normandy " - and "My- Sweetheart when A Boy"; Miss E. Taylor, "When the Heart is Young" and "There is only one England." A trombone solo was given by Mr. E. Goulden, entitled "The Death of Nelson," and a violin sOlo by Mr.. Bert Smith. Mr. Pearce. sang "Anchored" and "The Young Royalist,' and Mr. T. W. Roberts gave two humorous selections. Mrs. Stevenson was the accompanist. Each of the artistes was heartily applauded, and no mis- take could be made as to their services being appreciated. Dr. Murray, in a short speech, said they were indebted to Mr. Richard Routledge for the treat, as he had charge of :the arrangements. • Since the last treat seven of the old folk had left the district and ten had passed away. He touched sympathetically on the fact that many there had sons fighting in the war. He hoped they .might be spared to return, and that each one present.might be able to come again in a year's time.

Mr. J. T. Ambler thanked the Doctor for pre- siding, and said he hoped in a year's time we should have finished with the war, and all be able to meet again. The evening was rounded off with supper,. and parcels were handed to each guest—pipes and tobacco to the men, and something special in the way of grocery to the women. As usual, Mr. James Woodward had made himself responsible for the decoration of the MOM.

WHITLEY.

A HOMEWARD-BOUND HORSE.

At Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday, William Wilding of The Limes, Whitley, was charged with being the driver of a horse and float and not having the same under control at Stockton Heath, on the 16th ult. P.C. Crumpt,on stated that he saw the horse and float in London-road, Stockton Heath, with- out a driver. The horse was proceeding in the direction of Whitley, and there being consider- able traffic about, it was dangerous. The defendant said he had been doing some shopping in Stockton Heath, and had left the horse with its head turned in the direction of home. As soon as he left 'it the animal started to go home. He was fined ss.

Never let others do what you can do for volr- self. You thereby strengthen your own pow‘?is, independence and fitness to cope with the vicis.i- tudes of life.
