"DIVIDE THEM UP."

Suggested Alteration Of .Guardians' Contract System.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0191.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. JOLLEI'S " I kill 11 kit NiNt,.'

An alteration in the method of serving out contracts was suggested by Mr. W. J. Shaw. at the fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians on Wednesday morning, Mr. A Jolley presiding. _ _ .

Mr. ShaAwviii resolution was: "That the Board reconsider the present system of dealing with tenders- for supplies, and deem it expedient to make any alterations that may be agreed upon." He alleged that the way in which tenders were served out was not in, the best interests of the people and township in general. On a visit to. the Padgate Cottage Homes he had found that in many instances, a person who contracted for certain articles, but did not contract for the whole of the articles, was ruled out of order. On the last occasion they had contracts for margarine, which were given out af 545. per cwt. He thought at the time that this was rather a big price. In town be made inquiries, only to find that had the contract been given out in another way we should have saved 9s. per cwt. If this was not the time for economising, he did not know when that time would arrive, and he suggested that an alteration of the system should be made. By doing so they would give a great deal of satisfaction to the trades- people. It was only fair that all tradesmen should have an equal chance, and it was very 'unfair that a man,who failed to contract for the whole•should be prevented from supplying a portion.• if contracts were allowed to be divided Ur more, a great amount of money would be saved.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0192.txt | included=true | included =====
Pious Ilosolutions

The -Chairman: You arc not going to save money by passing pious resolutions. Over and over again the matter has been carefully con- sidered by the Board. Ile added that lie did not say they had a perfect system, but it was much better than they had years ago. In regard to food of all kinds,. they bad Set up a definite standard of quality, below which they would not go. They did not want ony of the inmates to have rubbishy stuff, and all contractors had to tender for a certain quality. They did not always choose the cheapest. , Mr. Shaw: My view is equal quality at equal prices.

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell wanted Mr. Shaw to offer some definite suggestion, *and not run off into: generalities.

Miss- Broadbent seconded Mr. Shan's motion, and hoped that they would have the power of dividing contracts. For years she had been wanting to do this, but she had always been put off.

The Chairman: The Board has always decided against it for perfectly sound reasons. Mr. W. Brooks: It might be advisable for a sub-committee of members who advocated the change to draw up a report on the matter and present it to the Board.

- The Chairman : If they do that we shall be glad to consider it.

Miss Broadbent: If we bring it before the next Board?

Chairman's "Fair Warning."

The Chairman: If you do that I give you fair warning that you might have to increase the clerical staff, for, instead ,of there being eight contractors, there might be fifty. A lot of book- keeping would be entailed, and you might have to have half a dozen more clerks.

The Rev. A. At. Mitchell pointed•out that Oa) Local Government Board had asked them to reduce work, and therefore they did not want more clerks.

On the motion being put to the meeting, four toted in its favor and five against. The Chairman: "The thing has dropped now. There won't be a sub-committee or anything else."

BLOOD•POISONING FROM A HAIRPIN.

An inquest was held at 131arkburn this week oft :Lizzie Dix, 32, of Snig Brook, who died from blood-poisoning following the removal of a pimple- from her. chin by the use of a hairpin. I WOMAN'S WORLD.

• factories. Then the women of the United States determined to make New York the fashion centre of the American Continents, if not of the world, and for a year it seemed as if suc- cess would crown their patriotic efforts. But when Paris bad settled down somewhat to the now state of things, the great artists formed a syndicate for the defence of French fashions. with M. Poiret at the head. The workgirls were unearthed from the munition factories and set to work in new buildings erected for the.pur- pose by the Syndicate whose heads spent their time alternately there and in the *trenches.

France Relains Supremacy. Beautiful new fabrics were designed and made in these buildings, and new fashions. were created and successfully launched. The most beautiful models were dispatched to New York. and two hundred exquisite gowns were sent by the Syndicate to the Panama Exhibition. Never before had Franca shown such perfect and delicately • beautiful confections, and the American firms unanimously yielded the palm to their rival, for their exhibits were poor in- deed in comparison, Thus the French overcame all the difficulties that beset 'them, and their courage in facing and overcoming them has been well rewarded. In the world of fashions France has once more proved that in the art of dress no other nation can approach her. • The Sales.

Indiscriminate buying at the sales is unwise at any time, but this year, when most people have less money to spare, demands extra judg- ment and discrimination, if purchasers are to get full value for their outlay. Bargains are plentiful enough in every department, for the stores and big shops are clearing out everything, and have reduced much of their stock to less than cost price, but bargains are dearly bought if they turn into white elephants. The present faAlions favour short- lengths; more especially does this apply to the pinafore and tabard overdresses, which will be in fashion throughout the 'spring, and be worn probably next summer also. Two yards of narrow width fancy ot plain silk makes a slip for wearing under either of these. provided sateen be employed for the foundation. And for the overdress, a short length will amply suffice, which may be of velvet or silk. Half-yards or even less, of coloured silks in fancy 'patterns. embroidered net, or embossed velvet., are useful for renovating purposes, and are sufficient to rejuvenate a partially-Worn blouse or bodice. Passementerie, gimps and galons are also worth

buying for the trimmittg of hats and gowns. Then again anything cheap and attractive in the way of a length of woollen material for a spring costume, should be considered. for there is not likely to be a fall in the high price of woollen goods for some time to come.

The Vogue of the Berthe. Now that The berthes and cuffs of Early Victorian days have come back again, those who possess these. treasures in real lace, may bring them out, and disport them with the fullest assurance that they are wearing the latest mode. The deeper and quainter the shape of both the better, for quaintness\is in vogue this season. Any scraps of old Italian. antique Brussels and Honiton lace can be used most effectively now for guimpes and motifs, on evening corsages and on blouses. The bertho is in perfect harmony with the Early Victorian gown, whether for afternoon or evening wear. If the rarer laces are not . forthcoming various English makes, more especially Buckinghamshire and Malmes- bury, are not to be despised, for both are hand- some and effective. There will be most tempt- ing offers of lace in the sales, for much of the best French and Flemish has found its way into our shops. It has been truly said that lace is a good investment, therefore the possessor of loose cash may do worse than take advantage of the present opportunity. Lace flouneings are again in fashion, and these will prove a good purchase. A generation or two ago. bridal sets of lace, including flounces, were a popular 'pre- sent. These are to-day real treasures, for lovely evening gowns may be evolved from them. Buckinghamshire Lace. The • pillow lace of. Buckinghamshire has always compared most favourably with Conti- nental productions of the same class. It com- bines lightness with durability, for a special thread, fine but very durable, is used for its con- fection. This fineness gives that dainty appear- ance which is a characteristic of this beautiful lace. For two centuries this industry flourished, and provided •employment for thousands of labourers' wives and daughters, but the advent of machinery practically killed it. A serious effort was made at the beginning of this century to revive the making of this lace, and with a fair amount of success, but the war has seriously, affected the workers, and brought the competi- tion of the Flemish refugee lacemakers. British arts and crafts are receiving much support, and it is to be hoped that this struggling industry vk ill not .be overlooked by those who have it in their'power to aid native talent.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

The Poultry Industry. Long before the war broke out, many dwellers in the countryside deplored the dying out of the poultry industry and the sending to foreign countries of from three to four million pounds annually for eggs alone. Again and. again economists demonstrated that with the adoption of scientific methods of •production, together with a business-like system el collection and distribution, every egg required by the popula- tion of these isles could easily and profitably be produced at home. The war was needed, it ould seem, to show us our failures and short- comings, and if it should bring back, only. a. measure of prosperity to the rural population some good will have resulted from untold evil. On some of the large estates schemes are being adopted for adding to the future food supply of cottage tenants and for building Up a flourish- Jug egg and poultry industry in this country.

•Lady Onslow's Scheme. For one of the best of these Lady Onslow is responsible. In the absence of Lord Onslow she has taken over the management of his extensive property in Surrey, with the most satisfactory results. To all the tenants willing to try pig- keeping and poultry-raising, she offers to supply materials for building tho requisite houses, or to put into thorough repair any existing but dilapidated buildings. Where the tenants are without the money to purchase the stocks, Lady Onslow offers a loan to the full value of the animals and birds purchased. These are. to be repaid by easy instalments, a little less than -five per cent. interest per annum being charged. Not only is the scheme practical, but it benefits the cottagers and the country. The 'years that follow the declaration of peace will in all probabily be lean indeed,. and every pound kept in the country will advance the interests of all classes of the community. France's Two Ware.

France is not only at war with the Huns. she is fighting to maintain her position as the foun- tain head of the .world's fashions. When war broke out, all the great fashion experts in such houses as Worth. Poiret and lloneet, joined the Army, and their workgirls went inth-munition

HOME NOTES. Profitable Recipes And Useful Suggestions.

Baked Rice.

Add a. cupful of milk and two well-beaten eggs to two cupfuls of cold boiled rice. Beat gently with a fork to free from all lumps. season lightly. Turn into a buttered dish and bake 20 minutes in a moderate oven. This is a good luncheon dish or a dinner vegetable served with boiled mutton or chicken.

Steamed Leg of Mutton. Take the bone out of a small and tender leg of mutton. Chop some apple and onion very finely, season with a few cloves, and a large tablespoonful of red currant jelly. Fill the cavity, made by. removing the bone, with this stuffing, roll it tightly, and place in a double cooking pot, so that the ivater will not touch the meat. If you have not this, set The meat on a dish, set on a basin, and have water below the dish, so that the steam may cook the meat. When the meat is cooked place it in a deep dish and pour the gravy over it. Exeter Stew.

Take 21b. of beefsteak, one large carrot and turnip, two onions. loz. of butter or beef drip- ping, a bunch of herbs, salt and pepper,' half a gill of vinegar, half a gill of water or stock, and two tablespoonfuls of flour. Cut the meat Into neat pieces about 2in. square. Prepare the vegetables, and cut them into neat rings or dice. Melt the butter in a pan. add the vege- tables, and stir them about in the hot fat for ten minutes. Then add the meat,. herbs (tied together), one small teaspoonful of salt, a dust of pepper, and the vinegar arid stock. Cover tightly and simmer very gently for at least three hours. Serve with a of the vege- tables round the dish, the meat in the centre, and the gravy poured round after it .has first been thickened by mixing the ti,uir- smoothly with a little water, adding it to the gravy, ,and then allowing it to boil well. • Cream cf Cheese Soup.

Put one quart of milk, one tablespoonful each of onion and carrot. coarsely minced, and a blade of mace into 'a double boiler, and heat but not scald. Blend togethet in a saucepan a. quarter of a. cupful of butter and two level tablespoonfuls of flour. To this add the hot milk, half a cupful at a time, stirring con- stantly, and cooking after each addition. When

all The milk has been Used, return, the mixture to the double boiler, straining through the sieve to remove the mace and vegetableS, and add half a cupful of grated cheese, stirring until melted. Season with salt and pepper. and pour over the beaten yolks of two eggs. Stir constantly, that the egg may be cooked erenly. Beat with an egg whisk until covered with a line froth, and serve at once.

Cottage Pie. Take a quarter of a Spanish onion, chop up fine, and add a large teaspoonful- of chopped parsley, mix together; butter a pie dish and cover -with breadcrumbs ; first put a layer of ripe tomatoes cu t< in thin slices, next one of thin. slices of cold roast mutton or beef, then a good sprinkling of the chopped onion" and parsley; cover with bread-crumbs, proceed in the same way till the dish is full, taking care to have the bread-crumbs at the top. Sprinkle pepper and salt lightly .between the layers, fill up with good Stock, and bake' in a slow oven for three- quarters of an hour.

Children, Join This Corner! Enter The Competitions! Write Your Best! Always Be Glad!

Uncle Jack, " Examiner," Warrington.

BACK AT SCHOOL.

MY DEAR CHILDREN,

From the large .number of letters I have ieceiv ed this week I gather that most of you have not only gone back to school, but that you have been glad to go back and that you are determined to do your best in the year that lies before you. Several of my nieces and nephews, young as they are, see quite plainly how necessary it is, in view of the future, that they should prepare themselves by educating themselves as thoroughly as possible. It would be a very good thing if all our.chil- dren could understand that the future pros- perity of their country depends upon them and their devotion from now onwards. In the German schools the children have been taught for years past to educate themselves better than the children of other countries, ,and they have been given the idea that they are to grow up and beat the children of all other countries in every way they can. The German children of twenty years ago are now trying to carry out their lessons by beating in war the English children of twenty years ago. They will not do so. They themselves will be beaten, but the rising generation of Germans will be taught to beat us in trade. It would be as serious for us to be robbed of the trade of the world as to be beaten in battle. We live by trade. The Eng- lish scholars of to-day must' therefore learn all they can. A natiOn of well-taught English boys and girls may be trusted to bear in trade the German or any other nation, just as we are going to beat them on land, as we have already beaten them on the sea.

I know you will all help .by doing the best with your brains and limbs for the good of the land that bore you.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0198.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PRIZE WINNER.

I have been soniewhat puzzled this week in awarding the prize for the essay "Going Back to School." Two young ladies came very nearly together, Niece Eva AL 3iforrall and Niece Lilian.Himsworth, 15, ,Fitiherbert-street, Both have won the prize previously, and I am pleased to- see them keeping -up such excellent

form. Taking everything into consideration I have decided to award the prize to Niece Eva M. Morrell (524), 47, Price-street, Warrington. She writes Going Back to School.—After a. rather quiet but • happy holiday, do I want to go back to school? Of course it does not do to say to all that one dreads returning to school when one likes it all the time, whilst one must not say I love to go back when all the time there is a • -iv ish.for the continuance of the holidays. At New Year resolutions are always made for the good—whether they are carried out remains to be seen, but at any rate, I mean to set to work with a will. I hope to take greater interest in my lessons than ever. I wish to make rapid progress, for really my school flays are now in their Serions period. as I am almost twelve. All this desire for hard School work is with the hope of helping me in ray life, on leaving my dear old school. . Niece Lilian llitusworth's letter says:--

" Dear Uncle jack,—Arriving bad: at school, the boys and girls cannot keep quiet because they have so much to tell about the holidays. This generally ends with a batch of imposi- tions or a bad mark being given. Going back to school means to some .children getting up earlier (me for one), because during the holidays they have no need to get up so early. Softie of us do not like the thought of going back, but some are just the opposite, and to them it is a pleasure. I will close now, wishing you and our soldier uncle a very happy New Year."

AN EARLY START AT LYMM.

The holidays in Cheshire were over earlier than in Warrington, which is shown by a letter from Niece Annie Massey, Hope Field Cottages, Sandy-lane, Lymm, who was our prize winner in the December 4th competition. "We started school," she writes, "on Thurs- day, January 6th,. and only a few turned up. I intend to be more earnest and persevering in my lessons and learn all I can, as I shall be leaving school shortly. My favourite lessons are history and geography. We had an ex- amination before we broke up, and I came the third from the top." History and geography are very good subjects in themselves; a liking for them shows as a rule the possession of a practical mind that will

turn itself to useful ends in life. I hope Niece Annie will distinguish herself in her special subjects;

"THERE'S NO TELLING."

It is Strange how many of this week's essayists are former prize winners. Here is another of them, Niece Mary Roche, 6, %stock- street, who having enclosed the name of a new member for the Corner, writes to say she will be very glad to get back. to school, where she will help her teacher in every way she can and learn her lessons as thoroughly as possible, "as there is no telling what position 1 may have through being well educated." That is a very wise position to take up. Prepare for whatever may happen, and then you are quite safe. Mary wishes the Corner every success and helps it in the very best way to succeed, as we have said, by bringing in a new member, whose name is Jennie Dowling. BRIGHT AND CHEERFUL.

HINTS ON HEALTH.

Useful Information In Popular Language.

When they are yours they are nerves; when somebody else's, temper.

Japanese inns furnish fresh tooth brushes every morning free to every guest. The brush is of wood, shaped like a pencil, and. frayed to a tufty brush of fibre at the large end. Stomach Troubles.

Some stomach upsets come on as a result of eating too often, or taking the meals too near together. Generally speaking, we should eat not mire often than three times a day, and have the meals about five hours apart. Ordinary food takes about four hours to digest. If we allow the stomach an hour's rest between then it is able to digest the next meal without undue strain. When •we crowd one meal upon another, and so keep the whole digestive organs continually at work, we must expect a break- down. When I hear persons say they must have food every two or three hours or they feel sinking, I know they are keeping themselves ill simply by the frequent taking of food. It is far better to go longer between metils than to have them too near together. And in certain cases it is better to eat only twice a day than to have three meals close together.—Dr.

Oatmeal. E. I, E. writes that she cannot eat oatmeal without suffering afterwards. Oatmeal is often bad and ill-cooked. It is best from Scotch mills. It should be cooked the night before, and kept heating all night in it 'Norway kitehener." is often spoilt in eating by putting cream and sugar thickly over it. It should be eaten dry, or may be boiled and chewed with a crust. It is one of the best of the cereals. The oat Cake is the best; .of all, breads, but should be made by a Scotchivoman, and .no sugar or grease of any kind used in making. Use in winter mainly. There are good fresh brands of oatmeal to be had. Rolled oats are a useful substitute. if not quite -so good as the fresh natural oatmeal. If kept a long time. they are -apt to taste of the packet. Good oatmeal must always ;he well cooked to make it digestible, and as English servants will not always take the trouble to do this people fall back on the packet substitute.—

THE RECTOR ON LOVE OF

PLEASURE.

Accusing England of selfishness and indiffer- ence to worship of God, and too much love of pleasure and money, the Rector (Rev. Canon Willis) at the Parish Church on Sunday even- ing asked whether we could give ourselves up to such a life of indulgence and pleasure, if we were really converted, and in earnest? Had we ever thought what it would have meant if this country had been invaded, like Belgium and Serbia? If we realised that, could we continue the pursuit of amusement to such a great ex- tent? It was only by the mercy of God that we had been spared so far. It must not be thought that he was against innocent. necessary recre- ation, hut when he saw churches comparatively empty, and heard of music halls and picture halls being crowded night after night. when he knew that the vaorking classes never had higher wages than at .present, he asked: Were 11)“.r denying thetusefves? 'Had they really thought. of those who were suffering in ut her countries? We should pray to God, not only for the con- version of - Germanl", but for the conversion of England.

ll"IRIED.

AI hy Christianity Did Not Mei ent AVar.

" Why has the Christian religion not pre- vented war?" This was what the Rev. Irvine Lister set him- self tp alswer at the Cairo-street Chapel on Sunday evening. He said that as we gazed upon the awful picture of the war, well might we exclaim, Why did not this' Christian faith. common to all, restrain them? Why did it not hold their hands and prevent them from all this bloodshed and violence? Why did it not show them another way and persuade them ,by its .sweet reasonableness and its brotherly 'love , During the past 18 months many had asked these questions, some in impotent fury, others with the satirist's snarl, but many more in the ' pain and distress of a. strickpn heart, and a broken faith. It was no use hiding from our- selves the painful fact, that somehow this faith, this religion we had held so dear, and which we still held dearer than anything else, !had failed us in the hour of need. It was the one great thing which could have prevented this ghastly catastrophe. It was the one thing upon which many of us pinned our whole faith, and it crumbled under our hands and was as nothing. When the spirit of anger rose, when the diffi- culties grew day by day, we all rushed • to our weapons, and waited for the time to strikn. was but 'a melancholy statement of fact to say

===== 0002601_19160115_art0211.txt | included=true | included =====
7c7cyco4 Cc2:4.112c:

that at the greatest .crisis at' the world's history, the Christian religion, which all nations pro- fessed and uiiheld, failed lo prevent the world- wide war and all its attendant bloodshed and b uff eri n g _

===== 0002601_19160115_art0212.txt | included=true | included =====
What We All Thought,

Nearly all thought that the Christian religuri should have prevented this war. Why then did it not do so? We should first of all ask our- selves a question which would very effectively clear the air for us: Was there anything wrong with the Christian religion itself, with Christian ideals, and with Christian teach- ing? Was there anything within the faith it- self to make it impotent and incapable of realisation? Was there anything .wrong, for ex- ample. with the conception of °the fatherhood of God? Was the brotherhood of man a false ideal? Was there anything in•what we called the distinct Christian virtues, which was not pure, and did the ideal which Jesus held up before us look like something unreal and cap- able of deluding us? An impartial examination of what we called the essential doctrines of Christianity could have but one effect, and that was to appeal to us more than ever as sound and reasonable. There was nothing in them which we could condemn or that we could wish to condemn. All knew front their own ex: perience that if they placed themselves under the leadership of Christianity they attained to e. -higher and nobler life than otherwise would be possible. Wherever and whenever the great social doctrines should hove been vigorously applied. the Church failed. I k 1641' Was and what was the result It wa in now for us all to behold. The whole of the religion we profess had not had its chance. It had not all been tried. Only a part of it had been preached and insisted 'upon, and consequently, it was never able to influence in any way the relations of great groups, like nations, with each ether. it was not. simply that Christ ianity..had failed to prevent this ,war. it was that the social aspect of Christianity had never been brought sufficiently into operation : it had never been tried, it had been overshadowed by the other aspect. and that was not sufficient in .itself to prevent this catastrophe.

LABOURER'S DEATH.

Further Evidence Wanted By

The Coroner.

The manner in which Peter Traynor, a labourer, aged 60 yean, of 5, Mill-lane, War- rington, met his death was described at an in- quest held by Mr. S. Brighouse at Warrington on Tuesday evening. Mrs. Ann Traynor said that her husband was employed at Messrs. Pearson and Knowles' works at Dallam Forge. About six months ago he complained that a piece of slag had fallen on his left foot. In the course of a few days it seemed to get all right again, and he continued to work until last Christmas. Then he began to get very weak and was unable tel follow his occupation. lli4 foot got swollen and Dr. Liston was called in. Later Dr. Liston ordered his removal to the hospital, where he died on Monday, the 10th inst. Dr. Jagger, the resident medical officer at the Union Hospital, said that Traynor was admitted to the hospital on the Bth. He was delirious and unable to give an account of him- self. His left foot was swollen and inflamed, as also was the right leg. Death was due to diabetic gangrene. Mr. 'l'. S. Steel, who represented Messrs. Pearson and Knowles, said that the accident had never been reported by Traynor. Protest By Mr. Bottomley.

Mr. A. Bottomley (clerk to the Union) pro- tested that it was not fair to the patient, to the Guardians, or to the medical staff that a man should be sent.° the institution when he was in a dying condition. The inquest was adjourned for a week in 'order that the evidence of Dr. Liston might be heard.

ADULT SCHOOL GARDEN.

Nature Helps Social & Religious Movement To Prosper.

Under the g'uidance and zeal of its manager, Mr. Sam Sproston, great improvements have been wide in the Bewsey Adult School garden, situated in Folly-lane, and the school has felt the benefit of introducing the garden into the members' interests.

This fact was dwelt upon in the Secretary's report at the annual meetings of the school on Saturday, when gobd attendances and a real Adult School spirit were marked features of the gatherings. _ .

The electicm of officers took place on Saturday afternoon, the most notable change being the election of Mr. Ben Sproston in the place of Mr. Harry Wright, whose work in the larger sphere of the movement is increasingly taking up More of his time. Thanks were expressed most cordially to Mr. Wright for his services. After tea, an evening ;meting and concert were held, during which • Mrs. Palmer, secretary of the Women's School, gave her annual report.. She said that whilst the year had hail 113 difficulties and dis- appointments, the outlook was new much more. ,!heeri ng.

The retiring sevretary of the Men's School. iii his annual report. spoke of the last twelve months as' being anxious and difficult; yet, cell- tiering all circumstances, -encouraging. Many members had responded to the call of the Colours, and this had been keenly felt, their' withdrawal being a loss to the school. Others at home were working long hours at their daily toil.- Attendances had fluctuated considerably. ' The Glory of the Garen.

A review of the social club proved that: this department was in a flourishing condition, thanks to the unremitting efforts of the joint secretaries, Mr. C. Starkey and Mr. R. lie.aley. Without reference to the Adult School.garden the report would be incomplete: Great im- provements have taken place, and this year wiji see the completion of the bowling green, thanks to the Union President, Mr. J. A. Raneome. The members, working under the supervision Of Mr. Sam Sproston, have turned a veritable wilderness into a delightful flower and vegetable garden, where flower 'shows have already been held. A greenhouse and tea room have been built, and great expectations are entertained in the utilisation of these means for social inter- course between Bewsey School and their many friends in other Adult Schools, both in Warring- ton and throughout the Lancashire and Cheshire Union.

In closing, the Secretary told of the corre- spondence which was now regularly carried on between the absent soldier-members and other members of the school, much to the helpfulness and inspiration of all.

During the ev6ing Mr. Harry Ward, the secretary of the Lancashire and Cheshire \ Adult School Union, gave an address, and Mr. J. A. RansoMe presented the prizes to the winners of the billiard handicap, and also a special prize on behalf of the school to Mr. Sam Sprostotr. for his splendid work in the garden. Mr. Ran- some expressed a. hope that these garden activities would be forerunners of similar efforts in other places. The musical items were much enjoyed, thanks to the artistes, Mrs. Worthington, Mr. J. Poole, Mr. J. Wilson, of Manchester; and tlieir accompanist, Mr. Hannan.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0218.txt | included=true | included =====
JOTTINGS BY AN OLD

JOURNALIST.

Outspoken Parson. The Rev. 'l'. Vickers' sermon, in which he depretated excess of worldly pleasure to the neglect of public worship, reminded me of an old Wesleyan supernumerary of my youth, Rev. James Luutit by name, who, in his pulpit utterances, was habitually bold and fearless. One Sunday morning, when there happened to

be quite a procession of late-comers, the

scandalised .old minister stood silent until all was quiet, and then administered this stinging rebuke: If it had been announced that Queen Victoria would be present at this service, you would have hastened to secure your seats; but you are invited to meet the King of kings, and yet seem to think you can come shuffling in at any time!"

Parasites. I once knew a Town Councillor who com- plained that his dog was "fearfully troubled with Pharisees," and the compiler of last week's poultry column had a useful paragraph on how to get rid of fleas. His suggestion as to kerosene or crude petroleum reminded me of a superannuated old compositor of my acquaint- ance who, although turped four score, had remained a bachelor and lived 'alone. I once entered his " combined " room; and found him freely besprinkling the bed with turpentine from a big bottle. I did not attempt to hide my surprise, but, without any interruption .of the process, tha old fellow explained that he was "only killing fleas!"

Creeds. The Rev. E. C. E. Calieton's vigorous pulpit reference to this subject reminded me of a newspaper blunder which I may possibly have )mentioned before. In a town in which I lived at the time the Vicar had been advertised to speak on "Why have a creed F" and in the report it appeared "Why have a Queen?" On the day of publication I had occasion to see the Vicar, and I found that he did not appreciate "the humour o't." I said "What shall be done unto him that hath done this thing?" Ile muttered in reply, and I cau/ht only the words "Something with boiling oil in it." An Extraordinary Gun.

The patent agents' summary of the year's in- ventions brought to my mind the case of one in a district in which I resided about 20 years ago, who invented a hydraulic gun which was supposed to be destined to revolu- tionise methods of warfare. I at- tended a test with a model,, as a result of AV hich at least two of those present agreed to help to finance the venture. However, it failed to impress the military authorities. I have since seen comments upon it in an engineering paper, and I learn that (luring the recent strenuous months the attention of the War Office has again been unsuccessfully called to the invention. Waste (?) Products. -

In that same report by Messrs. Hughes and Young, the phrase ".utilisation of waste pro- ducts" occurs. I first began to notice this par- ticalarly on the erection of a refuse destructor In a district in which I worked as a repos.....r at the time. Destructor and electricity works were set up side by side, and the former sup- plied energy for the latter. Meanwhile much waste that was an unhygienic eyesore has been put to good and profitable use. Slag refuse is not only being largely employed in the healing of our detrited shores, and in making bricks and paving blocks, but, in the form of slag wool, or silica wool, having an appearance and properties similar to asbestos, is ,now much used as picking for steam pipes, cylinders, boilers, &c., being excellent as a non-conductor of heat. The gas and ammonia evolved during blasting once poisoned the country all round; now ammonia gas is employed in the conversion of brine into alkali and sal-ammoniac. I turn ' to these processes on account of their pecu- liarly local interest. Thus it is pleasant to learn that the glycerine expressed during soap boiling and once treated as waste, is now a valuable commodity; yet it is not with an- mingled joy that we find jujubes evolved from the waste of a cotton mill. I somewhere read a while ago that the used-up pickers which have been discarded from the factory looms form the basis of certain jujubes or pastilles. As many of my readers know, the picker consists of a strip of hide studded with wire teeth, and when it becomes clogged with fluff, dirt, and grease, it has to be replaced by a new on, Until comparatively recently the discarded pickers were consigned, to the waste heap; now they form the staple of a profitable industry. The hide is separated from the wire, and freed from. grease and dirt; the gelatine is then ext-.-v..te i and purified, combined with glucose or sugar, colouring, and flavourings, and sent forth again itt a variety of 'tasty confections. Everyone knows now of lanoline or sheep's wool fat, for- nierly a " waste " product; everyone ought to know of the buttons made from old boot leather. and the uses of sewage.

Useful Knowledge. The latter is a big subject, and even in a column of miscellaneous matter must have a paragraph to itself. Bradford affords a won- derful and perhaps unique instance of muni- cipal enterprise in the direction now indicated. 'The Corporation there have in regular use extraordinary machinery for extracting market- able produce from the city's sewage. When I was last there they were making £30,000 a year from grease recovered from sewage, and were opening new works, as the result of which it was anticipated that the annual profit would be raised to £50,000. Moreover, good markets had been found abroad for the pressed cake which remained after the grease had been ex- tracted. At several other places I know of the sewage works constitute a more or less substan- tial financial burden with no set off. That does not apply to the sewage farm alone; even the 'wirer method of filtration through coke and other Materials treats the sewage as mere waste. At Oldham a while ago a system was installed in which, an attempt was made to re- cover the agricultural values of the sewage. The inventor, Dr. J. Grossmann, read a paper on the subject at the Birmingham meeting of the British AssoCiation. Ile said that the un- satisfactory results hitherto in using sludge as manure are due to the fatty matters it con- tains. These fatty matters come from soap and kitchen refuse, and they render the soil im- pervious to air and water. In the Oldham pro- cess the dried sludge from the town sewage is mixed ivith a small percentage of acid and sub- jected to the action of super-heated steam. This carries off the fatty matters and leaves a brown pa-sVder, inodorous and completely sterilised. which contains the essential components of a complete fertiliser. But surely Bradford is already far ahead of that. It was evident, how- over, that the inventor regarded the foregoing ag only a small part of the economic value of towt, sewage. It concerns the solid deposit alone. [Jr. Grossmann is of opinion that ten times the mantirial value of the sludge could, under proper chemical treatment, be recovered from the liquid part of the sewage. At present sewage farms recover a small fraction of this manurial value, but apparently in any system of bacterial filtration it is lost altogether. Dr. Grossmann estimates the potential worth of town sewage in this country at 02,000,000 per annum. Well, again there is the amazingly successfnl case of Bradford, so that one need not by any means treat the above as a dream or a fantasy of the chemist; the miracles of science have indeed wrought more wonderful things than the turning of town sewage into a source of income instead of heavy, expenditure, as at present it notoriously is in the great majority of cases.

Handicapped by the Unfit. 'We should have been stronger in this war had there not been so many physically "unfits," whose condition was due to ignorance and neg- lect by the nation of the physical side of educa- tion from early childhood onwards.—Lord lialdane, I

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JANUARY 15, 191 G.

APPLE WASSAILING.

Habits And Superstitions Relating To Apple Trees.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0222.txt | included=true | included =====
SURVIVALS FROM SAXON DAYS.

BY ARTHUR

Among odd things that survive in remoti, corners of the land must be placed the habit of invoking the deities who are supposed to live in the fruit trees. As this belongs to the

twelve days of the winter Solstice, of which we have been recently writing, it will be appro- priate to describe it at the present moment. We ' have already explained that in the remote past our ancestors believed that the twelve days from December 25th to January 6th were days of mysterious doings among the gods. After December 25th the sun appeared to be regaining power, the days lengthened and it seemed to them that the deities were preparing themselves after (perhaps) a winter's test to begin their exertions again. This observance of December 25th has nothing to do with Christian ideas. The festival was very oh! when Christianity took it over, and all through the ages the twelve days of mid-winter were observed as times of holiday and religious in- cantation and ceremony. Students of history will remember that in King Alfred's laws the twelve days were fixed by Statute as a public holiday. One of the customs that seems to have been observed at the end of the twelve days was that lof invoking the gods on behalf of the fertility of the earth. On January 6th the people went out to the fields and orchards, made fires and offered meat and drink offerings to the gods with the object of inducing them to continue their favours by sending fruitfulness and good cheer.

Of the exact form which these ceremonies took we have little evidence, but it is of great in- terest to notice that they continue yet in Here- ford, Devonshire, Gloucestershire and, perhaps, Coinwall.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0223.txt | included=true | included =====
TWELFTH EVE IN DEVON.

Let us first take a glimpse of Twelfth Night doings in remote Devonshire. The toasting or wassailing of the apple trees takes the form of dancing, music and various mystic rites. Commerce and education have pushed the habit away from the towns, but it is constant in the villages, and the old folks still believe that the trees would be fruitless if the wassailing were not performed: They hold to the idea. that the trees are sentient things and will do well or badly according as they are attended to on the spiritual side. Twelfth Day Eve sees the farm household assembled in the farm kitchen. Then after some good cheer a procession is formed and a march made to the orchard. The farmer walks in front with a lantern hung upon a pole. The chief farm hand follows with a large two. bandied loving cup and some pieces of toast. Next come men armed with guns, old Muzzle- loaders, blunderbusses—which never appear out of doors on any other occasion—drums, kettles, and anything else that will make a noise. This preparation for noise suggests that the old idea was to waken the divinity that sleeps within the tree. Indeed, the whole ritual is marked by noise. The crowd forms a ring under one of the trees; the farmer standing in the middle seizes a branch and pulls. it earth- ward with the invocation:—

Oh, apple tree, I wassail thee! In hopes that thou wilt blow, To blow and to bear well, So merry let us be.

The whole company then joins in a cry to "Tom Pod," the kettles and pans are banged together and, the guns go off. The mysterious Tom Pod is supposed ,by this time to be awake and. the cry is raised "Now Tom Pod we wassail thee." The bearer of the large cup hauls it to the farmer, who drinks the cider it contains with the toast " Good health to thee, old apple tree." The cup goes round to all, for there is no stint or good cider on Twelfth Night, and after all have given the toast a piece of toasted bread is saturated with. cider and .put up in the tree for the birds to eat. rhe procession is reformed and one by one the orchards of the village are wassailed. A supper in the farm kitchen completes the evening. •

We will not offer any speculations as to the

_ personality of Tom Pod. _ The name probably represents Some deity long since forgotten. Bat the word "blow " in the rhyme must not be passed over. It Pi the Saxon word for bloom, and represents a very distant past. Apple- blowth is the local word for blossom, and to

ANOTHER DEVONSHIRE WASSAIL.

In South Devonshire--so a correspondent tells us—there are some additional pieces of by-play and a different song. The procession goes to the orchard in the manner described, and while circling one of the largest trees they drink three times to the following toast:—

Here's to thee, old apple tree, Whence thou May'st bud and-whence thou may'st blow! And whence thou may'st bear apples enow! Hats full! caps full! Bushel! Bushel! Sacks, full, And my pockets full too! Huzza. •

After the ceremony they return to the house for supper; but the _ women folks who are within always bar the door and whatever the weather, keep them outside until they can guess -what is cooking on the fire, which is generally some out-of-the-way thing,l difficult to be guessed. The tit-bit is given to the yokel who is fortunate enough to guess aright. In West Devon they take a milk tankard of kider, and after each has drunk part of his cup he flings the remainder over the branches of the tree.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0228.txt | included=true | included =====
BAAL-FIRES IN HEREFORD.

In 'Herefordshire t hey 'retain the fires—the Baal-fires of the past—strangely reminiscent of heathen days. In the evening of January sth the company walk out to a wheat field where twelve small tires arranged in a circle and one larger fire in the centre are lighted. The farmer and his men stand in a circle and shut their shouts being echoed by the noises of other farm folks similarly employed upon their, own farms. "

Tho idea is clearly 'that of the invocation of the deities on behalf of the fruitfulness of the soil, and the twelve fires pertain lb the months of the year.

The next -stage in the Herefordshire pro- gramme is equally interesting. if the gods are to he invoked for the soil, why not for the cattle?

A supper follows the field fires, and upon the table there appears a cake with a hole through its 'centre. This is not touched at the table.. but when the meal :is ended it is carried to the shippon. Here a boat;ting ritual is gone through. Standing opposite to the first and fi nest of the oxen the 'farmer drinks from a cup- of 'cider or ale. addressing it, by name and toasting to its health and prosperity. The cake is then produced and with much ceremony placed on its horn, the horn being pushed through the hole in its *centre. The ox is then • tickled in order to in- duce a shaking or tossing of the head. If the cake is thrown behind it belongs to the farmer's wife, if: forward it goes to the farrmer's chief man./ This bit of 'merriment over,. they return to the house where the doors are locked against them until they have sung song. The rest of the evening is spent in merriment and the eating of the cake.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0229.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MEANING OF IT ALL.

These customs; which mean nt*-hing more than innocent mirth to the ruStics., are full of meaning to,the student of the -rust. The tires are pieces of Baal-wortihip condemned in dis-, tant;Bible days. as one of the evil things learned from the people of Ouinan. °The meat and drink offerings of those far-off dajs .are seen in cake and f•ider offered to the gnils. who are sup- posed in refurn to send fruitfulness to the earth, the trees and the cattle. Toastirg. the apple trees is. in simple language, offirring sacrifices to Baal and Astoroth (the lime() and female deities of fruitfulness) who are *,,eyposed to smell a sweet savour" and do _kindnesses in return. -

MIGHT HAAE BEEN WASTED.

But,- Instead These • Melt Pitt Their . Odd Moments To Good Advantage. Odd moments. may be termed the small change of life, which may either be wasted or used as the foundation of a fortune. Gladstone knew what the odd moments were worth. He always carried a book in his pocket, that none of them might be wasted. Alexander von Humboldt's days were so occupied that he had to use the early morning ,or night for his labours, while others were asleep. Charles C. Frost, a shoemaker in Vermont, resolved to use an hour a. day for study. He became one of the noted mathematicians in the United States. Dr. Meson Good translated Lucretius while travelling from Place to' place visiting his patients in .London. Dr. Charles Darwin composed most of his works by writing his thoughts on scraps of paper Wherever he happened to be. Henry Kirke White learned Greek while going to and frora the lawyer's office where he was studying law. Dr. Samuel Johnson wrote Itasselas of even- ings in a week, to meet the expense of his mother's funeral. Lincoln studied law during his spare moments. William T. Foter, a. poor boy,: educuled, , supported. himself, and saved enough money to start him to college at. the age of 17. - He had never studied Latin and was informed that it required two years of Latin to • enter -the school. It was just tw.o months before the opening of the schooL .He studied for two months, took tho examination, and entered college, where, of course, he made a good record.

CHARACTER STUDIES,

' No, 1. An Old Hollander At Work And Play.

CHILDREN AND FLOWERS.

By HAROLD MAYBURY.

I Mr. Maybury, whose bright articles on many *subjects have become quite a feature of I( The Examiner," has arranged to write a series of character sketches for us.

The first of these word-pictures is given below. It will be noticed that it is written in Mr. Maybury's characteristically vigorous and appealing style.

The place I write about is not far from Amsterdam, a spot -which, in the 611/111MT time, is a feat of colour, where whole tracts .of country are clothed in glorious bloom with all the colours of the rainbow; where hyacinths, Lulips, daffodils and crocuses .smile at you as you wend your way along the sleepy roads and high- ways. Just now the low-lying land is drab and monotonous, with cold winds and fog blowing from the North Sea, but no. matter what season it may be, there will you find that tiller of the soil who daily performs his task unmolested and unnoticed. He is the old Dutch- tulip farmer. It is nigh the eventide of his life; his forehead is wrinkled with grooves deep and prominent, figure bent with the passing of the years, eyes blue as the summer skies under which he has worked these three score years, and hair white as the palest tulip he ever helped to rear and tend. When the " Stadhuis " bell proclaims the hour of six and the lark in the fields soars in the skies there you will find him, summer or winter, performing his daily task. Jan, as he is called, is known throughout the country- side, he greets you cheerily as you pass, he knows the children from • the farmsteads for miles .around and he watches them at childish play when labours are o'er. There is only one thing that will ever make him tarry in. the midst of his work, and that is. when he greeted by the little Dutch boys or girls on their way home from .school. "They are like the flowers," he says, " slender aml delicate, requir- ing careful tending; else ,-oung lives are broken. They grow up and burst forth into blossom, some beautiful and pure, others drooping and storm:toSsed. Are they not like the flowers?" _ _ „

he says, and you have no answer for him. Some say he is in his second rchildhood..otherSiltat „as a young man he experienced a great sorrow. No one know S the I'th, for Jiiit-iS;; reticent, never speaking of bygone days. He nevetk goes into, the. village !` tapperiz "a do the, other tulip farmers, instead, he trudges on his way to the little clean and -Picturesqn.e cottage hidden in the trees along the winding canal. There in

the summer you can see Old ,Jan seated outside his humble dwelling, smoking his long pipe, watching the curls of. smoke rising in the taint breeze.

SHAKESPEARE'S SPIRITS.

Did The Bard Believe In The Supernatural ?

===== 0002601_19160115_art0237.txt | included=true | included =====
GHOSTS, DREAMS AND VISIONS.

When Shakespeare introduced ghosts, witches and elemental disturbances into his plays did he believe in them? -

." That he uses the supernatural With re- markable effect •is uOiversally ailmitted; it seems only when interpretation of the phen- omena is attempted that moderri ideas on the subject are found to be so confused and contra- dictory.'?

So remarked Mr. W. Al. Sherburn at Monday evening's meeting of the Warrington. Literary Lial Philosophical Society in Cairo-street HalL and in analysing "Julius 'Ckesar," "Macbeth," "Richard III.,". and " Hamlet " he came to the conclusion. that the phenomena occurred too often, and in too ;many Cases where " effect " was not sought, for that e.xplanation to Satisfy. In. Biblical records, he said, there were signifi- cant instances, such as that which took place at the Crucifixion, when, in the middle of the day, there was darkness for the .space of three hours, and the veil of the temple was rent from top to bottom. In other places it was recorded that' 'the graves gave up their dead." •

===== 0002601_19160115_art0238.txt | included=true | included =====
The Blessing of the Sun.

It was also on record that on the happening of many great events in history, at the birth or passing of "a,, child of the gods," the elements seemed often to have shown sympathy with the events:: Even the most prosaic of us delighted to have the sympathy of the elements on our wedding morn—(laughter)—and from a deeper motive than that of bodily comfort.. He had in mind modern instances of apparent elemental sympathy and he had a conviction that there was more scientific foundation for the psychical phenomena observed in "Julius Ca3sar " and " Macbeth " than had yet been formulated. Often .when dreams or visions were. related there seemed to be a Decius Brutus about to try to pierce them with raillery or exclaim Indigestion." Much more potent weapons were required, however, to dispose of that which wonted to have accompanied mankind ever since the dast-n of reason. It was impossible to con- ceive what human life .would have been could dreams and visions have been eliminated from history and experience. Often the dreams were symbolic. like Calphurnia's, the neglect of them being usually followed by like -consequences. In our own time, whenever a shipwreck or

_ ntining accident •occurred. some evidence was sure to be .forthcoming of lives saved through obedience to a dream. vision or some other portent experienced either by the individual or some sensitive friend, and many a life had been

lost by disregarding..sneh warning. In his plays Shakespeare. had introduced apparitions in different stages of development and for different purposes. Troublesome Apparitions.

The appearances to Macbeth, at the wadies' incantation, were the only ones for which pre- paration was made. All the others came un- bidden, generally unwelcome—those appearing to Brutus, Macbeth (at the supper) and Richard to 'convict of murder: to Richmond to hearten and encourage; and to Hamlet to expose base murder and treachery done by another, in- :Adentally all the cases going to prove the con- tinuity of life and thought after leaving the murdered body. The ghost of Hamlet's father, being by far the most fully developed and natural ghost of theta all, Would best repay consideration. Between Hamlet senior and the other ghosts there was also this difference: they were only seen by the individuals concerned, while he, at first, was seen by all present, although he spoke only to the one concerned. He was a perfectly natural ".gliost.. Accord- ing to history, peetry and experience, ghosts had always attended mankind and still per- sisted, and it ''W.aS scareely to our .Credit as reasonable beings that our general- attitude to- ward such phenomena was no more scientific than it was, say, a thousand years ago.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0239.txt | included=true | included =====
HIGH WROUGHT IMAGINATION.

A I.frikht discussion followed. In proposing a vote of thanks to Mr. Sheiburn, the Rev. E. thinityr renuirked that the subject of the led i are was a very ticklish one, but there was much more Teason to believe in the super- nal urn!,, as given in the, Bible and in old literature (such as in Shakespeare) than there used to be, for during the last few years psychical research had been earnestly pros& cuted. One could not read some of the state- ments published by the 'Psychical Research Society without feeling -that one must accept very- much what one used to - pooh-pooh; Bui one wondered. whether Shakespeare really did believe in the reality-of these things 'himself, or whether it was that he presented other people's opinions.. In all the plays there was what one' might call "dramatic fitness " in bringing forward these, supernatural • elements. The minds' of Julius Ca3Sar„ Lady. Macbeth, and Richard, were wrought to a very high pitch of excitement and imagination and, thus, at those times—as-in the case of the soldiers who thought they saw angels at Mons—they were apt to See and believe things, which, in their cooler moments, they would neither see nor believe. "To a certain extent we are all super- stitious,". remarked' Mr. Hedley Taylor "I am,superstitions about one thing: I never eut my finger nails on Friday. (Laughter.) But. I have a particular reason. .One of my greatest friends was once cutting his nails on a Friday when his mother told him to stop as it was bad luck. He did not, and had very soon finished the process. Within two and a - half hours of his mother telling him that. my friend had lost two and a half fingers. So I never cut my nails on a Friday." (Laughter.)

===== 0002601_19160115_art0240.txt | included=true | included =====
WHEN THE GHOST WALKED.

Mr. H. Grosvenor was of opinion that as there was no chorus to/fill in the gaps in the plays a ghost was introduced into "hamlet," and witches into "Macbeth." They took the part of the chorus and, without them, it would be very difficult to imagine certain ideas in the plays. Neither the ghost nor the witches, how- ever, were always essential to the play, for in travelling companies which could hardly pay their way these characters were cut out and instead, the stage carpenter did some knocking or speechifying behind the scenes. When the companies got enough money or could afford a ghost. the ghost walked, and when they could not afford, the ghost tin the person of the stage carpenter, not an actor) rested behind the scenes. it was always a question of "Will the ghost walk this week?" (Laughter.)

MARKT

MR. HERBERT SAMUEL THE NEW HOME SECRETARY,

It is officially announced that the King has approved the appointment of Mr. Herbert Samuel to be Home Secretary in succession to Sir John Simon, resigned. Mr. Samuel, who now becomes (lief of the department of which he .was Tinder Secretary from 1905-9, has been generally nominated for the office ever since the resignation of Sir John Simon was first talked about. He is a native of Liverpool, and 45 years of age. Although he has only been in the House of Commons for about 13 years he has had a varied and exten- sive experience of office. He has been Under Secretary to the Home Department, Chancellor of the Duchy (twice), President of the Local Government Board, and Postmaster-General. Since Mr. Churchill resigned the Charwellor- ship of the Duchy, Mr. Samuel has held that office, together with the Postmaster-General- ship, and his re-election for the Cleveland division a few weeks ago: after a rather farcical contest, will bo recalled. •

CYCLING AND MOTORING.

Should The Drivers' Age. Limit Be Reduced?

THE OLD ORDINARY.

In 1887 the " Ordinary," as it .was termed,, still held the field, and its riders looked down with something akin to contempt on the safety. A writer in the " Irish Cyclist " has been busy turning over the pages of some old voluines of the paper, and he draws some very interesting deductions concerning cycling thirty years ago. According to him those who preferred the high bicycle had good grounds for their preference. it possessed many features which were then Wanting in the low typo of machine. It was a more sporting machine;' there was a feeling of greater exhilaration in riding it. It gave. a ' better view of the country passed through, and was more reliable. It was more picturesque and exciting as a. lacing machine, and, strange as it may appear, a fall from a high bicycle was less serious than one from the safety, be- cause the rider of the former had time to save himself by taking the first impact on his hands, and, turning -like a Catherine wheel, the next Place which struck was his shoulders. As a result serious injuries were very rare.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0245.txt | included=true | included =====
THE TWO TYPES OF MACHINE,

At the time to which I •am referring there was a great deal of correspondence as to the relative itualities of the ordinary and the safety. •It appears that one writer had ex- pressed the opinion that in luggage carrying capacity the safety stood first, but he was at once taken to task for doing so, one critic stating that he had ridden both mactines and found that in the case of the safety .he could only carry luggage on the handlebar, whereas on the ordinary he could carry a•ritultum in parvo bag strapped on the backbone, where it was. quite out of the way. The. same writer stated that he had ridden the safety '1,300 miles during the, Season and went back gladly to the -ordinary, which, in his opinion, would never be surpassed for lightness, easy running and simplicity. His objection to the . safety was complication, : liability to sideslip, the nuisance of cleaning and adjusting the chain and rattling mud- guards. At the period in question, the writer may have thought he was • correct in his view that the ordinary was the best all-round machine. but he proved hopelessly wrong in his opinion that it would never be surpassed.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0246.txt | included=true | included =====
RIDING TOO HIGH.

There is no more common fault made by be- ginners—and even experienced riders are often guilty of the mistake—than riding too high. Particularly i§ this noticeable in the case of young children who come into possession of a machine formerly belonging to one ,of their elders. Time after time I have seen boys and girls vainly trying to reach the pedals of a machine much too big for them, and have won- dered who was responsible. A cycle must; be of suitable height if any real enjoyment • can be 'secured. As a. general rule a good test is to try and rest the instep of the foot on the pedal at its lowest: . If this can't be done lower the saddle at once. • . A small frame with a high 'saddle pillar, is not really as effective as a larger frame with less of the pillar protruding. True, the actual distance from the bracket to the saddle is exactly the §ame in both eases, but in • the former the rather 111111iti oral position throws an undue strain on the frame and makes comfortable rid lag • almost impossible. -For growing boys and girls it is particularly desir- able to have the longest—but not tow 10ng.... frame possible at the time to allow fur future growth. PETROL CONSUMPTION..

Whenever two or three motor cyclists gather together they are almost certain to -discuss the number of miles they can run to the gallon on their respective mounts. At the moment this question is of even more importance than it was feiV, months ago, for last week there- was another penny per gallon added to the price— this on top of the *enormous advance which occurred a couple of months ago. No. 2 quality petrol is .now 2s. Id. per gallon—three months ago it was is. 7d., and that was bad enough in all conscience. As a general rule when every- 'thing is considered the petrol consumption is about the same, but there are instances in which the average mileage is much below what should be Obtained: I came across a case a few days ago. The owner of a 31 h.p. motor cycle, with sidecar combination stated that he could only get about 40 mp.g. This is only two-thirds' of ; What should be obtained with the machine he rides. When riders 'eipe'rienee such a decrease in their mileage it may of course be due to leakage, but this can easily- be ascertained. More ;probably, however, it is caused by a dirty epgine.- This state of affairs increases petrol consumption enormously. By removing the carbon deposit from piston and cylinder, re- nlacing and regrinding valves, fitting new springs, and adjusting the valve tappets, a great improvement can quickly be effected.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0247.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DRIVING AGE-LIMIT.

It has recently been urged that the age limit of drivers should be greatly reduced, and that boys of 15 years and upwards should be granted licences. But surely this would be a mistake. Driving a motor vehicle of any kind requires a good deal of judgment,. a steady nerve, and plenty of resource and experience. Few lads of 15 can be said to possess these qualities. A few days ago a lady was killed in Loudon by a Leavy motor car driven by a lad of 17, who at the inquest was said to have had very little experience. The darkened streets of London and other towns are bad enough as they are; they will become infinitely worse if fifteen-year- oldlads.are,.to be allowed to drive. Of one thing I am certain, and that is that if the 'age limit

is to bo reduced there should be a strict ex- aination of • efficiency before a licence is granted.. At the present moment age is the only qualificatiOn-needed to obtain a licence. It is most desirable that this should be accompanied by an examination.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0248.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====

===== 0002601_19160115_art0249.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DANGERS OF STARTING.

As long as it is necessary to stand in front of a ear aid turn a handle until the engine starts just so long must repeated warnings be uttered against starting in gear. Again and again we have reports of accidents from every quarter caused by careless.. starting, every one of which might have been prevented by the exercise of a little care. There is only one safe plan; be- fore attempting to turn the starting handle, always feel that the gear. lever is in the neutral position... Do not assume that because every- thing was right when you left the car perhaps half an hour previously„ that the same happy state of affairs is likely to continue indefinitely. There are always small boys about who want little encouragement to amuse themselves with a waiting car. Only this week I heard Of a case where the gear lever had been placed in the reverse' position and the brake taken off with rather-alarming, although, fortunately, not serious consequences. . After all it is a very easy matter always to make a careful scrutiny before starting.

CRANE GIVES RAY.

• Labourer Crushed .To Heath At Messrs. Itylands's. Early ,on Monday morning William Warbur- ton (50, a labourer, of 12, Forshaw-street, War- rington, 'met with a fatal aCcident whilst em- ployed at Messrs. Itylands Bros.' works in Church-street. An inquest was held by 'Mr. S. Brighouse on Tuesday evening. Mr. T. S. {Reel (for Messrs. Henry (Treenail and Co.) represented the firm, and Mr. Seabury (Gas Workers' and General Labourers'-jjnion) was for the widow. Mr. IL 11. Brothers (ILM. • Inspector of Factories) was also present. - John King, crane driver, living at P. Wel- liugton-streett said that at 6.10 a.m. on Monday he was engaged in company with a man named Michael Bird and iWarburton emptying wire from certain annealing pots at the works by . means of an electric crane. Witness was driv- ing the crane and Warburton and Bird were hooking the wire on to the crane from the pots. They had taken two lifts of wire out and had . raised the third about six inches when the head - of the crane gave way and came toppling over, Witness was thrown just clear, but Warbuton was crushed beneath it. Witness shouted for assistance and obtained a "jack," and the man was liberated, but he was dead. Tike crane was registered to lift four tons, but at the time .of the accident was not lifting more than 10 cwt. Witness had been driving the crane for about four or five months. In reply to the Coroner, witness said that the crane was examined once a week. Questioned by Mr. Brothers, he said t.Dat the crane was working very well at the time. John Kennedy, fitter, said the crane was in his charge, and he was responsible for its examina- tion. It was examined On Deceniber 6th last. The part broken was .121 t. Gin, above the ground, and witness had to get on to a ladder to make the examination, and he did so carefully. If there was a defect it was one that could not be seen. It had been a weak casting. .11e could not say what exactly was the cause of the accident; no man. knew. There was no evidence when he made the examination that an accident might occur. The crane had been working for seven y ears withmit an accident oceurring before. On July 13th I lie crane was taken to pieces and no defect could then be seen. in reply. to Mr. Steel, be said that the crane was supplied to Messrs. llylands Bros. about seven years ago, and it had been in constant use night. and day, It was supplied by a well-known firm. and there was a guarantee with it that it would lift up to four tons. Last week it was lifting from 21, to 3 tons all the time. A verdict of "Accidental death" was re, turned.

A USEFUL MEDICINE.

A correspondent who in the " Times " deals with medical work in the Army describes what is known as "Wardle," where all sorts of ,cases are gathered together. The following extract is amusing, and perhaps instructive :—" But we do think we know how to deal with the skrim- shanker.' We gill) him every chance; we even have it consultation over him, and then, if we are quite certain in our mind, we put him on our celebrated Mixt. Diabolica. Its com- position is, and must remain, a secret, but its action is certain and speedy. It is almost as miraculous as the Holy Water of Lourdes. To -show you what it can do, it cured in one dose a man who co%uplained of deafness and blind- ness, pains in the back and side, pneumonia of eight weeks' standing, inability to feel-his feet or stand, and a few other trifles. lie lay on his cot, 'a helpless wreck for hours before, but after one dose, all he wanted was water to drink, and , to get back to his regiment as quickly • as pos- sible!"

Tom Reece's Entertaining Dis- plays At Billiards.

A WEEK IN WARRINGTON.

Warrington's most distinguished visitor this .week has been Torn Reece, the billiard player. He arrived on Monday with a couple of cues under one arm and a basket of snooker balls in .the hand of the other. Like Kubilik and hi- -violin, wherever Reece goes so does his pro Aessional equipment. Since he came he has been looking into ,he -places where green tables are congregated. He was at the Liberal Club oh Wednesday even. Winwick Hospital on Thursday afternoon. ,Junior Conservative Club in the evening, and the Conservative Club last night. Reece is a player who can give any man alive a. run for his money. .iint in Warrington this week he has not been after money: he has heed playing on behalf of a war charity—The women's •sewing funds for soldiers—and, through the -high prices and the considerable audiences, ci 'good sum has been raised. Generally, ladies are not supposed to under. :-stand billiards. but there were about a dozen at the Liberal Club, whose eyes (one could dis- tinguish them through the smoky atmosphere) 'twinkled with pleasure as Reece executed won- derful strokes.

FINED FOR SERVING WOUNDED SOLDIERS.

At Blackpool Police Court, Charles Crewe, licence-holder of the County and Lane Ends Hotel, was summoned for, supplying beer to wounded soldiers, the offence being ,one against the Defence of the Realm Act. The evidence of the police was Mit on Christ- mas Eve a police officer visited the vaults of the hotel, and there saw two wounded soldiers with • partly consumed pint glases of beer in front of them. They were both wearing the distinctive uniform of the military convalescent

camp at Squire's Gate. The police officer asked the men if they were wounded soldiers, and they replied that they were. Thee' also said that tit, barmaid had seed the beer. The defence was that the men had their coats buttoned up when ,they went in, and that the barmaid was unable to see from their dress that they were wounded soldiers. " The Bench said they were of opinion that there had been a certain amount of negligence. The penalty was £lOO. but they had decided to impose a fine of Ms.

WLY YOU ARE

WEARING YOURSELF OUT.

All bur life through, we are all wearing 'ourselves out—using tip the substance of our bodies ! Hard, laborious toil wastes us away •quickly, but every expenditure of energy. ..every movement, every thought, causes some wastage; and this waqage should be repaired by our daily food, but that is only possible when it is well-digested. Think, then, of the im- portance of keeping your digestive organs in good working order. especially in these dhys when we are called upon to give the most that is in us. If for any reason your organs of digestion, the stomach, liver and bowels. fail

to-perform.their functious perfectly. there is no remedy that will so soon restore them to health and vigour as Mother Seigel's_ Syrup. As a stomachic remedy and liver corrective, it would be difficult to• find its equal.

Clubs must provide .a ground with a clear playing space of not less than 80 yards by 50 yards, and must be responsible for the conduct of the spectators.

Any club raising an objection under these by- laws musf place such objection, along with de- posit of five shillings, in the hands of the secre- tary within 48 hours of the conclusion of the match, and such deposit shall be forfeited should the committee consider the objection frivolous. The decision of the committee shall in all cases be final, subject to the right of appeal to the County Committee, from whose decision there shall be no appeal at a court of law. In case of appeal a deposit of one guinea must he made to the County Committee, who shall have power to confiscate, return, or other- wise deal with such deposit as they may think fit. Any objection as to ground, posts, ball or similarity of jerseys must be made to the referee before the commencement of the game. A copy of objection under bylaw 19 must be sent to the club objected to, who may make counter objections, which must be in the hands of the hon. secretary of the League within 24 hours of the receipt of the objection, together with a deposit of tis.. which shall be subject to the provisions of bylaw 19. A copy of counter objection must be sent to the secretary of the club objected to, or the objection may not be entertained.

Each club must vegist.r the names of their players not later than September Ist in each season, after which no new players shall be allowed to play in any League match until he has obtained the permission of the League Com- mittee or the provisional sdnction of the hon. secretary, the same to be confirmed or otherwise at the next meeting of the committee. No player shall be allowed to transfer or sign on in this League after February Ist in each season. Any player having played with a club in the League and wishing to transfer to another club in the League must obtain permission in writing from the club he has last played with. This transfer must be in the hands of the secre- tary three clear days before he can play with his new club, and must be confirmed at the next meeting of the committee.

In case of objection on question of age the onus of proof shall rest with the club objected to. In the event of the objection not being sustained, any cost of birth certificate., which the objected club have been obliged to procure shall be borne by the objecting club.

NORTHERN UNION.

Local "Derby" At St. Helens.

The eight matches of thf. Northern Union played on Saturday contained no striking features. There was a hard struggle at St. Helens be- tween the two local teams, and the spoils went to the Recs.' this being the second occasion on which they have defeated the town team this season.

STANLEY YOUNG'S FINE PLAY.

In the Rochdale—Oldham match, Stanley Young, who was playing half-back for Roch- dale, distinguished himself immensely. The "Athletic News" states that in last Satur- day's match he "demonstrated his value as an opportunist and a great defensive player. His save in the second half when Oldham had their best chance of scoring will not be forgotten in a hurry. He seemed to spring from nowhere, took the ball at the most difficult of angles, and then ploughed his_way thro-ugh the opposition, finally to find touch at the centre line. Young fully deserved the applause which was forth- coming." The results of the matches were as follows : Rochdale IL, 2 points; Oldham, 0.
