INTERMEDIATE LEAGUE.

Some Good Games And Tall

Scores.

For the first time for about a month, the weather on Saturday was fine, and some good

football was witnessed. In addition to the weather, the grounds both at Wilderspool and Victoria Park were .dry, and passing movements, for once in a way, were Permitted.

If there was one team whfch the conditions

did epecially suit, that team was Parochial, and in their match with Sacred Heart they indulged in some excellent passing bouts, which in nine cases out of ten; bore fruit. Of course. Sacred Heart had not a full team_ On

by any means, but even had they been at full Strength it is questionable whether the score would have been much different, for the Parochial lads seemed to be in one of their irresistible moods. Little wonder then at the score of 24 points to nil. _ .

At Wilderspool there was little to choose be- tween the Beamont and St. Mary's teams, and after a good tussle, the former team scraped home by eleven points to eight. We noticed that Beamont had one or two fresh players assisting them, .one of whom, Jackson; .who played in the forward line acquitting himself very creditably. • St. Thomas' and St. James' were also in opposition, and the first-named team playing the better game throughout, ran out winners by thirteen points to nil.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0264.txt | included=true | included =====
EXCITING GAME AT WILDERSPOOL.

The encounter between the St. Thomas' and St. James' teams proved exciting from the first to last, and though the lads from Stockton Heath won by 13 points to nil, they did not have things exactly their own way. By strenuous play they soon put the St. James' line in danger and were early rewarded. St. James' men were penalised and Berry

opened the score for Stockton Heath by kicking a goal. Berry was doing good work in his position at half-back, and by a bit ,of fine play scored a try soon after he had kicked the goal. Glee was successful in converting, and half- time found St. Thomas' leading by seven points to nil.

The second half opened with the St. James' lads getting the ball- and practically sweeping their opponents off their feet. For five minutes the St. Thomas' team had a rough time of it, but good play by the backs kept the Opposition at bay. A piece of fine play, on the 'part of Stockton Heath threequ,arters relieved the Pressure and took play. to the other end of the field, and three points were added to • St. Thomas' lead by Gill scoring practically on the touch-line. The position was difficult and the additional points were not added The last -try of the game, scored by Rylands for Stockton Heath, was one of the best seen at Wilderspool this season. The St. James' full- back fumbled the ball in midfield. Rylands picked it up smartly and raced down the field with about four or five of his opponents straggling after him. He was too good a sprinter for them and scored practically under the sticks. The goal-kick was again a failure, and the game closed with the score thirteen points in favour of the St. Thomas' lads.

BEAM ONIT AND ST. MARY'S WELL MATCHED.

After opening briskly, the first half of the match between Beamont and St. Mary's settled down and became a very tame affair. In the first tev minutes Barlow scored three points for Beamont, and nothing more was ,Tored until the teams changed positions. On the resumption St. Mary's took up an im- mediate offensive, and it was not many minutes before Conner had scored under the Beamont posts thus equalising the score. The try was. converted and these two points put the St. Mary's' lads in front. This lead, however, did not last very long, for Beamont soon had their revenge and Makin scored for them. St. Mary's were awarded a free-kick, but nothing came, of it: 'Towards the close of the game, they became' very aggressive and played finely. Ultimately Rimmer scored a try for them, making the scores again equal. Practically in the closing stage Jackson scored for Beamont, giving them the lead of three points and the game ended : Beamont, 11 points; St. Mary's 8 points.

A BRIGHT PROSPECT.

Intermediate League's Care Of

The Future.

Whilst certain newspaper critics—outside Warrington, be it remarked—have been endeavouring to agitate the public mind against two or three members of the Northern Rugby Football Committee, because the clubs they represent refused to take any part in senior football "for the duration of the war," and have been decrying the General Committee because of its inactivity, a sub-committee has been engaged assiduously in drafting standard rules for Junior and Intermediate Leagues. We are in- formed that those rules, which were submitted at a meeting of the General Committee, held in Manchester on Tuesday evening, and adopted, will soon be in the hands of the club secretaries.

It will be remembered that the question of intermediate football has been -under considera- tion for some time. In fact, to a great extent, Warrington may be considered to be the 7 pioneers in the forward movement of teaching the young idea, and of fostering a system which has for its object the training of 'boys to play the game, so that the most capable will b- able to graduate from 'a school! team to the senior.. The President of the Warrington Club (Mr. E. Osborne) in returning thanks on his election to that position a couple of years ago, emphasised the fact. "At the present time," he said, "on leaving school, the boys were abso- lutely neglected. They went out of football for two or three years', and then had to begin to learn the game over again. The intention of of committee was to get a League for boys of from 14 to 16 years of age working successfully, and then, in a year of so, they would form a ihird League for youths of from 16 to 20." With the hearty co-operation of the school teachers. those objects have become an accomplished fact locally, and now that the -Union has taken the matter up it will, when the war is over, and times ' become normal, be worked in "every town and district where the game is popular. The out- look is bright for football when the great match between the Allies and the Huns is over. That must be won first.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0271.txt | included=true | included =====
TO SAFEGUARD INTERESTS OF FOOTBALL. ,

At a meeting of the General Committee of the Union in November the following points were agreed upon:—

I.—Unification of rules governing inter- mediate and junior football. One set of rules to govern every district, with special additions to meet any particular area. 2.—That each Northern League Club or com- bination of League Clubs, be invited to organise and control an Intermediate League in their own area.

3.—The Northern Union shall subsidise the Intermediate Leagues to such an amount as they may consider advisable. 4.—ln order to safeguard the interests of the Northern Union, the . district members of the Northern Union and County Committee shall be allocated to the various Intermediate Leagues. s.—The appointment of organisers, who shall assist in/the organisation of clubs and Junior and Intermediate Leagues, the appointments to be made by each local area. A sub-committee was appointed to draft a model- set of rules, to' consider details of the scheme, and to consider and report to the General Committee. We have been favoured by a perusal of those details, and have much pleasure in presenting to our readers a number of them. They appear to us to be splendidly drafted and they will, no doubt, meet 'with the hearty approval •of all concerned with the pro- tress of such a. popular sport.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0272.txt | included=true | included =====
SOME OF THE RULES.

The standard bylaws of the Northern Rugby Football Union intermediate Leagues include the following : The League shall .be governed by a committee consisting of one representative from each club in membership, one representative from ea,:n Northern . Union League or Combination Otto , in . the 'lntermediate area, district members a the Northern . Rugby Football Union, and County Committees, a treasurer and secretary who shall be ex-officio members .of the com- mittee. Five members shall form a quorum. That the entrance fee (if any) and annual subscription 'shall be such sum, or sums as the League may qetermine .at the annual general meeting, and such subscription shall be due on October Ist in each season. Any club whose subscription is not paid by that date shall be dealt with as the committee may decide. ' The annual general meeting shall be held in the month of June in each year to receive the secretary's report and balance sheet, and for the consideration-of bylaws and rules of the League, and ,any other .bUsiness. • Each club to be entitled to send one representative,to a general meeting' exclusive of the officers of the .League. In all cases of protests, disputes or other com- plaints, all representatives or officials directly affected shall retire while the decision is being considered.

That the committee may elect any subcom- mittee it may deem fit; and define its duties. That no club be allowed to withdraw from the League until all liabilities in connection witi the League, and other competitions governed by the League, be fully discharged.

That the official time of kick-off shall be fixed by the League Committee. In the event of any match not being played through the visiting team not turning up on day fixed, the committee may deduct two points from the club so Offending. and order such coin pensation as it may deem fit. _ _ _

LOCAL STARS OF THEiFUTURE.

Without inviting criticism on the ground of being too optimistic, we have no hesitation in saying that when the game recommences the.e will be an added interest to it by sue,h a com- mendable spirit which will permeate it. If each club could be assured of a team of local "stars "—and it seems to us probable with such a system—the matches would be of real import. That there will be considerable com- petition for places, and a keener knowledge of the game, we have not the slightest doubt. Played as we have seen it—we remember Fish and several smaller fry—there is no game to equal it, and we can enjoy it—when the war is won.

ASSOCIATION.

St. Elphin's Improve Their Lead Among Juniors.

Last Saturday the conditions for plying was much more favourable; some keen games were witnessed dnd plenty of goals were scored. The leaders of the League continued on their suc- cessful journey towards championship honours. They journeyed t& Walton to play their return fixture with the Scouts, and in a heavy scoring game they came out winners by seven goals against three. The resul# was somewhat dis- appointing for the Scouts, and they did not relish the defeat at all. Several of their players appeared to have only one object in view, and that was to subdue- the efforts of Boyle, of the "Saints." In this respect their efforts, to some extent, were rewarded, and while they succeeded in preventing Boyle from scoring his usual goals, the bitterness of the defeat was keenly felt by the fact that Rigby, a former player of the Scouts, piled up the goals for the "Saints," scoring on five occasions. This defeat of, the Scouts puts them. back into third position now.

A very keen game was witnessed between the Fairfield and the Howley teams. The United was not at full strength, Cooper, Tunstall and England being away, and Woodhouse played in the rear division. At the close of play the United Reserves came out winners by the odd goal in three. This success places them as runners up to the St. Elphin's.

.A very keen game was also played at Arpley between the Mersey Juniors and the Bewsey Club, and as both 'teams were at the foot of the table it was a race between the two as to which was going to •leave the other- at the bottom. Owing to the keen tackling both teams were awarded penalty-kicks in the first half. • The Juniors failed to improve theirs while, on the other 'hand, Smallman was Successful. The second portion opened very keenly, as Walters scored for the Juniors, and then Smallman did the "hat trick" for the visitors. After this reverse the juniors fell off in their game. and they were chiefly defending until the call of time.

Hassall was very unfortunate with some of his shots. lie hit the crossbar on three occasions, but smceeded later in getting one below.

Perks, by his recent displays in goal, is proving himself to be one of the cleverest junior custodians in the Warrington district.

Tlie Warrington Central Club .were • dis- appointed in their original fixture owing to the Widnes St. Mary's cancelling the match on -Friday night. For the -benefit of their players they arranged to play the Wirenetting Depart- ment of Rylands's. The -latter .having the assistance of T. Knight of the Emmanuel Institute in goal, a very good and enjoyable game was played. The Central just managed to gain the lead in the first half. The second por- tion was well contested, and the " Netters" managed • to equalise, but unfortunately for them, a mistake by Jackson allowed Mason to regain the lead for the Central. This appeared to have a little effect on the display of some of the ," Netters," who, although defeated were by no means disgraced, by a score of four goals against ,two.

The return fixture between the Emmanuel Institute and the Penketh Albions was played in the Victoria Park. The visitors were not able to play their usual team owing to Army recruit- ing. The Institute were minus some of their regular players, but they got some-good 'substi- tute g in Siddall and Stubbs. The game ended in a very easy victory for the Institute owing to their forwards' good shooting. The visitors on several occasions showed good Combination in midfield, but when within :shooting distance their efforts lacked sting. lied it not been for Faulkner at fill-back their defeat would have been much .heavier.

LEITHER.

Trade Still ImproN int! In Warrington And District.

Steady progress continues, says the War- rington correspondent of the "Leather World." Both inquiry and orders are stronger, and, eoupled with the forward booking, it is beyond doubt the year has had a very auspicious be- ginning. What is even more important than the immediate business is the healthy tone of the market. The improving tone was fostered by the trade meeting in London last week, and the information before the meeting gave every assurance of a very busy season, and tanners are quite prepared to work up to the full limit of capacity, provided there is a reasonable probability of there being sufficient tanning materials to go round. This unfortunately is the central point of the position to-day, and the one- about which most doubt exists; it is true the scarcity has caused nothing worse than inconvenience RO far, but, working as we are with supplies as they come in from day to day, with no reserves and no stocks in the country, it is easy to imagine the straits we would be in if anything occurred to further diminish or delay the scanty shipments which are at pre- sent coming along.

• Baai Prices In Germany. Owing to the famine prices for leather, which have prevailed in Germany throughout' the war, says the "Leather Trade Review," boots and shoes are now selling at *figures which elevate them to the dignity of a luxury. Con- ditions in the trade have become so abnormal and retail custom has dropped off to such an alarming extent that the National League of Boot and Shoe Dealers has just petitioned the Imperial Chancellor to take radical measures to regulate the market. The League points out that boots and shoes are "a particularly im- portant object of daily requirement," and that while remedial action ought, to be taken to pre- vent, still further increase of prices, dealers cannot safely face any reduction in the exor- bitant figures now being asked for their wares. The Government has declined to accede to the League's- suggestion for the fixing of maxi- mum prices for boots and shoes, pointing out that the establishment of such prices for tanned- hides and skins ought automatically to regulate the price of all leather products.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JANUARY 15, 1916.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0297.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ROMANCE OF SMUGGLING.

By the Act of 1816 the punishment for a smuggler, when caught, was thus set forth: "Every person found assisting in unsitipping any goods may be arrested, and if convicted, is to be committed to hard labour in the House of Correction for any term not ex- ceeding three years, nor less than one year. If any person so convicted is approved of as fit to serve His Majesty, the justice may ad- judge him to serve as a soldier or a sailor for a term of five years."

The punishment on the face of it reads se- vere, but the smuggler was a popular fav- ourite, the prison officials knew he was not a felon, though the law had condemned him, and their good sense refused to regard him as a criminal. The navy welcomed a useful man, and the boatswain who licked the raw re- cruits into shape, did not like a man the less who had a spice of the devil in him, and had defied the smuggling laws. We all have bur particular angle of vision, and the sea- man resented this official interference with his drink and his smoke.

Once on board a man o' war, the smuggler soon entered into the life and spirit of the service, and -Lord Exmouth is reported to have said that the smugglers were among the pick of his ship. Steadily the new system of protection began to prevail over the free trade, and created new and more subtle ways of cheating the revenue. The stuff had to be handled in smaller quantities.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0298.txt | included=true | included =====
A. SUCCESSFUL RUSE.

A lugger' would run up to the coast deliv- ering small consignments to the various vil- lages. A Customs officer received information of a craft that had been off the Yorkshire coast distributing tobacco, gave instructions to the men under his command to be extra watchful. A fisherman who had shipped some hard Cavendish, was steering his coble into the mouth of the river Tees and decided to land at a place called Seaton Snook. It was a fine evening, and he ran his coble up on the beach. His boy called his attention to a man seated among the long grass skirting the sands. Here was an awkward predicament, for no doubt he was a patrol, but the fisher- man was equal to it. He waded ashore and walked up to the concealed man. "I want your help," he said. "My coble has started one of her planks, do you mind helping me to drag her up the beach." The man addressed pleaded his duty tad the want of a pair of sea-boots, mending co. bles and wading after them was not his rou- tine; besides, the tide was running out, and the coble would be high and dry in a few minutes. The fisherman sighed, and said he supposed he would have to wait. "Here, boy, he shouted, "leave the coble and run up to the farm yonder for a hammer and a few copper nails." The boy ran off to the farm, and when the coast was clear he brought a man and a cou- ple of pack horses. The coastguardsman had a painful interview with his chief, and there was more than the usual amount of merri- ment in a score of houses and inns of that neighbourhood.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0299.txt | included=true | included =====
DARING COOLNESS.

A more daring piece of coolness took place at Hartlepool. A fish salesman named Horsley was augmenting his income by a lit- tle smuggling. A consignment arrived earlier than he anticipated. The Dutchman's vessel stood into t4e bay, and began making signals and his mission about as public as it could be under the circumstances.

Horsley had a fine nerve. He went to the coastguard station. "I want protection," he said to the chief officer. "That Dutchman is bringing over some bulbs for me, but he is a madman the skip Per, and to tell you the truth, I am afraid of him when he is in drink."

"I'll send a couple of men with you," said the obliging officer. "I think if you sent the coastguard pin- nace, it would give the bully a fright tbat would last him all his life," said Horsley. "Because they once licked the British fleet, there's no doing with these Dutchmen," he continued. "They think themselves superior to an Englishman." "0! 0! Do they ?" snapped the indignant representative of the service. "Man the pin- nace," he called out to a subordinate. They rowed off to the Dutchman. Horsley climbed on board and got an opportunity of instruct- ing the skipper in his part. The pinnace stood by, "He won't let me bring my bulbs away, and I have paid him the money," Horsey shouted to the boat. The Dutchman appeared at the same time gesticulating wildly. Two coastguardsman were ordered to board and the chief officer accompanied them. "Which are your cases ?" he asked. They were point- ed out. "Have you been paid ?' The skipper sullenly replied that he had. "Very well, what more do you want? Get those cases into the pinnace." The Dutchme thoroughly cowed or pretending to be, did as they were directed by the limn in blue and gold, and in a few minutes Horsley and his contraband were being rowed ashore in comfort and safe- ty by Her Majesty's servants. What is more, the coastguardsmen helPed to .get the pack- ages into Horsley's warehouse on the town wall, and everybody wanted to stand them a drink. Of course the chief officer found out eventually how he had been hoaxed, but the onl— wise course for his own sake was to keep silence, and make no charge or complaint.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0300.txt | included=true | included =====
SMUGGLER'S METHODS.

An officer of Customs noticed that old boil- ers used frequently to pass to and fro from, Goole and, Hull to Ostend and sometimes Ant- werp and other places on the Continent. He wondered why the same boilers should be carried to and fro so often as cargo. One day he had a boilersmith to take off one of the manholes, and when he had climbed into the structure, he found the reason in a ton of to- baeco and 112 Pounds of cigars. " This system of smuggling had been going on for years and only came to an end in 1890. Some very influential people were mixed up in this business, but one man took the blame and the punishment; he also took an extra share in the profits.

Across the -North Sea in all the seaport towns there are shops which 'cater specially for the smuggler. At Flushing We are told on the authority of - Commander Shore, "there • was a regular manufactory of tobacco made • up into ropes of all sizes, these being slight- ly washed in rum .presented all the appear- ances of genuine hempen stuff." In 1842 to- bacco was seized at Portsmouth which had been cut into the shape of a man's shoe. A favourite dodge is to hollow out a block of timber. Tobacco has been found concealed in almost everything, including 'family Bibles. There was a perplexing time for the Customs officer when ladies wore dress improvers. You could not search every woman who 'crossed the Channel or the North Sea, but the Re- venue put on a special supply of female Cus- toms officers, and they soon put an end to. their sister's methods. MODERN SMUGGLING.

Oecaslonally the doings of the o:d days are brought back in the columns of the press. 'rake the following as an example which I copy from a London paper for June 20th, 1908. "In the Nisi Prins Court, yftterday, b?fore the Lord Chief Baron and a • special jury, a case of an un- usual character was heard, disclosing exten- sive smuggling off the South West coast of Ireland.. The case was brought by the Irish Attorney-General arrainst Reelofs and others. The defendants are resident in Holland, and are the registered owners of a sailing veivel called the Cosmopolite, about 37 tons. The action was in the nature of a petition that the vessel and her cargo should remain for- feit to the Crown gihc 1,R,1 been ff•ur4 Illegally within three miles of the coast of the British Dominions.

" Lieut. Travers,-ii.N., who was in com- mand of the gunboat Skip Jack, stated that he saw the Cosmopolite heading out to. sea from the Coast at Cape Clear on May 30th, she was within a mile and a half of the coast when he saw her first. He sent a crew on board, and the captain told them that he was bound • for Iceland from Rotterdam- They searched the vessel, and found 3,640 lb. of Cavendish tobacco, 37 lb. of other sorts of tobacco, and 64 lb. of cigars, with st2o4 in Irish bank notes and silver.

The average pulse of a healthy man beats 7‘.: several seconds. The average pul6e of a healthy man beats ". times a minute. C

Suicide is less prevalent in Ireland than in any other country in the world.

JACK ASHORE AND JACK AFLOAT.

"'Who wouldn't sell a farm and come to sea ?" is an eternally unanswered question, which is asked in evety fo'castle and many a cargo-boat cabin thousands of times every year. .

- The proposition is usually put forth at times when, whether on account of hard work or stormy seas, the lot of the sailorman seems extraordinarily thankless. In the course of a long voyage every able-bodied seaman will vow that .hell seek a " shore-job " without fail as soon as the mudhook is down in Eng- lish ground: with strong words he will back this determination. And yet, the chances are that when he has spent his bard-earned wages, he will drift to a Sailors' Home or a Sailors' Boarding-house and there remain for a longer or shorter period till, once more, he signs articles. _

-The Boarding-house " runner " is the type of man whom Mr.. Morley Roberts describes as eighteen stone and as strong as a hip- popotamus," and it is his business to get hold of hard-up mariners and bring them along to his "boss;" a small commission is his for each man whom he brings to the establish- ment.

"Where's your book ?" is' the first question put to the "bold A. 8.;" by "the book" is meant the Board of Trade continuous dis- charge book, wherein the captains under whom the sailor has served have given their testimony as to conduct and ability. If there appear a sufficient number of " V.G." (very good) entries, the applicant is received into the house until the boss is able to find a ship for him d Tor this service, the Boarding- house master takes the sailor's "advance note," and draws his first month's pay.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0303.txt | included=true | included =====
"SHANGHAIED."

Many and varied are the stories of men who have been "Shanghaied," and more than a few of these hang around the waterside of San Francisco. The " shanghai " operation is generally performed by means of drugged drinks—internal application—or of a stout club or a slungshot—external application. In either case, when the victim recovers con- sciousness, he finds himself in the fo'castle of a deep-water sailing-ship; he has a nasty taste in his mouth or a mountainous bump on his cranium, and the chances undoubtedly are that his pockets are empty, and that he has no more than the rags in which he can stand up. Some Boarding-house master has con- tracted to supply a crew, and this is his last resouree for securing a full complement. Per- haps the most famous story of " shanghai- ing " is that which Mr. Morley Roberts has used as the basis of his yarn The Promo- tion of the Admiral." It is, I believe, found- ed on the fact that g certain 'Frisco Board- ing-house boss did succeed in drugging an American rear-admiral, who had been inves- tigating waterside life, and sending him on board a sailing-ship as an A.R.; moreover, there was no chance of getting ashore till the ship -reached the port for which she was bound. Similar stories of San- Francis° may be found in the works of the late Frank Nor- ris, and, if I remember right, such an inci- dent figures in Jack London's "Sea Wolf." It is told cf one Boarding-house boss who persuaded a young fel:ow 'in search of a plea- sure trip to join what was described as a fishing-boat. It was not till the victim was at sea that he discovered the vessel to be a whaler, bound for the Arctic on a two years' cruise!

===== 0002601_19160115_art0304.txt | included=true | included =====
THE "DEAD HORSE."

However good a pay-day the sailor may have touched when he got ashore, by the timc he joins the next ship, it is very, doubtful ii much of his money remains. He is lucky if Ix has retained the worn kit he brought back with him and lnckier still if he has replen- iAed his stores of clothing and sea-gear with which his chest ought to be furnished. I believe that, on " wiud-jammers " at any rate, there still remains the custom of " burning the old dead horse." The writer saw this ceremony take place a few years since on a barque bound from London River to Fremantle, W.A. As has been already stated, the sailor (or his representative) re- ceives a month's wages in advance, and, when his first month on blue water is passed, he can feel that he is really beginning to earn money again. To celebrate this, on the occa- sion I spoke of, the fo'castle rigged up their "dead horse" in the shape of an old tar- barrel which wa,s clad in a ragged suit so as to resemble a human form. This was hoisted up to one of the yards and set alight, while the sailors sang a fitting melancholy chant to show their joy at "letting the old horse die," and thus speeding the departing guest, who represented three or four pounds of which, most likely, the shipping-master had received the bulk. THE "SLOP CHEST."

But, though after the "dead horse" has perished miserably-, the sailorman begins to feel that " more clays, more dollars" is now true of .his existence, there are other calls upon his pocket, and this despite the fact that he may be thousands of miles from the nearest shop or sa:oon. Unless he has been very thrifty ashore, he will need to re- plenish his wardrobe; especially if the ship is running into 'cold latitudes south of the Cape or round by Mage!lan Straits. It is then that he must seek the steward and tell him that he ia com-pel:ed to draw upon the skipper's "slop-chest." This, in reality, is a stock of clothing which the captain keeps for the benefit of himself and his crew—for his own advantage, in that it is often his own specu- lation, and he is therefore able to command his own prices—for his crew's advantage, be- cause there is no other source whence ,they can obtain woollen clothing to keep off the cold, or oil-skins to shield them from the stinging lash of the rain or the hail. Thus the few pounds which Jack may have accumu- lated by the time he is home again are de- pleted on this head, let alone what he may have °drawn in foreign ports for his pleasure.

There is the 'Matter Of tobacco, too, but in regard to this, the sailor in the "deep water" ship (and, of course, in all classes of craft) is at an advantage for he gets all his duty free and it wil very like;y- not cost him more than is. Bd. a pound. Still in a long voy- age, :lack contrive:, to draw a good many pug s of "hard " Irom the skipper' and the steward does not fail to tally it off against his pay-day. During the hundred or more days which may pass .on the trip, there is little to amuse the fo'castle, so that it is not to be wonder.d at that at cribbage or poker, the men get into the way of wagering plugs .of toliac:•o upon their own or their friends' In fact, I have known 'a man amass as much as forty powids' weight of tobacco in the course of a voyage out and home. Some- times the gambling is in boxes of matches.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0305.txt | included=true | included =====
HARDSHIPS OF THE DEEP.

s-The British Board cf Trade has striven to improve the food of the ordinary seaman of late years, and one lies only to point to the daily ration of liniejuice in the tropics as one of the measures taken to keel) Jack "afloat in good health. But the ,man who sails in deep- sea ships is far less fortunate in respect of diet than the men who serve on cross-Atlan- tic passiagcs or in the boats plying across the North Sea. It happens not so very seldom that the men of the fo'eastle„ battling a:oft ira the icy p.x!es far south, or tired aftex fruitless "dry, pulls," may come down to find :he galley w-ashed out by a heavy sea and nothing but cold tack to be had. _ _

The -name given to New Brunswick seamen is not quite ex iinet yet; they are still known as '" Herringhaeks,” because they .are popu- lariv suppwed to feed on those fish the bons_ Atick. out of their spines.

Sti2, it is curkus to ti:ink that in these days when (ioverninent authorities legislate to improve the conditions and the dietary of sailors, those old sea-dogs of the 16th and 17th centuries won their way round thE world on food and suffering hardships which would daunt the mariner of to-day.

The lazy man aims at nothing and generally hits it.

Conscience is often a polite synonym ''er cowardice.

SONS OF TEMPERANCE.

With friendly society conditions being some- what at an ebb during the present days of war, it was with satisfaction that the members of the Warrington Sub-division of the Earlestown Grand Division of the Order of the Sons of Temperance received the news that the Society had made some little headway in its various departments. This information was ira- rarted at the annual meeting, held on Monday evening in the Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church Parlour, and the Grand Worthy Patriarch (Bro. W. Burrows), wilt!) presided over the gathering congratulated the Society upon its efforts, and expressed the hope that they would continue to make progress. The officers elected for service during the coming year were as follow :—Worthy Patriarch, Bro. R. Jones; Worthy Associate. Bro. Pattison; Financial Scribe, Bro. It Wardman; Recording Scribe, Bro. Simpson; Treasurer, Bro. Adams; Chaplain, Bro. Barton; Conductor, Bro. Grounds; Sentinel, Bro. Allen; Auditors, Bros. Wilkinson and Conway; Sick Stewards, Bros. Moores and Crooks, and Sister Simpson. The Grand Worthy Patriarch then installed the officers, the ceremony being conducted in an impressive manner. Afterwards the members proceeded to discuss the amendments to the rules as proposed by the Rules Committee of the Grand Division, and instructions were given to the delegates as to what action to take at the next general meeting of the Order. After hear- ing addresses from the Grand Worthy Patriarch and Grand Scribe (Bro. Briscoe) the brethren thanked them for their presence and help, after which the Worthy Patriarch brought the meet- ing to a close in the Society's usual way.

Apple Epigrams. The apple is an asset, financially, morally and politically. Thi apple is the aristocrat of foods and the best medicine. The climate and conditions that are best LAr apples are best for man. Apple orchards are better nurseries of citizen- ship than the deck of battleships or militai y camps. The man in the orchard is always a god citizen. The apple barrel is nature's medicine chet,t. Apples are an antidote for drink and tobacco.

CALL OF THE SEA.

The sea is calling my soul away; Stricken I stand on the throbbing shore. From the noisesome night and the drudging (lay . Let me set my sail and rettrn no more.

"The sea is calling," I cried aloud. And "Here, I come, for life grows drear!" Glad tears were streaming from head un- bowed; • Heaven's guardian angels hovered near.

" Mid the lonely seas I will wander wide By the lands of shine and the grandeur rude. With a spirit free as the tossing tide I will live with God in the solitude.

"The sea is calling; and I will brave Or blast-driven billow or fuming foam: The fowl shall consort me when calm is the wave; The wild Euroclydon shall carry me home."

The sea is calling, I know not why; Surging it sounds in my eager ears. But the rotting wherry was high and dry On barren rocks by the boding piers.

Still the sea calleth at ebb, at flow; There is balm in the breeze, there is salt in the spray! My heart is breaking; I cannot go-- The toil-tried wherry has had its day.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0311.txt | included=true | included =====
LOCAL AMUSEMENTS.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

Spy Play At Sir Joseph Beecham's

House.

This week, at the Royal Court Theatre, is being presented a play entitled "The Turning Point," the author of the same being a dis- tinguished Belgian named Henri Kistamackers. The play had a successful run at the St. James' Theatre, London, and Warrington audiences, who know a good play when they see one, have witnessed it in good numbers. There are many exciting moments and episodes'throughout the performance, especially in the second act, where Lieutenant-Colonel Felt, an officer, ad- mits to his wife having murdered one of their guests, Julius Gloga.u. The latter is a spy in the pay of the German Government. iMr. Rupert Lister, as Lieutenant-Colonel Felt, acts with power and intensity, .and is particularly fine. His bearing is distinguished to a degree. Miss Ccilie Barclay, as his wife, gives an emotional rendering of a very difficult part. They are assisted by able and talented artistes, including Charles Wiseman, as Julius Glogau; Horace A. Whitmee, as Marcel Beaucourt; and Kathleen Harrison, as Annette. To-night's performances commence at 6.30 and 8.30 p.m. respectively. Domestic Drama Next Week.

Mr. Joseph Millane is bringing his company next week to play an entirely new and up- (late drama entitled "The 'Confessions .of o Wife." There are seven scenes, and the natui of the play .may be inferred from mention of the fact that besides the lover and the husband there is a detective, who watches their progress. The play evidently answers the question. Should a woman tell?

===== 0002601_19160115_art0312.txt | included=true | included =====
" ROBINSON CRUSOE " AT TIIE lIIPPODRONIE.

Last week we stated that the name of Jazon and Montgomery stood for excellence, and it is proved to the hilt, judging from the crowded audiences this week which the pantomime Robinson Crusoe" is attracting to the Hippo- drome. The cast is an admirable one, embrac- ing such artistes as Rosali Jacobi, Bella Sylvester, Frances Fritroye, Irene Pearse, Harry Angers, Billy Curzon and Jim C. Max. Robinson Crusoe, played by the first-named, is splendid in every way. Easy .of carriage and possessing a rich • and melodious voice, she has endeared .herself to Warrington audiences. There is in the show an extensive element of humour of the right type, unhappily rare. in this particular class of entertainment, which redounds to the 'credit .of the part of the pro- ducers. For the most part this factor is due to 'Mr. Harry Angers, who plays the part of Billy Crusoe. His jokes and witticisms are first-rate, and one literally longs for him to occupy the boards right throughout the performance. It is not a one-man show, however, for he is capably assisted by Billy Curzon, who enacts the part of Dame Crusoe, Bert Escott, as the blood- thirsty pirate, is also worthy of praise. With respect to the ladies we have charming Irene Pearse as Captain Tom Bowling, Jessie Lee as Polly Perkins, and a host of other worthy artistes..

===== 0002601_19160115_art0313.txt | included=true | included =====
Next Week's Variety.

A troupe of Belgian entertainers will present a number of high-class and vocal selections next week, and they should prove to be exceed- ingly popular. Something of a novelty may be expected from Frou-Frou, the "French dog that understands the English language." "The Meanest Man on Earth" sounds rich, and, as Naylor Grimson is playing the part of Isaac Goldbery, there ought to be "something doing." The Soho Trio are artistic entertain- ers, and other good items promised are Monty Walker, the "Lancashire Komic "; Evelyn Earle, "the ouick-change girl with a voice"; and Abel and Welsh, a pair of eccentrics.

===== 0002601_19160115_art0314.txt | included=true | included =====
STAR KINEMA.

"The Heart of a Painted Woman," a fine five- part drama, indeed merited its position as premier picture at the Star Kinema House dur- ing the first part of the week, for there was not a dull moment during the whole time the picture was being screened. Quite as interest- ing and exciting as its predecessors, was the further episode of the " Exploits of Elaine," featuring "The Death Ray," while several good war scenes were depicted in the, " Pathe Gazette." The dancing film "Cameron Sisters," was especially enjoyed by the ladies, while the Keystone comedy, " Droppington's Devilish Deed," fairly "brought the house down." During the latter half of this Week a fine pic- ture, "The Secret Orchard," headed the bill.

LIVER POOL OLYMPIA.

Amusement lovers who have neglected to visit the Liverpool Olympia Circus and enjoy the celebrated band of His Majesty's Coldstream Guards, should make every endeavour to do so before it is too late, for next week is positively the last opportunity, as Mr. Wilkie Bard's Co. is coming in "The Whirl of the Town."

BELGIANS IN BELGIUM.

Over £l.BO To Be Sent From Warrington.

The Christmas Day collection on behalf of the Belgians who, though in their native coun- try, are starving under German rule, was a great success so far as Warrington is concerned. A meeting of the committee, which at the re- quest of the Mayor, organised the house-to-house collection in the town and district, was held on Thursday afternoon. Amongst those present were the Rev. W. T. Derry, the Rev. 11. Ogden, the Rev. Father Talon, Mr. IL Collin, and Mr. A. Tilling. Apologies for 'non-attendance were read from the Chairman of the committee (the Rev. Canon Willis) and Mr. C. E. Parker. . _

Mr. Tilling (the secretary) reported that fifty- four organisations in the town and district had taken part in the collection, only three churches having notified their inability to help in the work.

A financial statement was submitted by the joint Secretaries (Mr. H. Collin and the Rev. Father Tallon) showing that the gross amount realised was £lB3 7s. 9d. The expenses amounted to £2 7s. 9d., leaving a net balance of £lBl, which will be handed over to the Mayor for transmission to the National Committee for 'the relief of destitute Belgians in Belgium. In view of the many calls recently made upon the public, it was felt that the amount realised was very satisfactory. A hearty vote of thanks was accorded to all who had in any way assisted in the undertak- ing. '

The Difficulty Of Not Trading With

Germany.

The " Manchester Guardian" says:—

It is a strange world in which we find the "Westminster Gazette" airily approving of a prohibitive tariff against Germany after the war and the "Morning Post" drily explaining that the proposition is not "feasible," that we must not seek to go beyond what is "practically possible, and serviceable to this country," and- that " we lie in a material and practical world in which trade is one of the main elemeats." The " Westminister Gazette" wants to punish Germany for her " frightfulness'.' to non-com- batants, as. we all 'do, and .propounds a pro- hibitive tariff as the method: "Let there be no nonsense about it; let the duties be such as will stamp them with their political purpose." Has the "Westminster Gazette" considered how far we would be punishing ourselves as well as the Germans? Whatever • goods, we lxmght from Germany in time of peace we bought not to please the Germans nor becau%e we loved them, but to please ourselves, and be- cause we could not get the same things any- where else in 60 satisfactory a manner. And everything we got .from Germany we paid for ultimately in British goods or services— that is to say, in the labour of our own countrymen. . Of how much labour and employment do we propose to deprive our own people as one .of the re- wards for a victorious war? The "West- minster". realises the difficulty, but does not face it• beyond saying - that we are to' seek compensation for our loss of trade in an "enlargement of the • 'Free Trade area between the Allies and between Great Britain and her Dominions "—an excellent ideal but absolutely worthless as a business proposition. There is another point. We are fo German import into England; then obviously we shall Also prohibit British exports to Ger- many, for we could not stand before the word as a 'judge punishing Germany by refusing to buy from her while selling to her with all our customary zeal. Has it been considered how much jmnishment that measure would infii4 on our own people? It is true, however, as the "Morning Post" says, that this boycott is un- likely to. come into operation, because it collie not. be made effective so long as we trade freely with all Germany's neighbours. Or would it be part of the " Westminster's " nolicy. that it. peaee, as in -war, we should apply the doctriae of the "continuous voyage," 'demand guaran- tees against re-export if we sold goods to Gei'- many's neighbours. and insist on certificates I origin for everything that we bought frorn them? These large questions are sureiY entitled to a little thought before be;ntr disnosed of in the ,ninmary manner of the "Westminster Gazette.".

PRIVATE S. DICKENSON. Returns To Duty After Being Wounded In The Leg. Private Sam Dickenson, son of Mr. and Mrs. , Dickenson, 16, Haydock-street, was recently wounded in the leg while serving with the Loyal North Lancashire Regiment. The wound, however, was not serious, and after a short sojourn at the Base Hospital he was able to rejoin his regiment. Writing to his mother, he says he is in the best of health, and expresses the wish that she Spent a happy Christmas. Private Dickenson, who is 21 years of age, enlisted shortly after the outbreak of war, having been previously einployed as a scrap wheeler by the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd., at the Bewsey Forge. He was educated at the Heathside School. 1 His brother—Rifleman Tom Dickenson, of the
